# =============================================================================
# ENGLISH KNOWLEDGE BASE AND GRAMMAR ENGINE TRAINING DATASET
# =============================================================================
# This dataset teaches English from the ground up as structured, machine-
# readable knowledge: characters, words, word types, morphology, grammar,
# syntax, semantics, context, reasoning, and conversation.
#
# It is designed to be parsed by a simple line-oriented Rust program.
# No JSON/XML/YAML is used. The format is deterministic.
#
# =============================================================================
# FORMAT SPECIFICATION
# =============================================================================
# 1. Lines starting with '#' are comments and are ignored.
# 2. Blank lines are ignored.
# 3. A record begins with a line of the form:  TYPE: <TYPE>
#    Valid types:
#      CHAR, DIGIT, NUMBER, PUNCT, SYMBOL, ABBREV, POS, WORD, MORPH,
#      FAMILY, PREFIX, SUFFIX, PRONOUN, VERB, MODAL, TENSE, SENTENCE,
#      RULE, SUBJECT, OBJECT, QUESTION, NEGATION, VOICE, PHRASE, CLAUSE,
#      CONJ, RELATION, SEMANTIC, CONTEXT, AMBIGUITY, DIALOG, REASON,
#      ERROR, TABLE, SUMMARY, SECTION
# 4. A record ends with a line containing exactly:  END
# 5. Fields inside a record are lines of the form:  KEY: VALUE
#    KEY is an uppercase identifier ([A-Z_]+). VALUE is the rest of the line.
# 6. Multi-value fields use indented sub-lines (two leading spaces).
#    Sub-lines may be plain values or 'key = value' pairs.
#    Example:
#      EXAMPLES:
#        He runs.
#        She works.
# 7. Every record MUST carry a unique ID field:  ID: <dotted.id>
# 8. SECTION records mark numbered top-level sections and may span the
#    rest of the file until the next SECTION record.
#
# Parser sketch (Rust):
#   for line in input.lines():
#       if line starts with '#' or is empty: continue
#       if line == "END": finish current record
#       if line starts with "TYPE: ": start new record
#       else if in record: if line starts with two spaces -> append to
#           current list field; else parse "KEY: VALUE" pair
#
# GUIDING PRINCIPLES
# 1. Rules before examples. Examples illustrate rules.
# 2. Every rule has counterexamples. The AI learns from both.
# 3. Relationships are explicit (is_a, has, causes, part_of, ...).
# 4. Context resolves meaning; ambiguity is taught, not hidden.
# 5. Generalization: the AI must handle unseen sentences by applying rules.
#
# =============================================================================

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 1. ENGLISH FOUNDATIONS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.001
NAME: english_foundations
CONTENT: alphabet, case, vowels, consonants, digits, numbers, punctuation, symbols, abbreviations
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 1.1 THE ALPHABET
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.alphabet.summary
NAME: the_english_alphabet
LANGUAGE: English
SCRIPT: Latin
LETTERS_TOTAL: 26
VOWEL_LETTERS: a e i o u
CONSONANT_LETTERS: b c d f g h j k l m n p q r s t v w x y z
SEMIVOWEL_LETTERS: w y
NOTES:
  y acts as a vowel in 'my', 'gym', 'happy'
  w acts as a vowel in 'cow', 'low'
  The alphabet has 26 letters: 5 vowels and 21 consonants.
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.a
CHAR: a
UPPERCASE: A
LOWERCASE: a
CATEGORY: vowel
ORDER: 1
SOUNDS:
  short = /ae/ as in 'cat'
  long = /ei/ as in 'name'
  schwa = /uh/ as in 'about'
  article_variant: 'an' before vowel sounds
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.b
CHAR: b
UPPERCASE: B
LOWERCASE: b
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 2
SOUNDS:
  voiced_bilabial_stop = /b/ as in 'bat'
SILENT_IN:
  comb, climb, thumb, doubt, subtle
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.c
CHAR: c
UPPERCASE: C
LOWERCASE: c
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 3
SOUNDS:
  hard = /k/ as in 'cat'
  soft = /s/ before e,i,y as in 'city'
  ch = /tch/ as in 'chair'
NOTES:
  hard before a,o,u and consonants
  soft before e,i,y
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.d
CHAR: d
UPPERCASE: D
LOWERCASE: d
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 4
SOUNDS:
  voiced_alveolar_stop = /d/ as in 'dog'
SILENT_IN:
  edge, judge, badge (as part of 'dge')
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.e
CHAR: e
UPPERCASE: E
LOWERCASE: e
CATEGORY: vowel
ORDER: 5
SOUNDS:
  short = /e/ as in 'bed'
  long = /i:/ as in 'be'
  silent = final e marking long vowels as in 'make', 'bite'
  schwa = /uh/ as in 'the'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.f
CHAR: f
UPPERCASE: F
LOWERCASE: f
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 6
SOUNDS:
  voiceless_labiodental = /f/ as in 'fish'
SILENT_IN:
  half, calf, walk? no -> f silent in 'half', 'calf'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.g
CHAR: g
UPPERCASE: G
LOWERCASE: g
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 7
SOUNDS:
  hard = /g/ as in 'go'
  soft = /dzh/ before e,i,y as in 'gem', 'giant'
SILENT_IN:
  sign, design, foreign, gnaw
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.h
CHAR: h
UPPERCASE: H
LOWERCASE: h
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 8
SOUNDS:
  voiceless_glottal = /h/ as in 'hat'
SILENT_IN:
  hour, honest, honor, heir, vehicle
NOTES:
  silent h before 'o' in some French loanwords
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.i
CHAR: i
UPPERCASE: I
LOWERCASE: i
CATEGORY: vowel
ORDER: 9
SOUNDS:
  short = /i/ as in 'sit'
  long = /ai/ as in 'like'
  schwa = /uh/ as in 'animal'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.j
CHAR: j
UPPERCASE: J
LOWERCASE: j
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 10
SOUNDS:
  voiced_palato_alveolar = /dzh/ as in 'jump'
NOTES:
  j always has the same sound in English
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.k

  knee, knife, know, knock, knight
NOTES:
  silent k before n at word start
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.p
CHAR: p
UPPERCASE: P
LOWERCASE: p

ORDER: 16
SOUNDS:
  voiceless_bilabial = /p/ as in 'pen'
SILENT_IN:
  psychology, pneumonia, receipt, cupboard

  silent p before s and n in Greek loanwords
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.q
CHAR: q
UPPERCASE: Q
LOWERCASE: q
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 17
SOUNDS:
  /kw/ as in 'queen'
NOTES:
  q is almost always followed by u
  qu makes /kw/ or /k/ as in 'unique'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.r
CHAR: r
UPPERCASE: R
LOWERCASE: r
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 18
SOUNDS:
  voiced_alveolar_approx = /r/ as in 'run'
NOTES:
  r is silent in non-rhotic accents after vowels: 'car', 'mother'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.s
CHAR: s
UPPERCASE: S
LOWERCASE: s
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 19
SOUNDS:
  voiceless = /s/ as in 'sun'
  voiced = /z/ as in 'dogs', 'is', 'has'
  sh = /sh/ as in 'ship'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.t
CHAR: t
UPPERCASE: T
LOWERCASE: t
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 20
SOUNDS:
  voiceless_alveolar = /t/ as in 'top'
  tch = /tch/ as in 'watch'
SILENT_IN:
  listen, castle, whistle, often (optional)
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.u
CHAR: u
UPPERCASE: U
LOWERCASE: u
CATEGORY: vowel
ORDER: 21
SOUNDS:
  short = /uh/ as in 'cup'
  long = /ju:/ as in 'use'
  oo = /u:/ as in 'flute'
  ue = /u:/ as in 'blue'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.v
CHAR: v
UPPERCASE: V
LOWERCASE: v
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 22
SOUNDS:
  voiced_labiodental = /v/ as in 'van'
NOTES:
  v never ends a native English word
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.w
CHAR: w
UPPERCASE: W
LOWERCASE: w
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 23
SOUNDS:
  /w/ as in 'water'
SILENT_IN:
  write, wrong, wrap, answer, two, who, whole
NOTES:
  silent w before r at word start
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.x
CHAR: x
UPPERCASE: X
LOWERCASE: x
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 24
SOUNDS:
  /ks/ as in 'box'
  /gz/ as in 'exam'
  /z/ as in 'xylophone'
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.y
CHAR: y
UPPERCASE: Y
LOWERCASE: y
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 25
SOUNDS:
  consonant = /j/ as in 'yes'
  vowel_long_i = /ai/ as in 'my'
  vowel_long_e = /i:/ as in 'happy'
NOTES:
  y is a consonant at syllable start, a vowel elsewhere
END

TYPE: CHAR
ID: eng.alphabet.z
CHAR: z
UPPERCASE: Z
LOWERCASE: z
CATEGORY: consonant
ORDER: 26
SOUNDS:
  voiced_alveolar = /z/ as in 'zoo'
  sion/zz = /zh/ as in 'azure' (rare)
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.numbers.1_20
NAME: cardinal_numbers_1_20
USAGE: memorize these as base forms
VALUES:
  1 = one
  2 = two
  3 = three
  4 = four
  5 = five
  6 = six
  7 = seven
  8 = eight
  9 = nine
  10 = ten
  11 = eleven
  12 = twelve
  13 = thirteen
  14 = fourteen
  15 = fifteen
  16 = sixteen
  17 = seventeen
  18 = eighteen
  19 = nineteen
  20 = twenty
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.numbers.tens
NAME: tens_words
PATTERN: tens word + '-' + unit word for 21..99
VALUES:
  20 = twenty
  30 = thirty
  40 = forty
  50 = fifty
  60 = sixty
  70 = seventy
  80 = eighty
  90 = ninety
EXAMPLES:
  21 = twenty-one
  35 = thirty-five
  42 = forty-two
  58 = fifty-eight
  67 = sixty-seven
  74 = seventy-four
  86 = eighty-six
  93 = ninety-three
  99 = ninety-nine
END

TYPE: NUMBER
ID: eng.numbers.100
VALUE: 100
CARDINAL: one hundred
ORDINAL: hundredth
PATTERN: (one) + hundred + [tens-unit]
EXAMPLES:
  101 = one hundred and one
  110 = one hundred and ten
  125 = one hundred and twenty-five
  200 = two hundred
  300 = three hundred
  500 = five hundred
  999 = nine hundred and ninety-nine
END

TYPE: NUMBER
ID: eng.numbers.1000
VALUE: 1000
CARDINAL: one thousand
ORDINAL: thousandth
PATTERN: X thousand + [hundreds] + [tens-unit]
EXAMPLES:
  1000 = one thousand
  2500 = two thousand five hundred
  10000 = ten thousand
  25000 = twenty-five thousand
  100000 = one hundred thousand
  1000000 = one million
  1000000000 = one billion
  1000000000000 = one trillion
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.numbers.plural_units
NAME: plural_of_number_words
ACTION:
  numbers above one take plural unit nouns
EXAMPLES:
  three books
  five children
  two hundred people
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  three book
  two hundred peoples
WHY: unit nouns must agree in number with the numeral
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.numbers.and_rule
NAME: use_of_and_in_numbers
CONDITION:
  number has hundreds or thousands AND
  number is not a multiple of 100
ACTION:
  insert 'and' between hundreds and the tens/unit part
EXAMPLES:
  105 = one hundred and five
  2001 = two thousand and one
  2024 = two thousand and twenty-four
  345 = three hundred and forty-five
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  105 = one hundred five (US spoken form allowed)
  100 = one hundred and (no unit follows)
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.numbers.ordinals_1_20
NAME: ordinal_numbers_1_20
VALUES:
  1 = first
  2 = second
  3 = third
  4 = fourth
  5 = fifth
  6 = sixth
  7 = seventh
  8 = eighth
  9 = ninth
  10 = tenth
  11 = eleventh
  12 = twelfth
  13 = thirteenth
  14 = fourteenth
  15 = fifteenth
  16 = sixteenth
  17 = seventeenth
  18 = eighteenth
  19 = nineteenth
  20 = twentieth
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.numbers.ordinal_rule
NAME: forming_ordinals
CONDITION:
  cardinal ends in 'y' (twenty, thirty, ...)
ACTION:
  change y to ie + th
EXAMPLES:
  twenty -> twentieth
  thirty -> thirtieth
  ninety -> ninetieth
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  twenty -> twentyth
  one -> oneth
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.numbers.ordinal_composite
NAME: ordinals_for_composite_numbers
ACTION:
  only the last word becomes ordinal
EXAMPLES:
  21st = twenty-first
  42nd = forty-second
  73rd = seventy-third
  100th = one hundredth
  1000th = one thousandth
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  21st = first-twenty
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.numbers.ordinal_suffix
NAME: ordinal_suffixes
VALUES:
  1 = st
  2 = nd
  3 = rd
  4 = th
  5 = th
  11 = th
  12 = th
  13 = th
EXAMPLES:
  1st, 2nd, 3rd, 4th, 5th, 6th
  11th, 12th, 13th, 14th
  21st, 22nd, 23rd, 24th
  31st, 32nd, 33rd
NOTES:
  rule: teen numbers always use th; otherwise st/nd/rd follow 1/2/3
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 1.3 PUNCTUATION
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.period
CHAR: .
NAME: period
USES:
  ends declarative sentences
  ends imperatives (polite)
  marks abbreviations: Mr., Dr., e.g.
  decimals: 3.14
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.comma
CHAR: ,
NAME: comma
USES:
  separates list items: a, b, and c
  after introductory phrases: However, ...
  separates clauses: It rained, so we stayed.
  around appositives: My dog, Rex, barked.
  in dates: July 4, 1776
  in large numbers: 1,000,000
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.question_mark
CHAR: ?
NAME: question mark
USES:
  ends direct questions
  ends rhetorical questions
NOTES:
  indirect questions use a period: I asked if he was home.
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.exclamation_mark
CHAR: !
NAME: exclamation mark
USES:
  ends strong emotion: Stop!
  ends commands with emphasis
  interjections: Wow!
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.colon
CHAR: :
NAME: colon
USES:
  introduces a list: You need: a, b, c.
  introduces explanation: The rule is simple: practice.
  time: 3:30
  ratios: 2:1
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.semicolon
CHAR: ;
NAME: semicolon
USES:
  joins two related independent clauses: I came; I saw.
  separates complex list items: London, UK; Paris, France
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.apostrophe
CHAR: '
NAME: apostrophe
USES:
  contractions: don't, it's, I'm
  possession: John's book, the dogs' owner
  plurals of letters: A's, 1990's
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.quotation
CHAR: " "
NAME: quotation marks
USES:
  direct speech: He said, "Hello."
  titles of short works: "The Raven"
  scare quotes: a "solution"
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.single_quote
CHAR: ' '
NAME: single quotation marks
USES:
  quotes inside quotes: She said, "He yelled 'Stop!'"
  British English primary quotes
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.parentheses
CHAR: ( )
NAME: parentheses
USES:
  extra information: He (the doctor) arrived.
  numbers in lists: (1) first
  abbreviations: (etc.)
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.brackets
CHAR: [ ]
NAME: square brackets
USES:
  editorial insertions: He went to [the] school.
  corrections in quotes
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.dash
CHAR: -
NAME: hyphen
USES:
  compound words: mother-in-law, well-known
  numbers: twenty-one
  prefixes before capitals: pre-2000
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.em_dash
CHAR: --
NAME: em dash
USES:
  strong break: It was simple -- really.
  parenthetical aside: The dog -- a beagle -- barked.
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.ellipsis
CHAR: ...
NAME: ellipsis
USES:
  trailing thought: I wonder...
  omitted words: The rule... applies.
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.slash
CHAR: /
NAME: slash
USES:
  alternatives: and/or
  fractions: 1/2
  dates: 07/04/1776
  website addresses: https://example.com
END

TYPE: PUNCT
ID: eng.punct.underscore
CHAR: _
NAME: underscore
USES:
  replacing spaces in identifiers: file_name.txt
  emphasis in plain text
END

USAGE: title for men

FULL: Mistress
USAGE: title for married women
END

ID: eng.abbrev.title_ms

USAGE: title for women, marital status not stated
END

TYPE: ABBREV

ABBREV: Miss
FULL: Miss
USAGE: title for unmarried women (not an abbreviation)
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.title_dr
ABBREV: Dr.
FULL: Doctor
USAGE: title for medical or academic doctors
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.title_prof
ABBREV: Prof.
FULL: Professor
USAGE: academic title
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.title_sr
ABBREV: Sr.
FULL: Senior
USAGE: older of two with same name: John Smith Sr.
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.title_jr
ABBREV: Jr.
FULL: Junior
USAGE: younger of two with same name
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.time_am
ABBREV: a.m. / am
FULL: ante meridiem
MEANING: before noon
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.time_pm
ABBREV: p.m. / pm
FULL: post meridiem
MEANING: after noon
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.time_bc
ABBREV: BC
FULL: Before Christ
MEANING: years before year 1
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.time_ad
ABBREV: AD
FULL: Anno Domini
MEANING: years after year 1
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.etc
ABBREV: etc.
FULL: et cetera
MEANING: and so on
USAGE: I need pens, paper, etc.
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.eg
ABBREV: e.g.
FULL: exempli gratia
MEANING: for example
USAGE: fruits, e.g., apples
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.ie
ABBREV: i.e.
FULL: id est
MEANING: that is
USAGE: I.e. means clarification
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.nb
ABBREV: N.B.
FULL: nota bene
MEANING: note well
USAGE: N.B.: read the rules.
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.ps
ABBREV: P.S.
FULL: postscript
MEANING: after the letter
USAGE: P.S. Call me.
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.vs
ABBREV: vs.
FULL: versus
MEANING: against
USAGE: Team A vs. Team B
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.etc_units
ABBREV: cm, m, km, kg, g, l, ml
FULL: centimeter, meter, kilometer, kilogram, gram, liter, milliliter
USAGE: metric units; no period after metric abbreviations
END

TYPE: ABBREV
ID: eng.abbrev.directions
ABBREV: N, S, E, W, NE, NW, SE, SW
FULL: north, south, east, west, northeast, northwest, southeast, southwest
USAGE: compass directions
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.abbrev.common_short
NAME: common_abbreviations
VALUES:
  approx = approximately
  avg = average
  min = minimum
  max = maximum
  no. = number
  p. / pp. = page / pages
  ch. = chapter
  fig. = figure
  ed. = edition / editor
  vol. = volume
  sq. = square
  est. = estimated
  govt = government
  dept = department
  org = organization
  corp = corporation
  inc = incorporated
  ltd = limited
  vs = versus
  c/o = care of
  ASAP = as soon as possible
  FYI = for your information
  RSVP = please reply (French: repondez s'il vous plait)
  DIY = do it yourself
  ID = identification
  IQ = intelligence quotient
  VIP = very important person
  UFO = unidentified flying object
  ATM = automated teller machine
  GPS = global positioning system
  PDF = portable document format
  PC = personal computer
  TV = television
  CD = compact disc
  DVD = digital versatile disc
  USB = universal serial bus
  CEO = chief executive officer
  HR = human resources
  IT = information technology
  PhD = doctor of philosophy
  BA = bachelor of arts
  MA = master of arts
  St = street / saint
  Ave = avenue
  Rd = road
  Blvd = boulevard
  UK = United Kingdom
  USA = United States of America
  UN = United Nations
  EU = European Union
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.abbrev.period_rule
NAME: period_after_abbreviations
ACTION:
  abbreviations of single words take a period: Mr., Dr., St.
  acronyms spoken as words usually omit periods: NASA, NATO
  letter-by-letter acronyms may keep or omit periods: U.S.A. or USA
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 1.6 BASIC RULES ABOUT LETTERS AND WORDS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.capitalization
NAME: capitalization_rules
ACTION:
  capitalize the first word of every sentence
  capitalize the pronoun I
  capitalize proper nouns: John, London, Monday, January
  capitalize titles before names: Doctor Smith, Queen Elizabeth
  capitalize languages and nationalities: English, French
  capitalize the first word of a quote: He said, "Go."
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  john went to london.
  i am here.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.article_an
NAME: a_vs_an
CONDITION:
  next word starts with a vowel SOUND
ACTION:
  use 'an'
EXAMPLES:
  an apple
  an hour (silent h)
  an honest man
  an MP (letter M pronounced 'em')
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  a apple
  an book
WHY: choice depends on pronunciation, not spelling
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.syllable_basic
NAME: basic_syllable_rules
DEFINITION:
  a syllable contains one vowel sound
RULES:
  every syllable has exactly one vowel sound
  vowel + consonant = closed syllable: cat, sit
  consonant + vowel = open syllable: be, go
  two vowels together often make one sound: boat, rain
EXAMPLES:
  cat = 1 syllable
  table = 2 syllables
  banana = 3 syllables
  information = 4 syllables
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.found.case_pairs
NAME: uppercase_lowercase_pairs
CONTENT:
  A=a B=b C=c D=d E=e F=f G=g H=h I=i J=j K=k L=l M=m
  N=n O=o P=p Q=q R=r S=s T=t U=u V=v W=w X=x Y=y Z=z
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.spelling_ie
NAME: i_before_e_rule
ACTION:
  i before e except after c
EXAMPLES:
  believe, field, friend, piece
  receive, ceiling, deceive (after c)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  weird, science, efficient, ancient
  either, neither, protein
WHY: the rule has well-known exceptions that must be memorized
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.silent_e
NAME: magic_e_rule
CONDITION:
  word ends in consonant + e and vowel before consonant is short
ACTION:
  the final e makes the preceding vowel long
EXAMPLES:
  hat -> hate
  bit -> bite
  hop -> hope
  cut -> cute
  mad -> made
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.double_consonant
NAME: doubling_rule_when_adding_ing
CONDITION:
  word ends in CVC (consonant-vowel-consonant) with one syllable
ACTION:
  double the final consonant before -ing/-ed
EXAMPLES:
  run -> running
  sit -> sitting
  stop -> stopped
  swim -> swimming
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  open -> opening (stress not final)
  look -> looking (two vowels)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.y_to_i
NAME: y_to_i_rule
CONDITION:
  word ends in consonant + y
ACTION:
  change y to i before adding suffixes (except -ing)
EXAMPLES:
  happy -> happier, happiness
  cry -> cried
  carry -> carried
  beauty -> beautiful
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  cry -> crying (keep y before -ing)
  play -> played (vowel + y, keep y)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.found.e_drop
NAME: drop_silent_e_before_vowel_suffix
CONDITION:
  word ends in silent e AND suffix starts with a vowel
ACTION:
  drop the e
EXAMPLES:
  make -> making
  write -> writing
  hope -> hoped
  use -> usable
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  make -> makeing
  change -> changeable (keep e after soft g/c)
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 2. PARTS OF SPEECH
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.002
NAME: parts_of_speech
CONTENT: noun, pronoun, verb, helping verb, modal verb, adjective, adverb, preposition, conjunction, article, determiner, interjection
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.1 NOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.noun
NAME: noun
DEFINITION: a word that names a person, place, thing, idea, or quality
PROPERTIES:
  has_number = singular/plural
  has_case = common/possessive
  has_gender_meaning = natural gender only (he/she for people and animals)
  can_be_countable = yes/no
  can_be_proper = yes/no
  can_be_modified_by_adjectives
  can_be_preceded_by_determiners
TYPES:
  common = dog, city, book
  proper = London, John, Monday
  concrete = table, water
  abstract = love, freedom, happiness
  countable = apple, car, idea
  uncountable = water, rice, information
  collective = team, family, flock
  compound = mother-in-law, toothbrush, washing machine
  gerund = running, reading (verb form used as noun)
RULES:
  countable nouns take a/an and plurals: an apple, two apples
  uncountable nouns take no plural and no a/an: water, some water
  collective nouns are singular in form, may take singular or plural verb
  proper nouns are capitalized
  nouns form possessives with 's: John's book
RELATIONSHIPS:
  modified_by = adjective
  determined_by = determiner, article
  replaced_by = pronoun
  can_be = subject, object, complement, object of preposition
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pos.noun.common
NAME: common_nouns
VALUES:
  person = teacher, doctor, friend, child, student
  place = school, city, park, kitchen, office
  thing = book, car, computer, chair, phone
  idea = idea, truth, justice, memory, thought
  quality = beauty, strength, kindness, honesty, courage
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.noun.countable_uncountable
NAME: countable_vs_uncountable
CONDITION:
  noun is uncountable
ACTION:
  no plural form; no a/an; use much, little, some, a lot of
EXAMPLES:
  much water
  little information
  some rice
  a lot of money
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  many water
  an information
  three furnitures
WHY: uncountable nouns cannot be counted individually
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.noun.uncountable_plural_nouns
NAME: nouns_plural_in_form_singular_in_meaning
EXAMPLES:
  news = singular: The news is good.
  mathematics, physics, economics = singular
  scissors, trousers, glasses = plural (always plural)
  police = plural: The police are here.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The news are good.
  The police is here.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.noun.possession
NAME: possessive_forms
EXAMPLES:
  singular: the boy's toy
  plural regular: the boys' toys
  plural irregular: the children's toys
  compound: my mother-in-law's car
  shared possession: Tom and Mary's house (one house)
  separate possession: Tom's and Mary's houses (two houses)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  the boys's toys
  Tom and Mary's houses (when separate)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.noun.noun_adj
NAME: noun_used_as_adjective
ACTION:
  a noun before another noun acts as an adjective (attributive noun)
EXAMPLES:
  a school bus
  a kitchen table
  a computer screen
  a history teacher
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  a bus school
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.2 PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.pronoun
NAME: pronoun
DEFINITION: a word that replaces a noun or noun phrase
PROPERTIES:
  has_person = first/second/third
  has_number = singular/plural
  has_case = subjective/objective/possessive/reflexive
  has_gender = he/she/it (third person singular only)
TYPES:
  personal = I, you, he, she, it, we, they
  possessive = mine, yours, his, hers, ours, theirs
  reflexive = myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself, ourselves, yourselves, themselves
  demonstrative = this, that, these, those
  relative = who, whom, whose, which, that
  interrogative = who, whom, whose, which, what
  indefinite = someone, anyone, nobody, everybody, something, anything, nothing, everything
  reciprocal = each other, one another
RELATIONSHIPS:
  replaces = noun phrase
  antecedent = the noun the pronoun refers back to
RULES:
  pronoun must agree with antecedent in person, number, and gender
  subject pronouns are used as subjects
  object pronouns are used as objects and after prepositions
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.pronoun.antecedent
NAME: antecedent_agreement
CONDITION:
  pronoun refers to a singular noun
ACTION:
  use singular pronoun (he/she/it)
EXAMPLES:
  The dog wagged its tail.
  Mary lost her keys.
  Each student brought his or her book.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The dog wagged their tail. (singular antecedent)
  Mary lost his keys.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.pronoun.subject_object
NAME: subject_vs_object_pronouns
EXAMPLES:
  subject: I saw him.
  object: He saw me.
  after preposition: between you and me
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Me saw him.
  between you and I
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.pronoun.one
NAME: indefinite_one
USAGE:
  one must always take care.
  One should do one's best.
  singular 'they' is accepted for unknown gender: Someone left their phone.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.3 VERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.verb
NAME: verb
DEFINITION: a word that expresses an action, occurrence, or state of being
PROPERTIES:
  has_tense = present/past/future
  has_aspect = simple/continuous/perfect
  has_voice = active/passive
  has_mood = indicative/imperative/subjunctive
  has_person = first/second/third
  has_number = singular/plural
  has_transitivity = transitive/intransitive/ditransitive/linking
  has_form = base/infinitive/-s/-ing/past/past_participle
TYPES:
  action = run, eat, write
  state = know, love, believe, own
  transitive = kick (takes object)
  intransitive = sleep (no object)
  ditransitive = give (two objects)
  linking = be, seem, become (takes complement)
  regular = walk, walked, walked
  irregular = go, went, gone
  helping = be, have, do
  modal = can, could, may, might, must, shall, should, will, would
RELATIONSHIPS:
  agrees_with = subject (person and number)
  takes = object (if transitive)
  modified_by = adverb
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.verb.transitivity
NAME: transitive_vs_intransitive
EXAMPLES:
  transitive: She kicked the ball. (ball = object)
  intransitive: She slept. (no object)
  ditransitive: He gave Mary a gift. (Mary = indirect, gift = direct)
  transitive with two objects: She told him a story.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She kicked. (incomplete for 'kick')
  He gave Mary. (incomplete for 'give')
WHY: transitive verbs require an object to complete their meaning
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.verb.state_vs_action
NAME: stative_vs_dynamic_verbs
CONDITION:
  verb is stative
ACTION:
  do not use continuous tenses (usually)
EXAMPLES:
  I know the answer. (not: I am knowing)
  She loves music.
  He owns a car.
  This tastes good.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I am knowing the answer.
  She is loving music. (except temporary/emphatic use)
WHY: stative verbs describe states, not ongoing actions
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.4 HELPING (AUXILIARY) VERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.helping_verb
NAME: helping_verb (auxiliary)
DEFINITION: a verb used with a main verb to form tense, aspect, mood, voice, or negation
MEMBERS: be, have, do, will, would, shall, should, can, could, may, might, must
PROPERTIES:
  carries_tense = yes
  carries_negation = yes (adds not)
  carries_inversion = yes (moves before subject in questions)
  modal_behavior = no -s for third person singular
ROLES:
  be = progressive and passive
  have = perfect tenses
  do = questions and negation in simple tenses
  will/shall = future
  modals = ability, permission, obligation, possibility
RULES:
  negation: subject + auxiliary + not + main verb
  questions: auxiliary + subject + main verb
  contractions: don't, doesn't, isn't, wasn't, won't, can't
EXAMPLES:
  She is running. (progressive)
  The cake was eaten. (passive)
  He has finished. (perfect)
  Do you like tea?
  They don't know.
  Will you help?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She do running.
  Do she like tea?
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pos.helping.contractions
NAME: auxiliary_contractions
VALUES:
  am = 'm (I'm)
  is = 's (he's)
  are = 're (they're)
  have = 've (we've)
  has = 's (she's)
  had = 'd (I'd)
  will = 'll (you'll)
  would = 'd (I'd)
  do not = don't
  does not = doesn't
  did not = didn't
  is not = isn't
  are not = aren't
  was not = wasn't
  were not = weren't
  have not = haven't
  has not = hasn't
  had not = hadn't
  will not = won't
  would not = wouldn't
  cannot = can't
  could not = couldn't
  must not = mustn't
  shall not = shan't
  should not = shouldn't
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.5 MODAL VERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.modal
NAME: modal_verb
DEFINITION: an auxiliary verb expressing ability, permission, obligation, possibility, or prediction
MEMBERS: can, could, may, might, must, shall, should, will, would
PROPERTIES:
  no_s_form = never take -s: he can (not he cans)
  no_to = followed by bare infinitive: I must go
  no_ing = no -ing form
  no_ed = no past form (except could/would/should which are distinct modals)
  one_modal_per_clause = cannot combine: I might can go (wrong)
ROLES:
  can = ability, permission, possibility
  could = past ability, polite request, possibility
  may = permission, possibility
  might = weaker possibility
  must = obligation, strong certainty
  shall = future (I/we), suggestion (shall we?)
  should = advice, expectation
  will = future, prediction, willingness
  would = polite request, hypothetical, past habit
RULES:
  negation: modal + not + base verb
  questions: modal + subject + base verb
  past of modals: modal + have + past participle (must have, should have)
EXAMPLES:
  I can swim.
  Could you help me?
  You may enter.
  It might rain.
  You must wear a seatbelt.
  We shall overcome.
  You should rest.
  It will snow tomorrow.
  Would you like tea?
  He must have left. (past deduction)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He cans swim.
  I must to go.
  I might can go.
  She will to come.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.modal.meaning_table
NAME: modal_meaning_map
CONDITION:
  modal = can, subject = person
MEANING: ability
EXAMPLES:
  She can speak French.
  I can't swim.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.modal.permission
NAME: permission_modals
CONDITION:
  modal = can/may
MEANING: permission
EXAMPLES:
  Can I leave? (informal)
  May I leave? (formal)
  You may go now.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.modal.obligation
NAME: obligation_modals
CONDITION:
  modal = must/should/have to
MEANING: obligation
EXAMPLES:
  You must stop. (strong)
  You should apologize. (advice)
  I have to work. (external obligation)
  You don't have to come. (no obligation)
  You must not smoke here. (prohibition)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.modal.possibility
NAME: possibility_modals
CONDITION:
  modal = might/may/could
MEANING: possibility
EXAMPLES:
  It may rain. (possible)
  She might come. (less likely)
  That could be true.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.modal.past_forms
NAME: modal_have_past_participle
ACTION:
  modal + have + past participle = past time reference
EXAMPLES:
  He must have seen it. (deduction)
  She should have told me. (criticism)
  I could have won. (unrealized ability)
  You might have forgotten. (possibility)
  They would have come. (hypothetical)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.6 ADJECTIVES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.adjective
NAME: adjective
DEFINITION: a word that describes or modifies a noun or pronoun
PROPERTIES:
  has_comparison = positive/comparative/superlative
  has_order = opinion-size-age-shape-color-origin-material-purpose
  gradable = yes/no (big, bigger; but: perfect, unique)
  predicative_use = yes/no (the man is tall)
  attributive_use = yes/no (a tall man)
TYPES:
  descriptive = big, red, beautiful
  quantitative = some, many, few (with nouns)
  demonstrative = this, that, these, those (with nouns)
  possessive = my, your, his (with nouns)
  interrogative = which, whose, what (with nouns)
  proper = French, Shakespearean
  compound = well-known, five-year-old
  participial = interesting, interested
RULES:
  adjectives come before nouns in attributive position
  adjectives follow linking verbs in predicative position
  multiple adjectives follow a standard order
  participles: -ing = causing, -ed = experiencing
EXAMPLES:
  a beautiful red Chinese vase
  The man is tall.
  an interesting film (the film interests you)
  an interested audience (the audience is interested)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  a red beautiful vase
  He is interesting in the film. (should be interested)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.adj.order
NAME: adjective_order
ACTION:
  opinion -> size -> age -> shape -> color -> origin -> material -> purpose -> noun
EXAMPLES:
  a lovely small old round brown Italian wooden dining table
  three big new red plastic cups
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  a wooden brown table
  a small lovely dog
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.adj.gradable
NAME: gradable_vs_non_gradable
CONDITION:
  adjective is non-gradable (extreme or absolute)
ACTION:
  do not use comparative/superlative or very
EXAMPLES:
  perfect, unique, dead, married, frozen
  very good (gradable) vs absolutely perfect (non-gradable)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  more perfect
  very unique
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.7 ADVERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.adverb
NAME: adverb
DEFINITION: a word that modifies a verb, an adjective, another adverb, or a whole sentence
PROPERTIES:
  has_type = manner/place/time/frequency/degree
  has_position = front/mid/end of clause
  many_formed_from = adjective + -ly
PROPERTIES_NOTE:
  flat adverbs: fast, hard, late, well (same as adjective)
  sentence adverbs: fortunately, honestly, actually
TYPES:
  manner = quickly, slowly, well, hard
  place = here, there, everywhere, inside
  time = now, then, today, yesterday
  frequency = always, usually, often, sometimes, never
  degree = very, too, quite, almost, extremely
  sentence = luckily, obviously, surprisingly
  interrogative = when, where, why, how
  relative = when, where, why (in clauses)
RULES:
  manner adverbs usually follow the verb or object
  frequency adverbs come before the main verb but after be
  sentence adverbs come first and are set off by a comma
EXAMPLES:
  She sings beautifully.
  He always arrives early.
  She is always happy.
  Fortunately, we won.
  He drives very carefully.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He sings very.
  Always he is late. (for 'always' mid-position preferred)
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pos.adv.frequency
NAME: frequency_adverb_order
VALUES:
  always = 100%
  usually = 90%
  normally / generally = 80%
  often = 70%
  sometimes = 50%
  occasionally = 30%
  rarely / seldom = 10%
  hardly ever = 5%
  never = 0%
EXAMPLES:
  He always comes. (before main verb)
  He is always here. (after be)
  I have never seen it. (after auxiliary)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.adv.ly
NAME: adverb_formation
ACTION:
  adjective + ly = adverb (most adjectives)
  adjective ending in -y: change y to i + ly
  adjective ending in -le: drop e + y
  adjective ending in -ic: add -ally
EXAMPLES:
  quick -> quickly
  happy -> happily
  simple -> simply
  basic -> basically
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  happy -> happyly
  fast -> fastly (fast is already an adverb)
  good -> goodly (well is the adverb)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.8 PREPOSITIONS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.preposition
NAME: preposition
DEFINITION: a word that shows the relationship between a noun/pronoun and another word in the sentence
PROPERTIES:
  takes_an_object = noun/pronoun/gerund (the object of the preposition)
  forms = prepositional phrase: prep + object
  one_word = in, on, at, by, with, from, to, of
  multi_word = in front of, next to, because of, instead of
CATEGORIES:
  place = in, on, at, under, over, between, behind, in front of, next to
  time = in, on, at, before, after, during, since, until, by
  direction = to, into, onto, towards, through, across, along, up, down
  manner = by, with, like, in
  cause = because of, due to, owing to
  possession = of
  agent = by (passive)
  accompaniment = with
  purpose = for
RULES:
  prepositions precede their object
  prepositions take object pronouns: for me, with him
  gerund after preposition: interested in reading
  preposition stranding is common in questions: Who did you talk to?
EXAMPLES:
  The cat is on the table.
  We meet at 5 o'clock.
  I go to school by bus.
  She is good at math.
  He left because of the rain.
  Who are you waiting for?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  on the table the cat is (word order)
  interested in read (should be reading)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.time_rules
NAME: prepositions_of_time
CONDITION:
  time unit = year, month, century
ACTION:
  use in
EXAMPLES:
  in 2024
  in March
  in the 21st century
  in the morning
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.time_rules_day
NAME: prepositions_of_time_days
CONDITION:
  time unit = day, date
ACTION:
  use on
EXAMPLES:
  on Monday
  on July 4th
  on my birthday
  on Christmas Day
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.time_rules_clock
NAME: prepositions_of_time_clock
CONDITION:
  time unit = clock time, night, weekend
ACTION:
  use at
EXAMPLES:
  at 5 o'clock
  at midnight
  at night
  at the weekend
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.place_rules
NAME: prepositions_of_place
CONDITION:
  context = enclosed space
ACTION:
  use in
EXAMPLES:
  in the room
  in the box
  in the city
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.place_rules_surface
NAME: prepositions_of_place_surface
CONDITION:
  context = surface
ACTION:
  use on
EXAMPLES:
  on the table
  on the wall
  on the floor
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.prep.place_rules_point
NAME: prepositions_of_place_point
CONDITION:
  context = specific point
ACTION:
  use at
EXAMPLES:
  at the door
  at the bus stop
  at the corner
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.9 CONJUNCTIONS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.conjunction
NAME: conjunction
DEFINITION: a word that joins words, phrases, or clauses
TYPES:
  coordinating = for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so (FANBOYS)
  subordinating = because, although, if, when, while, since, unless, until, after, before, that, whether, as
  correlative = both...and, either...or, neither...nor, not only...but also, whether...or
RULES:
  coordinating conjunctions join equal elements
  subordinating conjunctions introduce dependent clauses
  correlative conjunctions come in pairs
  comma before coordinating conjunction joining two independent clauses
  no comma before subordinating conjunction at clause end
EXAMPLES:
  I like tea and coffee.
  He was tired, so he slept.
  Because it rained, we stayed home.
  We stayed home because it rained.
  Both Tom and Mary came.
  Neither John nor Jane was there.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Because it rained, so we stayed home. (double connector)
  Both Tom or Mary came.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning
NAME: conjunction_meaning_map
CONDITION:
  conjunction = and
MEANING: addition
EXAMPLES:
  I bought bread and milk.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_but
NAME: conjunction_meaning_but
CONDITION:
  conjunction = but
MEANING: contrast
EXAMPLES:
  He is small but strong.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_or
NAME: conjunction_meaning_or
CONDITION:
  conjunction = or
MEANING: alternative
EXAMPLES:
  Tea or coffee?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_so
NAME: conjunction_meaning_so
CONDITION:
  conjunction = so
MEANING: result
EXAMPLES:
  It rained, so I took an umbrella.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_yet
NAME: conjunction_meaning_yet
CONDITION:
  conjunction = yet
MEANING: contrast (stronger than but)
EXAMPLES:
  He is poor yet honest.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_nor
NAME: conjunction_meaning_nor
CONDITION:
  conjunction = nor
MEANING: negative addition
EXAMPLES:
  He doesn't smoke, nor does he drink.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.conj.meaning_for
NAME: conjunction_meaning_for
CONDITION:
  conjunction = for
MEANING: cause (formal)
EXAMPLES:
  He left, for he was tired. (formal)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.10 ARTICLES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.article
NAME: article
DEFINITION: a determiner that marks definiteness or indefiniteness of a noun
MEMBERS:
  definite = the
  indefinite = a, an
  zero = (no article)
PROPERTIES:
  no_plural_form
  no_agreement
  position = before noun (and before adjectives)
RULES:
  a/an = one, non-specific, first mention
  the = specific, known, second mention, unique
  zero article = general statements, uncountable in general, plural in general
  a/an before consonant/vowel SOUND
EXAMPLES:
  I saw a dog. The dog was black.
  The sun rises in the east. (unique)
  Water is essential. (general, zero article)
  Dogs are loyal. (general plural, zero article)
  an hour (silent h)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I saw dog.
  The water is essential. (when meaning all water generally)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.article.first_second_mention
NAME: first_vs_second_mention
CONDITION:
  noun mentioned first time, indefinite
ACTION:
  use a/an
CONDITION2:
  noun mentioned again, now specific
ACTION2:
  use the
EXAMPLES:
  I bought a car. The car is red.
  There was a man. The man waved.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.article.geography
NAME: articles_with_geography
ACTION:
  the + rivers, seas, oceans, mountain ranges, deserts, plural countries, canals
  no article + single mountains, most countries, cities, lakes
EXAMPLES:
  the Thames, the Pacific, the Alps, the Sahara, the Netherlands
  Mount Everest, Japan, Paris, Lake Victoria
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  the Mount Everest
  Thames
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.article.jobs
NAME: articles_with_jobs_and_roles
ACTION:
  a/an before singular job nouns after be
EXAMPLES:
  She is a doctor.
  He became a pilot.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She is doctor.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.11 DETERMINERS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.determiner
NAME: determiner
DEFINITION: a word that introduces a noun and specifies it (definiteness, quantity, possession)
PROPERTIES:
  position = first in the noun phrase
  always_before = adjectives and noun
  mutually_exclusive = two determiners cannot usually combine
TYPES:
  articles = a, an, the
  demonstratives = this, that, these, those
  possessives = my, your, his, her, its, our, their
  quantifiers = some, any, many, much, few, little, several, all, each, every, both, either, neither, no
  numbers = one, two, three...
  distributives = each, every, either, neither
  interrogatives = which, what, whose
  relative = whose, which, that
RULES:
  determiners agree with countability: many + countable, much + uncountable
  each/every + singular noun
  both/either/neither + plural noun (both) / singular noun (either, neither)
  this/that + singular; these/those + plural
EXAMPLES:
  this book, these books
  my car, our house
  some water, any questions
  each student, every day
  many people, much time
  both hands, either hand
  no problem
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  these book
  much people
  each students
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.det.some_any
NAME: some_vs_any
CONDITION:
  sentence is affirmative
ACTION:
  use some
CONDITION2:
  sentence is negative or question (offering/asking yes)
ACTION2:
  use any
EXAMPLES:
  I have some money.
  I don't have any money.
  Do you have any questions?
  Would you like some tea? (offer)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't have some money.
  Do you have some questions? (informal offer only)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.det.many_much
NAME: many_vs_much
CONDITION:
  noun is countable plural
ACTION:
  use many
CONDITION2:
  noun is uncountable
ACTION2:
  use much
EXAMPLES:
  many books
  much water
  too many people
  too much noise
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  much books
  many water
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.det.each_every
NAME: each_vs_every
EXAMPLES:
  each: two or more, individual focus: Each student has a book.
  every: three or more, group focus: Every student passed.
  both take singular nouns and singular verbs
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Each students
  Every of them came. (should be: Each of them / Every one of them)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.12 INTERJECTIONS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: POS
ID: eng.pos.interjection
NAME: interjection
DEFINITION: a word or short phrase expressing emotion, not grammatically connected to the rest of the sentence
PROPERTIES:
  stands_alone = yes
  no_inflection = no plural/tense
  often_followed_by = exclamation mark or comma
TYPES:
  joy = wow, yay, hurray, great
  surprise = oh, ah, wow, gosh, really
  pain = ouch, ow, oof
  disgust = yuck, ugh, ew
  agreement = yes, yeah, right, okay
  disagreement = no, nope, nah
  attention = hey, hello, listen
  relief = phew, thank goodness
  hesitation = um, er, uh, hmm
  call = hey, psst, yo
RULES:
  strong emotion -> exclamation mark
  mild emotion -> comma
  interjections can be sounds, not words: oops, aha, shh
EXAMPLES:
  Wow! That's amazing.
  Ouch! That hurt.
  Hmm, let me think.
  Yes, I agree.
  Ugh, this is hard.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Wow that's amazing! (punctuation should follow interjection directly)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 2.13 PART OF SPEECH AMBIGUITY
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pos.ambiguity
NAME: words_that_change_part_of_speech
VALUES:
  run = verb (I run) / noun (a run)
  play = verb (play games) / noun (a play)
  light = noun (the light) / verb (light a fire) / adjective (a light bag)
  fast = adjective (fast car) / adverb (run fast)
  well = adverb (do well) / adjective (I am well) / noun (a well)
  book = noun (a book) / verb (book a room)
  watch = verb (watch TV) / noun (a watch)
  walk = verb (walk home) / noun (a walk)
  cook = verb (cook dinner) / noun (a cook)
  water = noun (drink water) / verb (water the plants)
  taste = noun (a good taste) / verb (taste the soup) / linking verb (it tastes good)
  like = verb (I like tea) / preposition (like you = similar to)
  round = noun (a round) / adjective (a round ball) / preposition (round the corner) / verb (round off)
  close = adjective (a close friend) / verb (close the door)
  present = noun (a present) / verb (present a talk) / adjective (present here)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pos.ambiguity.detect
NAME: how_to_detect_part_of_speech
ACTION:
  examine the word's position and the words around it
  if after a determiner -> noun (the water)
  if after a subject and before an object -> verb (water the plants)
  if before a noun -> adjective or determiner (a close friend)
  if after a linking verb -> adjective (it tastes good)
  if modifies a verb -> adverb (run fast)
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 3. WORD KNOWLEDGE
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.003
NAME: word_knowledge
CONTENT: structured word records with morphology, grammar, and meaning fields
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.words.format
NAME: word_record_fields
FIELD_LIST:
  text = the word
  type = part of speech
  lemma = dictionary form
  root = base morpheme
  prefix = derivational prefix if any
  suffix = derivational suffix if any
  plural / singular = number forms (nouns)
  tense_forms = base, -s, -ing, past, past participle (verbs)
  person = 1/2/3 (pronouns and verbs)
  number = singular/plural
  gender = masculine/feminine/neuter
  synonyms = same or similar meaning
  antonyms = opposite meaning
  related_words = morphological or semantic relatives
  semantic_category = meaning class
  countability = countable/uncountable (nouns)
  comparative / superlative = (adjectives and adverbs)
  meaning = definition
NOTES:
  a field is omitted when not applicable
  the format is: FIELD: value or multi-line indented lists
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.used_to
text: used (to)
type: verb
lemma: used to
tense_forms: used to (past only)
person: all
number: all
meaning: past habit or state that no longer exists
EXAMPLES:
  I used to play tennis. (past habit)
  He used to live here. (past state)
NEGATIVE: didn't use to
QUESTION: Did you use to ...?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I use to play tennis. (present)
WHY: 'used to' has a special past-only grammar
END

related_words: bigness (noun, rare)
semantic_category: size
meaning: of large size
END

text: small
type: adjective
lemma: small
comparative: smaller

antonyms: big, large, huge
semantic_category: size
meaning: of little size
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.good
text: good
type: adjective
lemma: good
comparative: better
superlative: best
synonyms: fine, excellent, great, nice
antonyms: bad, poor, terrible
related_words: goodness (noun), well (adverb)
semantic_category: quality
meaning: of high quality; morally right
NOTES: irregular comparison

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 3.4 COMMON DETERMINERS, PRONOUNS, ADVERBS, PREPOSITIONS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.many
text: many
type: determiner
lemma: many
comparative: more
superlative: most
synonyms: numerous, plenty of
antonyms: few
semantic_category: quantity
usage: countable plural nouns
EXAMPLES:
  many books
  many people
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.much
text: much
type: determiner
lemma: much
comparative: more
superlative: most
synonyms: a lot of
antonyms: little
semantic_category: quantity
usage: uncountable nouns
EXAMPLES:
  much water
  much time
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.few
text: few
type: determiner
lemma: few
comparative: fewer
superlative: fewest
antonyms: many
semantic_category: quantity
usage: countable plural nouns
NOTES: few = not many (negative); a few = some (positive)
EXAMPLES:
  few people came. (almost nobody)
  a few people came. (some people)
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.little_det
text: little
type: determiner
lemma: little
comparative: less
superlative: least
antonyms: much
semantic_category: quantity
usage: uncountable nouns
NOTES: little = not much (negative); a little = some (positive)
EXAMPLES:
  little water left. (almost none)
  a little water, please. (some water)
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.all
text: all
type: determiner
lemma: all
synonyms: every, whole
antonyms: none
semantic_category: quantity
usage: all + plural noun, all + uncountable, all of + pronoun
EXAMPLES:
  all students
  all the water
  all of them
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.none
text: none
type: pronoun
lemma: none
synonyms: not any, nobody (for people)
antonyms: all, some
semantic_category: quantity
usage: none of + noun/pronoun
EXAMPLES:
  None of them came.
  None of the water is left.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.some
text: some
type: determiner
lemma: some
synonyms: a few, several, a little
antonyms: none, no
semantic_category: quantity
usage: affirmative sentences; offers and requests
EXAMPLES:
  some books
  some water
  Would you like some tea?
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.any
text: any
type: determiner
lemma: any
synonyms: some (in negatives/questions)
semantic_category: quantity
usage: negative sentences and questions
EXAMPLES:
  I don't have any money.
  Do you have any questions?
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.every
text: every
type: determiner
lemma: every
synonyms: each, all
semantic_category: quantity
usage: singular noun + singular verb
EXAMPLES:
  every day
  every student is here
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.each
text: each
type: determiner
lemma: each
synonyms: every
semantic_category: quantity
usage: individual focus, singular
EXAMPLES:
  each child gets a gift
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.here
text: here
type: adverb
lemma: here
synonyms: at this place
antonyms: there
semantic_category: place
EXAMPLES:
  Come here.
  Here is your book.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.there
text: there
type: adverb
lemma: there
synonyms: at that place
antonyms: here
semantic_category: place
NOTES: existential 'there': There is a cat. There are three cats.
EXAMPLES:
  Go there.
  There is a problem.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.never
text: never
type: adverb
lemma: never

semantic_category: frequency
frequency: 0%
NOTES: negative adverb: makes the sentence negative without 'not'
EXAMPLES:
  I never smoke.
  I have never been there.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.very
text: very
type: adverb
lemma: very
synonyms: extremely, really, highly
semantic_category: degree
usage: modifies adjectives and adverbs, not verbs
EXAMPLES:
  very good
  very quickly
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I very like it. (use: I like it very much)
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.too
text: too
type: adverb
lemma: too
synonyms: also (meaning 1), excessively (meaning 2)
semantic_category: degree
NOTES:
  too + adjective = more than wanted
  too = also at end of sentence
EXAMPLES:
  The soup is too hot. (excess)
  I want to go too. (also)
END

TYPE: WORD

  enough water
END

TYPE: WORD

type: conjunction

semantic_category: cause

EXAMPLES:
  I stayed because it was raining.
  because of + noun: because of the rain
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  because the rain (use because of)
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.although
text: although
type: conjunction
lemma: although
synonyms: though, even though, while
semantic_category: contrast
meaning: introduces a contrast or concession
EXAMPLES:
  Although it rained, we played.
  We played although it rained.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.if
text: if
type: conjunction
lemma: if
synonyms: whether (in some cases), provided that
semantic_category: condition
meaning: introduces a condition
EXAMPLES:
  If it rains, we will stay.
  I don't know if he is here. (whether)
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.when
text: when
type: conjunction
lemma: when
synonyms: while, at the time that
semantic_category: time
meaning: introduces a time clause
EXAMPLES:
  When I arrived, they left.
  Call me when you are ready.
END

TYPE: WORD
ID: eng.word.while
text: while
type: conjunction
lemma: while
synonyms: during the time that, whereas
semantic_category: time, contrast
meaning: during; in contrast (while = whereas)
EXAMPLES:
  While I slept, it rained.
  Tom is tall while John is short. (contrast)
END

END

meaning: a building for people to live in
END

semantic_category: place, home

NOTES: at home (no article); home as adverb: go home (no to)

  go to home
END

meaning: a bright point in the night sky; a famous person
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 4. MORPHOLOGY
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.004
NAME: morphology
CONTENT: plural formation, prefixes, suffixes, roots, derivation, inflection, conjugation, comparison
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 4.1 PLURAL FORMATION
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.default
NAME: regular_plural
CONDITION:
  noun is countable
  noun ends in a consonant or vowel other than s,x,z,ch,sh,y,o
ACTION:
  add -s
EXAMPLES:
  cat -> cats
  dog -> dogs
  book -> books
  car -> cars
  table -> tables
  house -> houses
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  child -> childs
  foot -> foots
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.sibilant
NAME: plural_after_sibilants
CONDITION:
  noun ends in s, x, z, ch, or sh
ACTION:
  add -es
EXAMPLES:
  bus -> buses
  box -> boxes
  quiz -> quizzes
  church -> churches
  dish -> dishes
  glass -> glasses
  watch -> watches
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  bus -> buss
  box -> boxs
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.y
NAME: plural_after_consonant_y
CONDITION:
  noun ends in consonant + y
ACTION:
  change y to ies
EXAMPLES:
  city -> cities
  baby -> babies
  family -> families
  story -> stories
  berry -> berries
  party -> parties
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  city -> citys
  boy -> boies (vowel + y keeps y)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.y_vowel
NAME: plural_after_vowel_y
CONDITION:
  noun ends in vowel + y
ACTION:
  add -s (keep y)
EXAMPLES:
  boy -> boys
  day -> days
  toy -> toys
  key -> keys
  monkey -> monkeys
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.f
NAME: plural_after_f_or_fe
CONDITION:
  noun ends in f or fe (some nouns)
ACTION:
  change f/fe to ves
EXAMPLES:
  leaf -> leaves
  wolf -> wolves
  knife -> knives
  wife -> wives
  half -> halves
  shelf -> shelves
  life -> lives
  thief -> thieves
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  roof -> roofs (not rooves)
  chief -> chiefs
  belief -> beliefs
  safe -> safes
WHY: some f-ending nouns take regular -s; must be learned
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.o
NAME: plural_after_o
CONDITION:
  noun ends in o
ACTION:
  usually add -s; some add -es
EXAMPLES:
  piano -> pianos
  photo -> photos
  radio -> radios
  zoo -> zoos
  tomato -> tomatoes
  potato -> potatoes
  hero -> heroes
  echo -> echoes
WHY: o-ending plurals must be memorized in groups
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.irregular_vowel
NAME: irregular_vowel_change
EXAMPLES:
  man -> men
  woman -> women
  child -> children
  foot -> feet
  tooth -> teeth
  goose -> geese
  mouse -> mice
  louse -> lice
  person -> people
  ox -> oxen
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.unchanged
NAME: unchanged_plurals
EXAMPLES:
  sheep -> sheep
  fish -> fish (fishes = species)
  deer -> deer
  series -> series
  species -> species
  aircraft -> aircraft
  salmon -> salmon
NOTES:
  some animals have the same singular and plural
  with numbers: three sheep, two fish
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  three sheeps
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.latin
NAME: latin_and_greek_plurals
EXAMPLES:
  datum -> data
  medium -> media
  criterion -> criteria
  phenomenon -> phenomena
  analysis -> analyses
  basis -> bases
  crisis -> crises
  thesis -> theses
  axis -> axes
  focus -> foci/focuses
  cactus -> cacti/cactuses
  curriculum -> curricula/curriculums
  appendix -> appendices/appendixes
  index -> indices/indexes
  matrix -> matrices
  formula -> formulae/formulas
  bacterium -> bacteria
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.compound
NAME: compound_noun_plurals
ACTION:
  pluralize the main word of the compound
EXAMPLES:
  mother-in-law -> mothers-in-law
  passer-by -> passers-by
  toothbrush -> toothbrushes
  grown-up -> grown-ups
  woman doctor -> women doctors
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  mother-in-laws
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.plural.letters
NAME: plurals_of_letters_and_numbers
ACTION:
  add 's or just s
EXAMPLES:
  the 1990s
  two As
  mind your p's and q's
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.morph.plural.exceptions_table
NAME: important_plural_exceptions
VALUES:
  child = children
  man = men
  woman = women
  person = people
  foot = feet
  tooth = teeth
  goose = geese
  mouse = mice
  ox = oxen
  sheep = sheep
  fish = fish
  deer = deer
  money = (no plural; monies legal)
  news = (always singular)
  police = (always plural)
  clothes = (always plural)
  trousers = (always plural)
  scissors = (always plural)
  glasses = (always plural)
  thanks = (always plural)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 4.2 PREFIXES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.un
PREFIX: un-
MEANING: not; reverse of
USAGE: adjectives, verbs
EXAMPLES:
  happy -> unhappy
  lucky -> unlucky
  tie -> untie
  lock -> unlock
  do -> undo
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  good -> ungood (not a word)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.dis
PREFIX: dis-
MEANING: not; opposite of
USAGE: adjectives, verbs, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  agree -> disagree
  like -> dislike
  appear -> disappear
  honest -> dishonest
  order -> disorder
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.in
PREFIX: in-
MEANING: not
USAGE: adjectives (with il-, im-, ir- variants)
EXAMPLES:
  correct -> incorrect
  complete -> incomplete
  direct -> indirect
  possible -> impossible (im-)
  legal -> illegal (il-)
  regular -> irregular (ir-)
  responsible -> irresponsible (ir-)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.re
PREFIX: re-
MEANING: again; back
USAGE: verbs, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  do -> redo
  read -> reread
  build -> rebuild
  turn -> return
  write -> rewrite
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.pre
PREFIX: pre-
MEANING: before
USAGE: verbs, adjectives, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  view -> preview
  heat -> preheat
  school -> preschool
  pay -> prepay
  war -> prewar
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.post
PREFIX: post-
MEANING: after
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  war -> postwar
  graduate -> postgraduate
  pone -> postpone (root changed)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.over
PREFIX: over-
MEANING: too much; above
USAGE: verbs, nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  eat -> overeat
  sleep -> oversleep
  load -> overload
  head -> overhead
  time -> overtime
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.under
PREFIX: under-
MEANING: too little; below
USAGE: verbs, nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  estimate -> underestimate
  cook -> undercook
  ground -> underground
  water -> underwater
  age -> underage
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.mis
PREFIX: mis-
MEANING: wrongly; badly
USAGE: verbs, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  understand -> misunderstand
  spell -> misspell
  lead -> mislead
  take -> mistake
  use -> misuse
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.mid
PREFIX: mid-
MEANING: middle
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  night -> midnight
  day -> midday
  air -> midair
  summer -> midsummer
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.inter
PREFIX: inter-
MEANING: between; among
USAGE: adjectives, verbs, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  national -> international
  net -> internet
  view -> interview
  act -> interact
  state -> interstate
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.anti
PREFIX: anti-
MEANING: against
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  war -> antiwar
  freeze -> antifreeze
  social -> antisocial
  virus -> antivirus
  clockwise -> anticlockwise
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.pro
PREFIX: pro-
MEANING: in favor of; forward
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  war -> prowar
  active -> proactive
  logue -> prologue (forward speech)
  mote -> promote (move forward)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.ex
PREFIX: ex-
MEANING: former; out of
USAGE: nouns
EXAMPLES:
  husband -> ex-husband
  president -> ex-president
  tract -> extract (pull out)
  port -> export (carry out)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.co
PREFIX: co-
MEANING: together; joint
USAGE: nouns, verbs, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  worker -> co-worker
  exist -> coexist
  pilot -> co-pilot
  operate -> cooperate
  author -> coauthor
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.auto
PREFIX: auto-
MEANING: self
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  biography -> autobiography
  graph -> autograph
  mobile -> automobile
  pilot -> autopilot
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.tele
PREFIX: tele-
MEANING: far; distant
USAGE: nouns
EXAMPLES:
  phone -> telephone
  vision -> television
  scope -> telescope
  gram -> telegram
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.bi
PREFIX: bi-
MEANING: two
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  cycle -> bicycle
  lingual -> bilingual
  weekly -> biweekly
  annual -> biannual
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.tri
PREFIX: tri-
MEANING: three
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  cycle -> tricycle
  angle -> triangle
  lingual -> trilingual
  pod -> tripod
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.multi
PREFIX: multi-
MEANING: many
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  media -> multimedia
  national -> multinational
  cultural -> multicultural
  purpose -> multipurpose
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.semi
PREFIX: semi-
MEANING: half; partly
USAGE: adjectives, nouns
EXAMPLES:
  circle -> semicircle
  final -> semifinal
  sweet -> semisweet
  automatic -> semiautomatic
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.sub
PREFIX: sub-
MEANING: under; below; lesser
USAGE: nouns, adjectives, verbs
EXAMPLES:
  marine -> submarine
  way -> subway
  title -> subtitle
  zero -> subzero
  standard -> substandard
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.super
PREFIX: super-
MEANING: above; beyond; very
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  market -> supermarket
  hero -> superhero
  natural -> supernatural
  man -> superman
  star -> superstar
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.trans
PREFIX: trans-
MEANING: across; through
USAGE: nouns, adjectives, verbs
EXAMPLES:
  atlantic -> transatlantic
  port -> transport
  late -> translate
  action -> transaction
  parent -> transparent (show through)
END

TYPE: PREFIX
ID: eng.prefix.non
PREFIX: non-
MEANING: not
USAGE: nouns, adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  stop -> nonstop
  fiction -> nonfiction
  smoker -> nonsmoker
  violent -> nonviolent
  profit -> nonprofit
END

# 4.3 SUFFIXES

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.er
SUFFIX: -er
MEANING: one who does (agent noun); more (comparative)
USAGE: verbs -> nouns; adjectives -> adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  teach -> teacher
  write -> writer
  run -> runner
  big -> bigger
  fast -> faster
END

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.or
SUFFIX: -or
MEANING: one who does (agent noun, often Latin origin)
USAGE: verbs -> nouns
EXAMPLES:
  act -> actor
  direct -> director
  visit -> visitor
  invent -> inventor
  edit -> editor
END

TYPE: SUFFIX

  dirt -> dirty
  sleep -> sleepy

END

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.ing
SUFFIX: -ing
MEANING: action in progress; gerund; participle
USAGE: verbs -> nouns, adjectives, verb forms
EXAMPLES:
  swim -> swimming (gerund: Swimming is fun.)
  run -> running (progressive: He is running.)
  interest -> interesting (adjective: an interesting book)
  read -> reading (noun: Reading is useful.)
END

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.ed
SUFFIX: -ed
MEANING: past tense; past participle; having
USAGE: verbs -> verb forms; nouns -> adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  walk -> walked
  play -> played
  interest -> interested (adjective: an interested audience)
  skill -> skilled (adjective)
  learn -> learned
END

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.est

MEANING: most (superlative)
USAGE: adjectives, adverbs
EXAMPLES:
  big -> biggest
  fast -> fastest
  happy -> happiest
  early -> earliest
END

TYPE: SUFFIX
ID: eng.suffix.ship

  friend -> friendship

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 4.4 ROOTS AND DERIVATION
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.morph.root_types
NAME: root_word_types
CONTENT:
  root = base of a word that cannot be reduced further
  free root = stands alone: 'play' in playful
  bound root = cannot stand alone: 'ject' in reject, inject
  prefix + root = prefixation: re + play = replay
  root + suffix = suffixation: play + ful = playful
  prefix + root + suffix = both: un + play + able = unplayable
  compound = two free roots: black + board = blackboard
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.happy
ROOT: happy
DERIVED:
  happy (adjective)
  happiness (noun)
  happily (adverb)
  unhappy (adjective)
  unhappily (adverb)
  unhappiness (noun)
RELATIONSHIP:
  happy -> adjective
  happiness -> noun
  happily -> adverb
  unhappy -> adjective (negation)
NOTES: prefix un- + suffix -ness/-ly form the family
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.use
ROOT: use
DERIVED:
  use (verb/noun)
  useful (adjective)
  useless (adjective)
  user (noun)
  usage (noun)
  used (adjective/verb)
  reuse (verb)
RELATIONSHIP:
  use + ful -> useful (full of use)
  use + less -> useless (without use)
  use + er -> user (person who uses)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.create
ROOT: create
DERIVED:
  create (verb)
  creator (noun)
  creation (noun)
  creative (adjective)
  creativity (noun)
  creature (noun)
RELATIONSHIP:
  create + or -> creator
  create + ion -> creation
  create + ive -> creative
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.agree
ROOT: agree
DERIVED:
  agree (verb)
  agreement (noun)
  agreeable (adjective)
  disagree (verb)
  disagreement (noun)
  agreeable (adjective)
RELATIONSHIP:
  agree + ment -> agreement
  dis + agree -> disagree
  agree + able -> agreeable
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.act
ROOT: act
DERIVED:
  act (verb/noun)
  action (noun)
  active (adjective)
  activity (noun)
  actor (noun)
  actress (noun)
  react (verb)
  reaction (noun)
  interact (verb)
  interaction (noun)
RELATIONSHIP:
  act + ion -> action
  act + ive -> active
  act + or -> actor
  re + act -> react
  inter + act -> interact
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.structure
ROOT: struct
MEANING: build
DERIVED:
  structure (noun/verb)
  construct (verb)
  construction (noun)
  instruct (verb)
  instruction (noun)
  destroy (verb)
  destruction (noun)
RELATIONSHIP:
  con + struct -> construct (build together)
  in + struct -> instruct (build in = teach)
  de + struct -> destruct (unbuild)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.port
ROOT: port
MEANING: carry
DERIVED:
  port (noun)
  porter (noun)
  import (verb)
  export (verb)
  transport (verb/noun)
  report (verb/noun)
  support (verb/noun)
  portable (adjective)
RELATIONSHIP:
  im + port -> import (carry in)
  ex + port -> export (carry out)
  trans + port -> transport (carry across)
  re + port -> report (carry back)
  port + able -> portable (can be carried)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.ject
ROOT: ject
MEANING: throw
DERIVED:
  eject (throw out)
  inject (throw in)
  reject (throw back)
  project (throw forward)
  subject (throw under)
  object (throw against)
RELATIONSHIP:
  e + ject -> eject (out)
  in + ject -> inject (in)
  re + ject -> reject (back)
  pro + ject -> project (forward)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.spect
ROOT: spect
MEANING: look
DERIVED:
  inspect (look in)
  respect (look back at)
  expect (look out)
  spectator (one who looks)
  spectacle (a show to look at)
RELATIONSHIP:
  in + spect -> inspect
  re + spect -> respect
  ex + spect -> expect
  spect + ator -> spectator
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.dict
ROOT: dict
MEANING: say, speak
DERIVED:
  dictate (say)
  dictionary (book of words)
  predict (say before)
  contradict (say against)
  verdict (true saying)
  dictator (one who dictates)
RELATIONSHIP:
  pre + dict -> predict (say before)
  contra + dict -> contradict (say against)
  dict + ionary -> dictionary
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.vis
ROOT: vis / vid
MEANING: see
DERIVED:
  visible (can be seen)
  vision (sight)
  visit (go to see)
  television (far seeing)
  video (I see)
  evident (seen clearly)
RELATIONSHIP:
  vis + ible -> visible
  tele + vision -> television
  re + vis + e -> revise (see again)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.form
ROOT: form
MEANING: shape
DERIVED:
  form (noun/verb)
  formal (adjective)
  informal (adjective)
  inform (verb)
  information (noun)
  reform (verb/noun)
  transform (verb)
  uniform (noun/adjective)
RELATIONSHIP:
  in + form -> inform (shape the mind)
  re + form -> reform (shape again)
  trans + form -> transform (shape across)
  uni + form -> uniform (one shape)
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.mobile
ROOT: mov / mot / mob
MEANING: move
DERIVED:
  move (verb)
  motion (noun)
  mobile (adjective)
  automobile (noun)
  promote (move forward)
  remove (move back/away)
  emotion (movement of feeling)
  motor (that which moves)
RELATIONSHIP:
  pro + mote -> promote
  re + move -> remove
  e + motion -> emotion
END

TYPE: FAMILY
ID: eng.family.grow_family
ROOT: plant
DERIVED:
  plant (noun/verb)
  replant (verb)
  transplant (verb)
  plantation (noun)
  planter (noun)
  implant (verb)
RELATIONSHIP:
  re + plant -> replant
  trans + plant -> transplant
  plant + ation -> plantation
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 4.5 COMPARISON OF ADJECTIVES AND ADVERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.one_syllable
NAME: comparative_one_syllable
CONDITION:
  adjective has one syllable
ACTION:
  add -er for comparative, -est for superlative
EXAMPLES:
  tall -> taller -> tallest
  fast -> faster -> fastest
  small -> smaller -> smallest
  old -> older -> oldest
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  more tall
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.cvc
NAME: comparative_double_consonant
CONDITION:
  adjective ends in CVC pattern (one syllable)
ACTION:
  double final consonant before -er/-est
EXAMPLES:
  big -> bigger -> biggest
  hot -> hotter -> hottest
  thin -> thinner -> thinnest
  sad -> sadder -> saddest
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  biger
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.y_ending
NAME: comparative_y_ending
CONDITION:
  adjective ends in consonant + y
ACTION:
  change y to i and add -er/-est
EXAMPLES:
  happy -> happier -> happiest
  easy -> easier -> easiest
  heavy -> heavier -> heaviest
  pretty -> prettier -> prettiest
  early -> earlier -> earliest
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  happyer
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.two_syllable
NAME: comparative_two_or_more_syllables
CONDITION:
  adjective has two or more syllables (except y-ending)
ACTION:
  use more + adjective / most + adjective
EXAMPLES:
  beautiful -> more beautiful -> most beautiful
  difficult -> more difficult -> most difficult
  interesting -> more interesting -> most interesting
  important -> more important -> most important
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  beautifuller
  more happier
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.two_syllable_er
NAME: two_syllable_adjectives_that_take_er
EXAMPLES:
  clever -> cleverer -> cleverest
  gentle -> gentler -> gentlest
  narrow -> narrower -> narrowest
  simple -> simpler -> simplest
  quiet -> quieter -> quietest
NOTES: some two-syllable adjectives accept both forms
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.irregular
NAME: irregular_comparatives
EXAMPLES:
  good -> better -> best
  well -> better -> best
  bad -> worse -> worst
  badly -> worse -> worst
  far -> farther/further -> farthest/furthest
  little -> less -> least
  much/many -> more -> most
  old -> older/elder -> oldest/eldest
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  gooder
  bestest
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.equality
NAME: equality_comparison
ACTION:
  as + adjective/adverb + as for equality
  not as + adjective/adverb + as for inequality
EXAMPLES:
  He is as tall as his brother.
  She runs as fast as me.
  This is not as easy as that.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He is so tall as his brother. (negative only: not so tall as)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.than
NAME: comparative_than
ACTION:
  comparative + than introduces the second item
EXAMPLES:
  He is taller than me. (informal) / taller than I am. (formal)
  This is better than that.
  She works harder than before.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He is taller as me.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.double
NAME: double_comparative_for_growth
ACTION:
  the + comparative + the + comparative = parallel change
EXAMPLES:
  The more you study, the more you learn.
  The faster we go, the sooner we arrive.
  The sooner, the better.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.comp.least
NAME: superlative_least
ACTION:
  the least + adjective = minimum degree
EXAMPLES:
  the least expensive
  the least difficult
  I chose the least expensive option.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 4.6 VERB INFLECTION
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.s3
NAME: third_person_singular_s
CONDITION:
  tense = present simple
  subject.person = third
  subject.number = singular
ACTION:
  add -s/-es to the base verb
EXAMPLES:
  I work -> he works
  I watch -> she watches
  I study -> he studies
  I go -> she goes
  I do -> he does
  I have -> she has
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  he work
  she watchs
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.ing
NAME: forming_the_ing_form
RULES:
  default: add -ing: play -> playing
  silent e: drop e, add -ing: make -> making
  CVC one syllable: double consonant: run -> running
  ie: change ie to y: lie -> lying, die -> dying
  vowel + e: keep e: see -> seeing, be -> being
  vowel + y: keep y: play -> playing, enjoy -> enjoying
EXAMPLES:
  walk -> walking
  write -> writing
  sit -> sitting
  tie -> tying
  study -> studying
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  writeing
  lieing
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.ed
NAME: forming_the_past_simple_regular
RULES:
  default: add -ed: walk -> walked
  silent e: add -d: like -> liked
  consonant + y: change y to i, add -ed: study -> studied
  CVC one syllable: double consonant: stop -> stopped
  vowel + y: add -ed: play -> played
EXAMPLES:
  work -> worked
  live -> lived
  carry -> carried
  plan -> planned
  enjoy -> enjoyed
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  stoped
  studyed
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.morph.irregular_verbs
NAME: common_irregular_verbs
FORMAT: base = past simple = past participle
VALUES:
  be = was/were = been
  become = became = become
  begin = began = begun
  break = broke = broken
  bring = brought = brought
  build = built = built
  buy = bought = bought
  catch = caught = caught
  choose = chose = chosen
  come = came = come
  cost = cost = cost
  cut = cut = cut

  drive = drove = driven
