  fall = fell = fallen
  feel = felt = felt
  fight = fought = fought
  find = found = found
  fly = flew = flown
  forget = forgot = forgotten
  get = got = got/gotten
  give = gave = given
  go = went = gone
  grow = grew = grown
  have = had = had
  hear = heard = heard
  hide = hid = hidden
  hit = hit = hit
  hold = held = held
  hurt = hurt = hurt
  keep = kept = kept
  know = knew = known
  lead = led = led
  learn = learned/learnt = learned/learnt
  leave = left = left
  lend = lent = lent
  let = let = let
  lie = lay = lain
  lose = lost = lost
  make = made = made
  mean = meant = meant
  meet = met = met
  pay = paid = paid
  put = put = put
  read = read = read
  ride = rode = ridden
  ring = rang = rung
  rise = rose = risen
  run = ran = run
  say = said = said
  see = saw = seen
  sell = sold = sold
  send = sent = sent
  set = set = set
  shake = shook = shaken
  shine = shone = shone
  show = showed = shown
  shut = shut = shut
  sing = sang = sung
  sink = sank = sunk
  sit = sat = sat
  sleep = slept = slept
  speak = spoke = spoken
  spend = spent = spent
  stand = stood = stood
  steal = stole = stolen
  swim = swam = swum
  take = took = taken
  teach = taught = taught

  think = thought = thought
  throw = threw = thrown
  understand = understood = understood
  wake = woke = woken
  wear = wore = worn
  win = won = won
  write = wrote = written
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.irregular_groups
NAME: irregular_verb_patterns
GROUP:
  A-B-C = base, past, participle all different: eat ate eaten
  A-B-B = past = participle: buy bought bought
  A-B-A = past differs, participle = base: come came come
  A-A-A = all the same: cut cut cut
EXAMPLES:
  group_abc: begin began begun; drink drank drunk; ring rang rung
  group_abb: bring brought brought; catch caught caught; say said said
  group_aba: become became become; run ran run; come came come
  group_aaa: cost cost cost; hit hit hit; put put put; shut shut shut
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.infinitive
NAME: infinitive_forms
CONTENT:
  full infinitive = to + base: to go, to eat
  bare infinitive = base only (after modals, do, let, make, see, hear, help)
EXAMPLES:
  I want to go. (full)
  I must go. (bare, after modal)
  Let me go. (bare, after let)
  I saw him run. (bare, after see)
  Make him wait. (bare, after make)
  Help me lift it. (bare, after help)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I must to go.
  I want go.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.gerund
NAME: gerund_usage
DEFINITION: the -ing form of a verb used as a noun
USAGE:
  as subject: Swimming is fun.
  after prepositions: interested in reading
  after certain verbs: enjoy, finish, avoid, mind, suggest, keep, practice
  after certain expressions: it's no use, be used to, look forward to
EXAMPLES:
  I enjoy swimming.
  She avoids driving at night.
  He is good at cooking.
  I look forward to hearing from you.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I enjoy to swim.
  She avoids to drive.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.gerund_vs_infinitive
NAME: verbs_followed_by_gerund_or_infinitive
GERUND_ONLY:
  enjoy, finish, avoid, mind, suggest, keep, practice, consider, admit, deny
  miss, imagine, delay, postpone, risk, involve, recommend
INFINITIVE_ONLY:
  want, decide, hope, plan, promise, agree, refuse, learn, offer, manage
  seem, appear, pretend, afford, arrange, fail
BOTH_SAME:
  like, love, hate, start, begin, continue, prefer, intend
BOTH_DIFFERENT:
  remember (gerund = past memory; infinitive = future duty)
  stop (gerund = cease; infinitive = pause)
  try (gerund = experiment; infinitive = make effort)
  regret (gerund = past regret; infinitive = bad news)
  forget (gerund = past; infinitive = future)
EXAMPLES:
  I enjoy reading.
  I want to read.
  I like reading / I like to read. (same)
  I stopped smoking. (no longer smoke)
  I stopped to smoke. (paused in order to smoke)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.morph.verb.participle_adj
NAME: participle_adjectives
DEFINITION:
  present participle (-ing) describes the cause/thing
  past participle (-ed) describes the person feeling
EXAMPLES:
  The film is interesting. (the film causes interest)
  I am interested in the film. (I feel interest)
  The job is tiring. / I am tired.
  The news is surprising. / I was surprised.
  The book is boring. / He is bored.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I am interesting in the film.
  The film is interested.
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 5. PRONOUNS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.005
NAME: pronouns
CONTENT: personal, possessive, reflexive, demonstrative, relative, interrogative, indefinite pronouns
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.1 PERSONAL PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pronoun.personal
NAME: personal_pronouns
COLUMNS: person, number, gender, subjective, objective, possessive_adjective, possessive_pronoun, reflexive
VALUES:
  1st singular: I, me, my, mine, myself
  2nd singular: you, you, your, yours, yourself
  3rd singular masculine: he, him, his, his, himself
  3rd singular feminine: she, her, her, hers, herself
  3rd singular neuter: it, it, its, its, itself
  1st plural: we, us, our, ours, ourselves
  2nd plural: you, you, your, yours, yourselves
  3rd plural: they, them, their, theirs, themselves
NOTES:
  you = both singular and plural
  it has no possessive 'it's' (it's = it is)
  they can be singular for unknown gender: someone left their phone
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.person_agreement
NAME: pronoun_person_agreement
CONDITION:
  pronoun refers to a person (3rd singular)
ACTION:
  use he for male, she for female, it for things/animals
EXAMPLES:
  John -> he
  Mary -> she
  the cat -> it
  the sun -> it
  a ship -> it (or she in old style)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.royal_we
NAME: special_uses_of_we_and_you
NOTES:
  we = inclusive (speaker + listener) or exclusive (speaker + others)
  you = general people: You must be careful. (one = formal)
  one = formal general: One must be careful.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.2 POSSESSIVE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.possessive
NAME: possessive_adjectives_vs_pronouns
DEFINITION:
  possessive adjectives = my, your, his, her, its, our, their (need a noun)
  possessive pronouns = mine, yours, his, hers, ours, theirs (stand alone)
EXAMPLES:
  This is my book. (adjective + noun)
  This book is mine. (pronoun, no noun)
  Is this your pen? No, it's hers.
  Ours is bigger than theirs.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  This is mine book.
  This book is my.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.possession_agreement
NAME: possession_agreement
EXAMPLES:
  I have a car. It is my car. It is mine.
  He has a dog. It is his dog. It is his.
  She has a house. It is her house. It is hers.
  We have bikes. They are our bikes. They are ours.
  They have a garden. It is their garden. It is theirs.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.3 REFLEXIVE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.reflexive
NAME: reflexive_pronouns
DEFINITION: myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself, ourselves, yourselves, themselves
USAGE:
  object = same person as subject: I cut myself.
  emphasis: I did it myself.
  with prepositions: She lives by herself.
  after verbs: enjoy oneself, hurt oneself, help oneself
  by + reflexive = alone: He lives by himself.
EXAMPLES:
  She looked at herself in the mirror.
  He taught himself to play guitar.
  We enjoyed ourselves.
  Help yourself to cake.
  They built the house themselves. (emphasis)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I cut me. (when same person, use myself)
  He taught hisself. (hisself is nonstandard)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.reflexive_reciprocal
NAME: reflexive_vs_each_other
EXAMPLES:
  They looked at themselves. (each looked at his own)
  They looked at each other. (each looked at the other)
  They introduced themselves. (each introduced his own name)
  They introduced each other. (each introduced the other person)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.4 DEMONSTRATIVE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.demonstrative
NAME: demonstrative_pronouns
TABLE:
  this = singular, near speaker
  that = singular, far from speaker
  these = plural, near speaker
  those = plural, far from speaker
EXAMPLES:
  This is my house. (near)
  That is your car. (far)
  These are my books.
  Those are your pens.
  This/that one: I want this one, not that one.
NOTES:
  this/that + noun = demonstrative adjective: this book
  this/that alone = pronoun: This is good.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.demonstrative_reference
NAME: demonstrative_reference
USAGE:
  this = introducing new information: Listen to this.
  that = referring back: That was a great movie.
  this/these = things near in time: this week, these days
  that/those = things further in time: that year, those days
EXAMPLES:
  This is the plan. (introducing)
  That is what I meant. (referring back)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.5 RELATIVE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.relative
NAME: relative_pronouns
MEMBERS:
  who = people, subject: The man who called left.
  whom = people, object: The man whom I saw left. (formal)
  whose = possession: The girl whose bag was lost cried.
  which = things, animals: The book which I read was long.
  that = people or things: The book that I read was long.
NOTES:
  whom is formal; who is commonly used in object position
  that is often preferred over which in defining relative clauses
  commas mark non-defining clauses; which/whom, not that, for non-defining
EXAMPLES:
  The teacher who taught me retired.
  The car that I bought is red.
  The house, which was built in 1900, is huge. (non-defining)
  The boy whose dog ran away is sad.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The house that was built in 1900, is huge. (comma + that wrong)
  The man which called left. (which for person)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.relative_omission
NAME: omitting_the_relative_pronoun
CONDITION:
  relative pronoun is the OBJECT of the defining clause
ACTION:
  may be omitted
EXAMPLES:
  The book (that/which) I read was good.
  The man (who/whom) I met was kind.
  The song (that) she sang was beautiful.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The man I met him was kind. (no pronoun AND no omission)
  The man who I met him was kind. (double object)
WHY: never repeat the object after omitting the relative pronoun
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.relative_whose
NAME: whose_for_possession
EXAMPLES:
  the man whose car was stolen
  the students whose exams finished early
  the dog whose leg was hurt
NOTES: whose is used for people, animals, and sometimes things
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.relative_what
NAME: what_as_relative
DEFINITION: what = the thing that (no antecedent)
EXAMPLES:
  I saw what you did. (the thing that you did)
  What he said was true.
  Tell me what you know.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I saw the thing what you did.
  All what you said. (use: all that you said)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.6 INTERROGATIVE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.interrogative
NAME: interrogative_pronouns
MEMBERS:
  who = person, subject: Who is at the door?
  whom = person, object: Whom did you see? (formal)
  whose = possession: Whose is this?
  which = choice: Which do you prefer?
  what = thing: What is that?
RULES:
  who/whom/whose/which/what are pronouns when they stand alone
  same words are determiners before a noun: Which book? Whose pen?
EXAMPLES:
  Who called you?
  What happened?
  Whose coat is this?
  Which of these do you want?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Who did you see? (informal; whom is formal)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 5.7 INDEFINITE PRONOUNS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.pronoun.indefinite
NAME: indefinite_pronouns
COLUMNS: type, person, thing, place
VALUES:
  positive: someone, something, somewhere
  anyone-series: anyone, anything, anywhere
  no-series: no one, nobody, nothing, nowhere
  every-series: everyone, everybody, everything, everywhere
NOTES:
  everyone/everybody = plural meaning, singular verb
  indefinite pronouns take singular verbs: Everyone is here.
  someone/somebody = positive; anyone/anybody = questions and negatives
EXAMPLES:
  Someone is knocking. (affirmative)
  Is anyone home? (question)
  There is nobody here. (negative)
  Everyone knows that. (singular verb)
  Nothing is impossible.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Everyone are here.
  Somebody came? (statement as question is informal)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.indefinite_some_any
NAME: some_any_body_thing_where
CONDITION:
  sentence is affirmative
ACTION:
  use some-words: someone, something, somewhere
CONDITION2:
  sentence is negative or question
ACTION2:
  use any-words: anyone, anything, anywhere
EXAMPLES:
  I saw someone there.
  I didn't see anyone there.
  Do you know anything about it?
  Is there anything in the box?
  There is nothing in the box. (nothing = not anything)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I didn't see someone there.
  Do you know something about it? (positive-leaning question)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.pronoun.indefinite_nobody
NAME: nobody_vs_anybody_negative
EXAMPLES:
  Nobody came. = No one came. (one negation)
  I didn't see anybody. (negation + any)
  I saw nobody. (one negation, emphatic)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I didn't see nobody. (double negative, nonstandard)
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 6. VERBS: BE, HAVE, DO, AND MODALS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.006
NAME: verbs_be_have_do_modals
CONTENT: full conjugation and roles of be, have, do, and the modal verbs
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 6.1 THE VERB BE
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: VERB
ID: eng.verb.be.conjugation
VERB: be
FULL_CONJUGATION:
  present: I am, you are, he/she/it is, we are, you are, they are
  past: I was, you were, he/she/it was, we were, you were, they were
  present_participle: being
  past_participle: been
  infinitive: (to) be
SUBJECT_FORMS_PRESENT:
  I -> am
  you -> are
  we -> are
  they -> are
  he -> is
  she -> is
  it -> is
  singular_noun -> is (the cat is)
  plural_noun -> are (the cats are)
  uncountable_noun -> is (the water is)
SUBJECT_FORMS_PAST:
  I -> was
  he/she/it -> was
  you/we/they -> were
  singular_noun -> was
  plural_noun -> were
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.contractions
NAME: be_contractions
EXAMPLES:
  I am = I'm
  you are = you're
  he is = he's
  she is = she's
  it is = it's
  we are = we're
  they are = they're
  is not = isn't
  are not = aren't
  was not = wasn't
  were not = weren't
  I am not = I'm not (no 'amn't')
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.roles
NAME: the_three_roles_of_be
ROLE_1:
  name = linking verb (copula)
  structure = subject + be + complement
  examples = He is a teacher. She is happy. They are here.
ROLE_2:
  name = helping verb for progressive
  structure = subject + be + present participle (-ing)
  examples = I am working. She was sleeping. They will be waiting.
ROLE_3:
  name = helping verb for passive
  structure = subject + be + past participle
  examples = The cake was eaten. Letters are sent daily.
DETECTION:
  if followed by adjective or noun -> linking
  if followed by -ing verb -> progressive helper
  if followed by -ed participle -> passive helper
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.question
NAME: be_questions
ACTION:
  move be before the subject
EXAMPLES:
  He is happy. -> Is he happy?
  They are working. -> Are they working?
  She was late. -> Was she late?
  I am ready. -> Am I ready?
  You were there. -> Were you there?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.negation
NAME: be_negation
ACTION:
  add not after be
EXAMPLES:
  I am not tired.
  He is not here. / He isn't here.
  They are not ready. / They aren't ready.
  It was not cold. / It wasn't cold.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.there
NAME: there_is_are
STRUCTURE:
  there is + singular noun
  there are + plural noun
  there was/were in past
  there will be in future
EXAMPLES:
  There is a book on the table.
  There are three books on the table.
  There was a party last night.
  There were many people.
  There will be a test tomorrow.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  There is three books.
  There are a book.
WHY: be agrees with the noun that follows 'there'
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.be.subjunctive
NAME: be_subjunctive
DEFINITION: use bare 'be' after verbs like suggest, recommend, demand
EXAMPLES:
  I suggest that he be careful. (formal)
  It is important that she be there.
  The teacher demanded that we be quiet.
NOTES: also were for unreal conditionals: If I were you...
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  If I was you, I would go. (informal; were is formal standard)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 6.2 THE VERB HAVE
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: VERB
ID: eng.verb.have.conjugation
VERB: have
FULL_CONJUGATION:
  present: I have, you have, he/she/it has, we have, you have, they have
  past: had (all persons)
  present_participle: having
  past_participle: had
  infinitive: (to) have
SUBJECT_FORMS:
  I -> have
  you -> have
  we -> have
  they -> have
  he -> has
  she -> has
  it -> has
  singular_noun -> has
  plural_noun -> have
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.have.roles
NAME: the_roles_of_have
ROLE_1:
  name = main verb (possession)
  examples = I have a car. She has two brothers.
ROLE_2:
  name = helping verb (perfect tenses)
  structure = have/has/had + past participle
  examples = I have finished. She has gone. They had left.
ROLE_3:
  name = have to (obligation)
  structure = have to + base verb
  examples = I have to work. She has to study.
  negatives = don't have to (no obligation)
ROLE_4:
  name = causative have
  structure = have + object + past participle
  examples = I had my hair cut. Have the car fixed.
ROLE_5:
  name = experience have
  structure = have + noun
  examples = have breakfast, have a shower, have a good time
DETECTION:
  followed by past participle -> perfect helper
  followed by to + verb -> obligation
  followed by object + past participle -> causative
  otherwise -> main verb
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.have.question
NAME: have_questions
ACTION:
  have/has/had moves before subject in questions
  do/does/did + have for simple possession questions
EXAMPLES:
  Have you finished? (perfect)
  Has she eaten? (perfect)
  Do you have a car? (possession)
  Does he have any money?
  Did you have a good time?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Have you a car? (old formal style; Do you have... is standard)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.have.negation
NAME: have_negation
EXAMPLES:
  I haven't finished. (perfect, British also 'I've not finished')
  She hasn't arrived yet.
  I don't have a car. (possession)
  He doesn't have any money.
  I didn't have time.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 6.3 THE VERB DO
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: VERB
ID: eng.verb.do.conjugation
VERB: do
FULL_CONJUGATION:
  present: I do, you do, he/she/it does, we do, you do, they do
  past: did (all persons)
  present_participle: doing
  past_participle: done
  infinitive: (to) do
SUBJECT_FORMS:
  he/she/it -> does
  all others -> do
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.do.roles
NAME: the_roles_of_do
ROLE_1:
  name = main verb (perform)
  examples = I do my homework. She does the dishes.
ROLE_2:
  name = helping verb (questions, simple tenses)
  structure = do/does/did + subject + base verb
  examples = Do you like tea? Does she work here? Did they come?
ROLE_3:
  name = helping verb (negation, simple tenses)
  structure = subject + do/does/did + not + base verb
  examples = I don't know. She doesn't smoke. They didn't leave.
ROLE_4:
  name = emphasis
  examples = I DO like it! She DID come.
ROLE_5:
  name = avoiding repetition (pro-verb)
  examples = I like tea and so does she. He works harder than I do.
DETECTION:
  do + subject = question helper
  do + not = negation helper
  do + noun = main verb
  before a main verb = emphasis or pro-verb
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.do.question
NAME: do_questions
ACTION:
  use do/does (present) or did (past) + subject + base verb
EXAMPLES:
  You like tea. -> Do you like tea?
  She works here. -> Does she work here?
  They went home. -> Did they go home?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Do she works here? (main verb stays base)
  Did they went home?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.do.negation
NAME: do_negation
EXAMPLES:
  I do not know. / I don't know.
  She does not smoke. / She doesn't smoke.
  They did not come. / They didn't come.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I not know.
  She doesn't smokes.
  I didn't went.
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 6.4 MODAL VERBS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.can
MODAL: can
MEANINGS:
  ability = I can swim.
  permission = Can I go now? (informal)
  possibility = It can get cold here. (general possibility)
  request = Can you help me?
NEGATIVE: cannot / can't
QUESTION: Can + subject + base verb?
PAST_FORM: could (ability in past)
NOTES: can has no infinitive or -ing; use 'be able to' for those
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.could
MODAL: could
MEANINGS:
  past_ability = I could swim when I was five.
  polite_request = Could you open the door?
  possibility = It could rain later. (less certain than can)
  suggestion = We could go to the park.
  unreal = I could have won. (didn't win)
NEGATIVE: couldn't
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.may
MODAL: may
MEANINGS:
  permission = May I come in? (formal)
  possibility = It may snow tomorrow. (50%)
  wish = May you be happy. (formal)
NEGATIVE: may not (refusal of permission)
NOTES: may is more formal than can for permission
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.might
MODAL: might
MEANINGS:
  possibility = She might be at home. (weaker than may)
  polite_suggestion = You might try this.
  past_possibility = He might have missed the bus.
NEGATIVE: might not
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.must
MODAL: must
MEANINGS:
  obligation = You must wear a seatbelt. (strong)
  prohibition = You must not smoke here.
  deduction = He must be tired. (logical conclusion)
  past_deduction = He must have left. (certain about the past)
NEGATIVE: must not / mustn't (prohibition)
NO_OBLIGATION: don't have to (no obligation)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  You don't must go.
  You mustn't do it. vs You don't have to do it. (prohibition vs no obligation)
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.shall
MODAL: shall
MEANINGS:
  future_with_I_we = We shall overcome. (formal)
  suggestion = Shall we go? (let's go?)
  offer = Shall I open the window?
QUESTION: shall + I/we (suggestion)
NOTES: will is more common in modern English
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.should
MODAL: should
MEANINGS:
  advice = You should rest. (it's a good idea)
  expectation = The train should arrive soon. (probable)
  obligation_weak = You should apologize.
  past_criticism = You should have called. (you didn't)
NEGATIVE: shouldn't
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.will
MODAL: will
MEANINGS:
  future = It will rain tomorrow.
  prediction = She will win.
  willingness = I will help you.
  promise = I will be there.
  request = Will you pass the salt?
  habit = He will always complain. (annoying habit)
NEGATIVE: will not / won't
END

TYPE: MODAL
ID: eng.verb.modal.would
MODAL: would
MEANINGS:
  polite_request = Would you help me?
  polite_offer = Would you like some tea?
  hypothetical = I would go if I had time.
  past_habit = When young, he would swim daily. (= used to)
  reported_speech = She said she would come.
NEGATIVE: wouldn't
NOTES: would like = want (polite): I would like tea.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.verb.modal.general_rules
NAME: modal_general_rules
RULES:
  modal + base verb (no to, no -s, no -ing)
  one modal per verb phrase
  questions: modal before subject
  negation: modal + not
  past: modal + have + past participle
  no modals with 'to' except 'have to' (semi-modal)
EXAMPLES:
  She can swim.
  Can she swim?
  She cannot swim.
  She could have swum.
  I have to go. (semi-modal have to)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She cans swim.
  Can she swims?
  I must to go.
  She might can come.
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.verb.modal.semi_modals
NAME: semi_modals
VALUES:
  have to = obligation: I have to go. (has to, had to)
  have got to = obligation (informal): I've got to go.
  ought to = advice: You ought to see a doctor.
  used to = past habit: I used to play.
  need to = necessity: You need to rest.
  dare = challenge: I dare say. / How dare you!
  had better = strong advice: You had better hurry.
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 7. TENSES
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.007
NAME: tenses
CONTENT: the twelve major tense structures with meaning, structure, and transformation rules
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.tenses.overview
NAME: tense_system_overview
STRUCTURE:
  present = simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous
  past = simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous
  future = simple, continuous, perfect, perfect continuous
NOTES:
  tense = time (past/present/future) + aspect (simple/continuous/perfect)
  simple = fact or habit
  continuous = action in progress
  perfect = completed action with present relevance
  perfect continuous = duration up to a point
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 7.1 PRESENT TENSES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.simple
NAME: present_simple
TIME: present
ASPECT: simple
MEANING: habits, facts, permanent situations, schedules, opinions
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + base verb (+ -s for he/she/it)
  negative = subject + do/does + not + base verb
  question = do/does + subject + base verb
AUXILIARY: do/does (questions and negatives only)
VERB_FORM: base form (third person singular adds -s/-es)
SUBJECT_RULE:
  I/you/we/they -> base verb: I play
  he/she/it -> base verb + s/es: he plays
NEGATIVE_RULE:
  I/you/we/they + don't + base verb: I don't play
  he/she/it + doesn't + base verb: She doesn't play
QUESTION_RULE:
  Do + I/you/we/they + base verb: Do you play?
  Does + he/she/it + base verb: Does she play?
EXAMPLES:
  I work every day.
  She works in a bank.
  Water boils at 100 degrees. (fact)
  We don't watch TV.
  Does he speak French?
  The train leaves at six. (schedule)
  I like coffee. (opinion/state)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She work in a bank.
  I doesn't like tea.
  Does he works here?
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: place subject before verb and remove do/does
  negative -> positive: remove don't/doesn't, restore verb form
  statement -> question: add do/does before subject, base the verb
NOTES:
  used with frequency adverbs: always, usually, often, sometimes, never
  stative verbs usually use this tense, not continuous
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.continuous
NAME: present_continuous
TIME: present
ASPECT: continuous
MEANING: action happening now; temporary situation; future arrangement
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + am/is/are + verb-ing
  negative = subject + am/is/are + not + verb-ing
  question = am/is/are + subject + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: am/is/are
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE:
  I -> am
  he/she/it -> is
  you/we/they -> are
NEGATIVE_RULE: be + not + -ing: She is not sleeping.
QUESTION_RULE: be before subject: Is she sleeping?
EXAMPLES:
  I am reading now.
  She is working at the moment.
  They are playing football.
  We are not waiting.
  Are you listening?
  I am seeing the doctor tomorrow. (arrangement)
  He is getting taller. (change)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I am read now.
  She is work at the moment.
  Do you are listening?
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move be after subject
  negative -> positive: remove not
  simple -> continuous: be + -ing (only for action verbs)
NOTES:
  used with: now, at the moment, today, this week
  stative verbs do not usually take continuous: I know (not I am knowing)
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.perfect
NAME: present_perfect
TIME: past-to-present
ASPECT: perfect
MEANING: past action with present relevance; experience; result in the present; recent events
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + have/has + past participle
  negative = subject + have/has + not + past participle
  question = have/has + subject + past participle
AUXILIARY: have/has
VERB_FORM: past participle
SUBJECT_RULE:
  I/you/we/they -> have
  he/she/it -> has
NEGATIVE_RULE: have/has + not + participle: I haven't seen it.
QUESTION_RULE: have/has before subject: Have you seen it?
EXAMPLES:
  I have finished my homework.
  She has visited Paris twice. (experience)
  We have lived here for ten years. (duration up to now)
  They have just left. (recent)
  The door is open; someone has broken it. (result)
  Have you ever eaten sushi?
  He hasn't arrived yet.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I have finish my homework.
  She has visited Paris last year. (past time -> past simple)
  Have you saw it? (need past participle seen)
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  negative -> positive: remove not
  question -> statement: move have/has after subject
  present perfect -> past simple: if a finished past time is mentioned
NOTES:
  used with: just, already, yet, ever, never, since, for, so far, recently
  not used with finished time words: yesterday, last week, in 2020
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.perfect_continuous
NAME: present_perfect_continuous
TIME: past-to-present
ASPECT: perfect continuous
MEANING: action that started in the past and continues to now, focusing on duration
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + have/has + been + verb-ing
  negative = subject + have/has + not + been + verb-ing
  question = have/has + subject + been + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: have/has + been
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE:
  I/you/we/they -> have been
  he/she/it -> has been
NEGATIVE_RULE: have/has + not + been + -ing
QUESTION_RULE: have/has before subject
EXAMPLES:
  I have been studying for two hours.
  She has been working here since 2020.
  It has been raining all day.
  How long have you been learning English?
  They haven't been sleeping well.
  He has been running, so he is tired. (result visible now)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I have been study for two hours.
  She has been worked here since 2020.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move have/has after subject
  focus on completed result -> present perfect simple: I have read the book. (finished)
  focus on duration -> perfect continuous: I have been reading for two hours.
NOTES:
  used with: for + duration, since + point, how long, all day/week
  stative verbs use perfect simple instead: I have known him for years (not have been knowing)
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 7.2 PAST TENSES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.past.simple
NAME: past_simple
TIME: past
ASPECT: simple
MEANING: finished action at a definite past time; past habit; past fact
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + past verb form
  negative = subject + did + not + base verb
  question = did + subject + base verb
AUXILIARY: did (questions and negatives only)
VERB_FORM: past simple (regular -ed or irregular)
SUBJECT_RULE: same form for all persons: I/you/he/she/it/we/they walked
NEGATIVE_RULE: didn't + base verb: I didn't go.
QUESTION_RULE: did + subject + base verb: Did you go?
EXAMPLES:
  I walked to school yesterday.
  She saw a movie last night.
  They lived in London in 2020.
  We didn't watch TV last night.
  Did he call you?
  When I was young, I played football every day. (past habit)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I didn't went.
  Did she went?
  She has seen a movie yesterday. (yesterday -> past simple)
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: remove did, restore verb past form
  negative -> positive: remove didn't
  present simple -> past simple: shift verb to past form
NOTES:
  used with: yesterday, last night/week/year, ago, in 2010, when I was...
  past habits: used to + base or would + base
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.past.continuous
NAME: past_continuous
TIME: past
ASPECT: continuous
MEANING: action in progress at a past moment; interrupted action; background action; parallel actions
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + was/were + verb-ing
  negative = subject + was/were + not + verb-ing
  question = was/were + subject + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: was/were
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE:
  I/he/she/it -> was
  you/we/they -> were
NEGATIVE_RULE: was/were + not + -ing
QUESTION_RULE: was/were before subject
EXAMPLES:
  I was reading at 9 pm.
  She was cooking when the phone rang. (interrupted)
  While they were playing, it started to rain.
  We were watching TV and talking. (parallel)
  The sun was shining. (background)
  Was he sleeping when you arrived?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I was read at 9 pm.
  When the phone rang, she cooked. (wrong for interrupted action)
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move was/were after subject
  interrupted action -> past simple for the interruption, past continuous for the longer action
NOTES:
  used with: at 8 o'clock, while, when, all morning
  while + continuous; when + simple
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.past.perfect
NAME: past_perfect
TIME: past-before-past
ASPECT: perfect
MEANING: action completed before another past action or time
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + had + past participle
  negative = subject + had + not + past participle
  question = had + subject + past participle
AUXILIARY: had
VERB_FORM: past participle
SUBJECT_RULE: had for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: hadn't + participle
QUESTION_RULE: had before subject
EXAMPLES:
  When I arrived, the train had left.
  She had finished before he came.
  They had never seen the sea until that day.
  Had you eaten before the party?
  After the film had ended, we left.
  I realized I had forgotten my keys.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  When I arrived, the train has left.
  She had finish before he came.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move had after subject
  sequence: the earlier action takes past perfect, the later takes past simple
  if the order is clear without it, past simple is enough: After the film ended, we left.
NOTES:
  used with: before, after, by the time, already, never, when
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.past.perfect_continuous
NAME: past_perfect_continuous
TIME: past-before-past
ASPECT: perfect continuous
MEANING: duration of an action up to a past point
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + had + been + verb-ing
  negative = subject + had + not + been + verb-ing
  question = had + subject + been + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: had + been
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE: had been for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: hadn't been + -ing
QUESTION_RULE: had before subject
EXAMPLES:
  She had been waiting for an hour when he arrived.
  They had been living there for five years before moving.
  I was tired because I had been running.
  Had you been sleeping long?
  He hadn't been feeling well, so he left early.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She had been wait for an hour when he arrived.
  I was tired because I had been run.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move had after subject
  focus on finished result before past -> past perfect simple
  focus on duration -> past perfect continuous
NOTES:
  used with: for, since, how long, all day, before
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 7.3 FUTURE TENSES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.future.simple
NAME: future_simple
TIME: future
ASPECT: simple
MEANING: prediction, decision at the moment, promise, willingness, offers
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + will + base verb
  negative = subject + will + not + base verb
  question = will + subject + base verb
AUXILIARY: will (shall for I/we in formal style)
VERB_FORM: base form
SUBJECT_RULE: will for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: won't + base verb
QUESTION_RULE: will before subject
EXAMPLES:
  It will rain tomorrow. (prediction)
  I will help you. (promise)
  The phone is ringing; I'll answer it. (decision at the moment)
  She won't agree.
  Will you come to the party?
  We shall overcome. (formal)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I will to help you.
  Will she comes?
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move will after subject
  negative -> positive: remove won't/not
  planned future -> be going to or present continuous for arrangements
NOTES:
  will for instant decisions; be going to for prior plans
  will for prediction with think, expect, probably
  future time words: tomorrow, next week, soon, later, in 2030
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.future.continuous
NAME: future_continuous
TIME: future
ASPECT: continuous
MEANING: action in progress at a future time; planned future activity; polite question about plans
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + will + be + verb-ing
  negative = subject + will + not + be + verb-ing
  question = will + subject + be + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: will + be
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE: will be for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: won't be + -ing
QUESTION_RULE: will before subject
EXAMPLES:
  This time tomorrow, I will be flying to Paris.
  She will be working at 10 am.
  Don't call at 8; we will be having dinner.
  Will you be using the car tonight?
  They won't be coming to the party.
  I will be waiting for you.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  This time tomorrow, I will fly to Paris. (statement about ongoing future action)
  She will be work at 10 am.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move will after subject
  future simple -> future continuous: add be + -ing for in-progress meaning
NOTES:
  used with: this time tomorrow, at 8 o'clock, while, soon
  polite: Will you be...? is softer than Will you...?
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.future.perfect
NAME: future_perfect
TIME: future
ASPECT: perfect
MEANING: action completed before a future time
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + will + have + past participle
  negative = subject + will + not + have + past participle
  question = will + subject + have + past participle
AUXILIARY: will + have
VERB_FORM: past participle
SUBJECT_RULE: will have for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: won't have + participle
QUESTION_RULE: will before subject
EXAMPLES:
  By 2028, I will have graduated.
  She will have finished the report by Friday.
  They will have arrived before midnight.
  Will you have completed the work by then?
  He won't have left yet.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  By 2028, I will graduated.
  She will have finish the report by Friday.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move will after subject
  future perfect -> future simple: if no completion deadline is important
NOTES:
  used with: by, by the time, before, in (in two years)
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.future.perfect_continuous
NAME: future_perfect_continuous
TIME: future
ASPECT: perfect continuous
MEANING: duration of an action up to a future point
STRUCTURE:
  positive = subject + will + have + been + verb-ing
  negative = subject + will + not + have + been + verb-ing
  question = will + subject + have + been + verb-ing
AUXILIARY: will + have + been
VERB_FORM: present participle (-ing)
SUBJECT_RULE: will have been for all persons
NEGATIVE_RULE: won't have been + -ing
QUESTION_RULE: will before subject
EXAMPLES:
  By June, I will have been working here for ten years.
  She will have been studying for five hours by midnight.
  They will have been traveling for a month by then.
  Will you have been waiting long?
  He won't have been sleeping at that time.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  By June, I will have work here for ten years.
  She will have been study for five hours by midnight.
TRANSFORMATION_RULES:
  question -> statement: move will after subject
  duration -> future perfect: drop continuous if duration is not important
NOTES:
  used with: by + time, for + duration
  rare but used for durations emphasized at a future point
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 7.4 TENSE SELECTION RULES
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.time_words
NAME: time_word_to_tense_map
VALUES:
  every day / always / usually / often / sometimes / never -> present simple
  now / at the moment / right now / today -> present continuous
  just / already / yet / ever / never / recently / so far -> present perfect
  since 2020 / for two years (up to now) -> present perfect
  yesterday / last week / last year / ago / in 2010 -> past simple
  at 8 o'clock yesterday / while / when (background) -> past continuous
  by the time / before (earlier action) -> past perfect
  tomorrow / next week / soon / later / in 2028 -> future simple
  this time tomorrow / at 8 tomorrow -> future continuous
  by Friday / by then / by 2030 -> future perfect
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.simple_vs_continuous
NAME: simple_vs_continuous
CONDITION:
  verb is stative (know, believe, own, like, want)
ACTION:
  use simple tenses
EXAMPLES:
  I know the answer. (not: I am knowing)
  She owns a car. (not: She is owning)
  This tastes good. (not: This is tasting)
NOTES:
  some verbs have state and action meanings
  have = possess (state) vs have = take/eat (action: I am having breakfast)
  think = believe (state) vs think = consider (action: I am thinking about it)
  see = perceive (state) vs see = meet (action: I am seeing the doctor)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.arrangement
NAME: future_arrangement_forms
VALUES:
  will = instant decision: I'll call him now.
  be going to = prior plan: I am going to visit my sister.
  present continuous = fixed arrangement: I am meeting her at 6.
  present simple = timetable: The train leaves at 7.
EXAMPLES:
  Look at those clouds; it is going to rain. (evidence)
  I will help you. (willingness)
  We are having dinner with them on Friday. (arranged)
  The museum opens at 9. (schedule)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.sequence
NAME: tense_sequence_in_time_clauses
CONDITION:
  future time clause introduced by when, after, before, until, as soon as
ACTION:
  use present tense in the clause, future in the main clause
EXAMPLES:
  I will call you when I arrive. (not: when I will arrive)
  She will eat after she finishes. (not: after she will finish)
  Wait until the rain stops.
  As soon as he comes, we will leave.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I will call you when I will arrive.
  We will start after he will come.
WHY: time clauses use present tense for future meaning
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.reported
NAME: tense_shift_in_reported_speech
CONDITION:
  reporting verb is past (said, told, asked)
ACTION:
  shift the tense one step back
EXAMPLES:
  present simple -> past simple: He said he liked tea.
  present continuous -> past continuous: She said she was working.
  present perfect -> past perfect: He said he had finished.
  past simple -> past perfect: She said she had seen it.
  will -> would: He said he would come.
  can -> could: She said she could help.
  must -> had to: He said he had to go.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He said he will come. (if still true, will is possible but shift is safer)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.no_shift
NAME: when_tense_shift_is_not_needed
CONDITION:
  reported fact is still true
ACTION:
  tense shift is optional
EXAMPLES:
  She said the earth is round. (still true, no shift needed)
  He said he likes tea. (still true)
  They said they are leaving now. (reporting soon after)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.past_habit
NAME: past_habits
STRUCTURES:
  used to + base verb = past habit/state now ended
  would + base verb = past habit (action verbs only)
  past simple + frequency = past habit
EXAMPLES:
  I used to play tennis. (no longer)
  He would visit us every summer. (habit)
  She always walked to work. (habit)
  Did you use to live here?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I use to play tennis.
  He used to played.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.tense.selection.zero_conditionals
NAME: conditionals_overview
ZERO:
  structure = if + present, present
  meaning = general truth
  example = If you heat ice, it melts.
FIRST:
  structure = if + present, will + base
  meaning = real future possibility
  example = If it rains, we will stay.
SECOND:
  structure = if + past, would + base
  meaning = unreal present/future
  example = If I had money, I would buy a car.
THIRD:
  structure = if + past perfect, would have + participle
  meaning = unreal past
  example = If I had known, I would have come.
MIXED:
  structure = if + past perfect, would + base (past condition, present result)
  example = If I had studied, I would be rich now.
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.continuous_future_note
NAME: present_continuous_for_future
STRUCTURE: subject + am/is/are + verb-ing + future time word
MEANING: fixed personal arrangement
EXAMPLES:
  I am flying to London tomorrow.
  She is having dinner with us tonight.
  We are moving next month.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I am fly to London tomorrow.
END

TYPE: TENSE
ID: eng.tense.present.simple_future_note
NAME: present_simple_for_timetables
STRUCTURE: subject + base verb + future time word
MEANING: scheduled event
EXAMPLES:
  The train leaves at 6.
  The film starts at 8.
  The shop opens at 9.
  The term ends in June.
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 8. SENTENCE STRUCTURE
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.008
NAME: sentence_structure
CONTENT: subject, verb, object, indirect object, complement, modifier, determiner, adverbial, predicate
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.sentence.components
NAME: sentence_components
COMPONENTS:
  subject = who or what the sentence is about; usually before the verb
  verb = the action or state
  object = receiver of the action
  indirect_object = who benefits from or receives the direct object
  complement = completes the meaning of subject or object
  modifier = adds description (adjective, adverb)
  determiner = introduces the noun (a, the, this, my)
  adverbial = time, place, manner, frequency information
  predicate = everything except the subject (verb + objects + complements + adverbials)
IDENTIFICATION:
  find the verb first, then ask 'who/what + verb?' for the subject
  ask 'verb + what/whom?' for the direct object
  ask 'verb + to/for whom?' for the indirect object
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.sentence.basic_structures
NAME: basic_sentence_patterns
VALUES:
  S + V = Birds fly.
  S + V + O = She reads books.
  S + V + C = He is a doctor. (subject complement)
  S + V + O + O = He gave Mary a gift. (indirect + direct)
  S + V + O + C = They made him captain. (object complement)
  S + V + A = She lives here. (adverbial required by verb)
  S + V + O + A = I put the book on the table.
EXAMPLES:
  S+V: The sun rises.
  S+V+O: The boy eats an apple.
  S+V+C: She became a teacher.
  S+V+O+O: She told me a story.
  S+V+O+C: We elected him president.
  S+V+A: He went home.
  S+V+O+A: She placed the cup on the desk.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.subject_identification
NAME: how_to_find_the_subject
ACTION:
  1. find the main verb
  2. ask who/what performs the verb
  3. the answer is the subject
EXAMPLES:
  The boy eats an apple. -> subject = The boy, verb = eats, object = an apple
  The old man walked slowly. -> subject = The old man
  My two sisters live in Paris. -> subject = My two sisters
  Running is healthy. -> subject = Running (gerund)
  To err is human. -> subject = To err (infinitive)
  What he said was true. -> subject = What he said (noun clause)
NOTES:
  subject can be a noun, pronoun, gerund, infinitive, or noun clause
  the subject usually comes before the verb
  in questions the subject comes after the auxiliary
EXAMPLES_QUESTIONS:
  Is she happy? -> subject = she
  Where do they live? -> subject = they
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.subject_verb_agreement
NAME: subject_verb_agreement
RULE:
  singular subject -> singular verb
  plural subject -> plural verb
EXAMPLES:
  The cat is asleep. (singular)
  The cats are asleep. (plural)
  He works here. (singular)
  They work here. (plural)
  Everyone is here. (indefinite singular)
  Neither of them knows. (singular)
  The team is winning. (collective singular, US)
  The team are winning. (collective plural, UK)
  Mathematics is difficult. (subject in -s is singular)
  The news is good.
  A number of people are waiting. (plural: a number of)
  The number of people is growing. (singular: the number)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The cat are asleep.
  They works here.
  Everyone are here.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.predicate
NAME: predicate_definition
DEFINITION: the predicate is everything in the sentence except the subject
STRUCTURE:
  predicate = verb phrase + (objects) + (complements) + (adverbials)
EXAMPLES:
  The boy [eats an apple]. -> predicate = eats an apple
  She [has been reading a book in the garden]. -> predicate = has been reading a book in the garden
  We [are tired]. -> predicate = are tired
NOTES:
  every sentence has a predicate with at least a verb
  the predicate tells what the subject does or is
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 8.1 OBJECTS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.direct_object
NAME: direct_object
DEFINITION: the receiver of the action; answers 'verb + what/whom?'
EXAMPLES:
  She kicked the ball. -> object = the ball
  I read a book. -> object = a book
  He loves her. -> object = her
  They built a house. -> object = a house
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She kicked. (incomplete: kick is transitive)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.indirect_object
NAME: indirect_object
DEFINITION: the person who receives the direct object; answers 'verb + to/for whom?'
PATTERNS:
  S + V + indirect + direct: He gave Mary a gift.
  S + V + direct + to/for + indirect: He gave a gift to Mary.
EXAMPLES:
  She told me a story. (me = indirect, a story = direct)
  He bought his mother flowers. (his mother = indirect)
  Please pass me the salt.
  I wrote a letter to my friend.
  She made a cake for the children.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He gave a gift Mary. (needs to/for or order change)
  She told a story me.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.object_of_preposition
NAME: object_of_preposition
DEFINITION: the noun/pronoun after a preposition inside a prepositional phrase
EXAMPLES:
  She sat on the chair. (chair = object of on)
  He talked to me. (me = object of to)
  The cat jumped onto the table.
  I looked at the picture.
  They arrived after the show.
NOTES:
  the object of a preposition takes object pronouns: for me, with her
  prepositional phrases modify nouns or verbs
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.object_complement
NAME: object_complement
DEFINITION: completes the meaning of the object; follows verbs like make, call, name, elect, find, consider
PATTERN: S + V + O + OC (noun or adjective)
EXAMPLES:
  They made him captain. (captain = object complement, noun)
  We named the dog Rex.
  The news made her happy. (happy = object complement, adjective)
  I found the book interesting.
  They elected her president.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  They made him captain person. (redundant)
  I found the book to be interesting. (informal reduction: 'to be' dropped)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.subject_complement
NAME: subject_complement
DEFINITION: follows a linking verb and describes or renames the subject
LINKING_VERBS: be, seem, appear, become, feel, look, sound, taste, smell, stay, remain, turn
PATTERN: S + linking verb + C
EXAMPLES:
  He is a doctor. (noun complement)
  She seems happy. (adjective complement)
  The soup tastes good. (adjective; not 'tastes well')
  He became a pilot.
  She stayed calm.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The soup tastes well. (taste = linking verb, needs adjective)
  He seems a doctor. (seem usually takes adjective; 'seems to be a doctor' for noun)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.linking_vs_action
NAME: linking_verb_vs_action_verb
DETECTION:
  if the verb can be replaced by 'is/are' with the same meaning -> linking
  if it can take an object -> action
EXAMPLES:
  He looks tired. -> linking (he is tired)
  He looks at the picture. -> action (object: the picture)
  She feels happy. -> linking
  She feels the fabric. -> action
  The flowers smell sweet. -> linking
  I smell the flowers. -> action
END

# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------
# 8.2 MODIFIERS, DETERMINERS, ADVERBIALS
# -----------------------------------------------------------------------------

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.modifier
NAME: modifiers
DEFINITION: words or phrases that add description to another element
TYPES:
  adjective = modifies noun: a red car
  adverb = modifies verb: runs quickly
  adverb = modifies adjective: very tall
  adverb = modifies adverb: extremely quickly
  prepositional phrase = modifies noun or verb: the book on the table / ran in the park
  relative clause = modifies noun: the man who called
  participle = modifies noun: a broken window
EXAMPLES:
  The small dog barked loudly. (small = adjective, loudly = adverb)
  She is very clever. (very = adverb modifying clever)
  The man in the blue shirt smiled. (in the blue shirt = phrase modifier)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.adverbial
NAME: adverbials
DEFINITION: adverbs, phrases, or clauses that give time, place, manner, frequency, reason, or condition
TYPES:
  time = yesterday, at 5, after lunch, when I arrived
  place = here, at home, in the garden
  manner = quickly, with care, like a professional
  frequency = always, usually, never
  reason = because of the rain, because I was tired
  condition = if it rains
  degree = very, quite, too
POSITION:
  manner: after the verb or object: She sang beautifully.
  place: after the verb: They live here.
  time: at the end or beginning: Yesterday I met him. / I met him yesterday.
  frequency: before main verb, after be: He always wins. She is often late.
EXAMPLES:
  She spoke quietly in the library yesterday.
  Because it was cold, we stayed inside.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.sentence.determiner_position
NAME: determiner_position
ACTION:
  determiner comes first in the noun phrase, before adjectives
EXAMPLES:
  the three big red apples
  my old brown coat
  this little white cat
  every single day
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  big three red apples
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 9. SUBJECT DETECTION (DIFFICULT CASES)
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.009
NAME: subject_detection
CONTENT: rules and difficult cases for finding the subject
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.gerund
NAME: gerund_as_subject
STRUCTURE: verb-ing + verb (singular verb)
EXAMPLES:
  Running is healthy. -> subject = Running
  Swimming makes you fit.
  Reading helps you learn.
  Smoking causes illness.
  Playing chess requires patience.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Running are healthy.
WHY: a gerund acts as a singular noun
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.infinitive
NAME: infinitive_as_subject
STRUCTURE: to + verb + verb
EXAMPLES:
  To err is human.
  To travel broadens the mind.
  To learn a language takes time.
  To succeed, you must work hard. (fronted purpose, different function)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.there
NAME: there_as_dummy_subject
STRUCTURE: there + be + noun
EXAMPLES:
  There are three books on the table. -> real subject = three books
  There is a problem. -> real subject = a problem
  There was a loud noise.
  There were many people at the party.
  There has been an accident.
NOTES:
  there is a dummy (empty) subject
  the verb agrees with the noun after be
  there + be = existence; there + adverb = location: The book is there.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  There is three books.
  There are a problem.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.it
NAME: it_as_dummy_subject
USES:
  weather: It is raining. It is cold.
  time: It is 5 o'clock. It is Monday.
  distance: It is far to the station.
  extraposed subject: It is important to study. (= To study is important.)
  extraposed subject: It is true that he left. (= That he left is true.)
  empty subject with seem: It seems that he is right.
EXAMPLES:
  It is raining. (weather; no real subject)
  It takes two hours to get there.
  It was nice to meet you.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Raining is. (English needs a subject)
WHY: English sentences usually need a subject; it fills the role
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.noun_clause
NAME: noun_clause_as_subject
STRUCTURE: wh-clause or that-clause + verb
EXAMPLES:
  What he said was true. -> subject = What he said
  That she left was a surprise.
  Whether we go depends on the weather.
  How it happened remains a mystery.
  Where they live is unknown.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  What he said were true. (clause subject takes singular verb)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.imperative
NAME: imperative_subject
EXAMPLES:
  Go now! -> subject = (you), understood
  Please sit down. -> subject = (you)
  Let's go. -> subject = we (implied)
NOTES: imperatives omit the subject; it is always 'you'
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.prepositional_phrase_error
NAME: prepositional_phrase_is_not_subject
EXAMPLES:
  The box of apples is on the table. -> subject = The box (not apples)
  The group of students was happy. -> subject = The group
  A bunch of flowers sits on the desk. -> subject = A bunch
  The picture on the wall shows a ship. -> subject = The picture
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The box of apples are on the table.
  The group of students were happy.
WHY: the object of a preposition never acts as the subject
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.compound
NAME: compound_subjects
EXAMPLES:
  Tom and Mary are here. (plural: and)
  Tom or Mary is here. (singular: or)
  Neither Tom nor Mary is here. (singular: neither...nor)
  Either the boys or Mary is coming. (verb agrees with nearest)
  Both Tom and Mary are coming.
  Tom, together with Mary, is coming. (together with does not make plural)
  Tom as well as Mary is coming.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Tom and Mary is here.
  Tom together with Mary are coming.
WHY: and = plural subject; or/nor and with-phrases keep singular agreement
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.interrogative
NAME: subject_in_questions
DETECTION:
  in questions, find the auxiliary, then the subject after it
  wh-word may itself be the subject
EXAMPLES:
  Is she at home? -> subject = she
  Who called you? -> subject = Who (wh-word is the subject)
  What happened? -> subject = What
  Where do they live? -> subject = they
  Which book did you buy? -> subject = you
  Who is knocking? -> subject = Who
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Who did call you? (only for emphasis: Who DID call you?)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.subject.quantifier
NAME: quantifier_phrases_as_subject
EXAMPLES:
  Some of the water is cold. (uncountable -> singular)
  Some of the students are here. (countable plural -> plural)
  Most of the cake was eaten.
  Most of the cakes were eaten.
  All of the money is gone.
  All of the people are gone.
  Half of the class is here.
  None of them knows / none of them know (both accepted)
  A lot of time was wasted.
  A lot of people were waiting.
WHY: verb agrees with the noun after 'of'
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 10. OBJECT DETECTION
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.010
NAME: object_detection
CONTENT: direct object, indirect object, object of preposition, object complement
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.direct_detection
NAME: how_to_detect_direct_object
ACTION:
  find the transitive verb
  ask 'verb + what?' or 'verb + whom?'
  the answer is the direct object
EXAMPLES:
  She kicked the ball. -> kicked what? the ball
  I met John. -> met whom? John
  They ate lunch. -> ate what? lunch
  He wrote a letter. -> wrote what? a letter
  We watched the movie. -> watched what? the movie
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She slept the ball. (sleep is intransitive, no object)
  I went school. (go takes 'to': went to school)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.passivization
NAME: passivization_as_object_test
ACTION:
  if the sentence can become passive, the direct object is the passive subject
EXAMPLES:
  She kicked the ball. -> The ball was kicked (by her). object = the ball
  I wrote a letter. -> A letter was written. object = a letter
  He loves her. -> She is loved (by him). object = her
  They built a house. -> A house was built. object = a house
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He lives in London. -> London was lived. (impossible: no direct object)
WHY: only direct objects become passive subjects
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.double_object
NAME: double_object_verbs
VERBS: give, send, show, teach, tell, lend, bring, buy, pass, offer, promise, read, write, sell
PATTERNS:
  S + V + IO + DO: He gave Mary a book.
  S + V + DO + to + IO: He gave a book to Mary.
  S + V + DO + for + IO: She bought a gift for her mother.
EXAMPLES:
  Tell me the truth.
  Show him the photo.
  Pass me the salt.
  I lent John my car. / I lent my car to John.
  She baked us a cake. / She baked a cake for us.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I gave to him a book. (order: IO before DO without to)
  She explained me the problem. (explain takes 'to': explained the problem to me)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.verbs_with_to_only
NAME: verbs_that_take_to_with_object
VERBS: explain, describe, suggest, say, announce, report, mention, introduce
PATTERN: S + V + DO + to + person
EXAMPLES:
  She explained the problem to me. (not: explained me the problem)
  He described the house to us.
  I suggested a plan to them.
  She said goodbye to him.
  They announced the news to everyone.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She explained me the problem.
  He suggested me a plan.
WHY: these verbs do not allow the indirect object pattern
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.pronoun_forms
NAME: object_pronoun_forms
EXAMPLES:
  direct: He saw me. (not 'I')
  indirect: She gave him the book.
  object of preposition: between you and me
  after be (formal): It is I. (informal: It's me.)
  after than/as (formal): He is taller than I. (informal: than me)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He saw I.
  She gave he the book.
  between you and I
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.gerund_object
NAME: gerund_as_object
EXAMPLES:
  I enjoy swimming. (enjoy + gerund)
  She finished working.
  He admitted taking the money.
  They avoided meeting him.
  I don't mind waiting.
  She suggested going out.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I enjoy to swim.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.object_complement_detection
NAME: detecting_object_complements
DETECTION:
  ask 'verb + object + what?' for noun complements
  ask 'verb + object + how/what like?' for adjective complements
VERBS: make, call, name, elect, appoint, consider, find, keep, paint, turn
EXAMPLES:
  They elected her president. -> president = object complement
  The news made him sad. -> sad = object complement
  We painted the wall blue. -> blue = object complement
  I consider him a friend. -> a friend = object complement
  Keep the door closed. -> closed = object complement
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  They elected her president woman. (noun complement cannot double the object)
WHY: the complement refers to the object, not the subject
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.preposition_phrase_function
NAME: prepositional_phrase_as_object_related
EXAMPLES:
  She is afraid of spiders. (of spiders = object of preposition, complement of afraid)
  He is interested in music.
  I am good at math.
  They are worried about the exam.
  She apologized for being late.
NOTES:
  adjectives often pair with specific prepositions
  the whole phrase follows the adjective
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.ambiguous_cases
NAME: ambiguous_object_cases
EXAMPLES:
  She found the book boring. -> book = object, boring = object complement
  She found the book. -> book = direct object
  I made him a cake. -> him = indirect, cake = direct
  I made him a hero. -> him = object, a hero = object complement (he became a hero)
  I made him a soldier. -> ambiguous: baked a soldier for him OR turned him into a soldier
  They left the room. -> room = object
  They left. -> no object
  He ran the race. -> race = object (cognate object)
  He ran a mile. -> a mile = adverbial of distance (not an object)
WHY: context and verb meaning determine the analysis
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.object.cognate
NAME: cognate_objects
DEFINITION: an object that repeats the meaning of the verb
EXAMPLES:
  He lived a happy life.
  She dreamed a strange dream.
  They fought a brave fight.
  He smiled a sad smile.
  She sang a beautiful song.
NOTES: cognate objects are rare and mostly idiomatic
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 11. QUESTIONS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.011
NAME: questions
CONTENT: yes/no questions, wh-questions, transformation rules, question words
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.yes_no_general
NAME: yes_no_questions_with_auxiliaries
ACTION:
  move the auxiliary (be, have, will, can, must...) before the subject
EXAMPLES:
  He is happy. -> Is he happy?
  They are working. -> Are they working?
  She was late. -> Was she late?
  I have finished. -> Have I finished?
  You have seen it. -> Have you seen it?
  He will come. -> Will he come?
  She can swim. -> Can she swim?
  They must leave. -> Must they leave?
  You would like tea. -> Would you like tea?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  He is happy? (intonation question, informal only)
  Is he is happy?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.yes_no_do
NAME: yes_no_questions_with_do
CONDITION:
  sentence is present or past simple, no auxiliary
ACTION:
  add do/does/did before the subject; base the verb
EXAMPLES:
  She likes tea. -> Does she like tea?
  You play tennis. -> Do you play tennis?
  They went home. -> Did they go home?
  He works here. -> Does he work here?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Does she likes tea?
  Did they went home?
  Like she tea?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.answers_short
NAME: short_answers
STRUCTURE:
  yes/no + subject pronoun + auxiliary
EXAMPLES:
  Is he here? -> Yes, he is. / No, he isn't.
  Do you like tea? -> Yes, I do. / No, I don't.
  Did they come? -> Yes, they did. / No, they didn't.
  Can she swim? -> Yes, she can. / No, she can't.
  Have you seen it? -> Yes, I have. / No, I haven't.
  Will he come? -> Yes, he will. / No, he won't.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Yes, he is here. (full answer; short answers use pronoun + auxiliary)
  Yes, I like. (missing auxiliary)
  No, I don't like. (incomplete: need object or 'it')
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.wh
NAME: wh_questions
STRUCTURE: wh-word + auxiliary + subject + main verb
WH_WORDS:
  who = person (subject): Who called you?
  whom = person (object, formal): Whom did you see?
  whose = possession: Whose book is this?
  what = thing: What is that? What do you want?
  which = choice: Which do you prefer?
  when = time: When did she arrive?
  where = place: Where do they live?
  why = reason: Why are you crying?
  how = manner: How does it work?
  how many = countable number: How many books do you have?
  how much = uncountable amount: How much money do you have?
  how long = duration: How long have you been here?
  how far = distance: How far is the station?
  how often = frequency: How often do you exercise?
  how old = age: How old are you?
EXAMPLES:
  Where do you live?
  When did she leave?
  Why is he angry?
  What are you doing?
  Which color do you like?
  Whose car is that?
  How many sisters do you have?
  How much does it cost?
  How long will you stay?
  How far is it to London?
  How often do you travel?
  How was your day?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Where you live?
  When she left?
  Why he is angry?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.wh_subject
NAME: wh_word_as_subject
CONDITION:
  wh-word asks about the subject
ACTION:
  no auxiliary needed; wh-word + verb
EXAMPLES:
  Who called you? (who = subject)
  What happened? (what = subject)
  Which train goes to London? (which + noun = subject)
  Who is knocking? (who = subject)
  What made you cry?
  Who broke the window?
  How many people came?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Who did call you? (needs emphasis context)
  What did happen? (informal/emphatic)
WHY: when the wh-word is the subject, the word order is statement order
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.wh_object
NAME: wh_word_as_object
CONDITION:
  wh-word asks about the object
ACTION:
  wh-word + auxiliary + subject + verb
EXAMPLES:
  Who did you meet? (object)
  What are you eating?
  Which book did you buy?
  Who(m) did she marry?
  What did they build?
  Whom did you see? (formal)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Who you met?
  What you are eating?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.preposition_end
NAME: questions_ending_with_preposition
EXAMPLES:
  Who are you waiting for?
  What are you looking at?
  Where does he come from?
  What is this made of?
  Who did you go with?
  What are you talking about?
  Which hotel did you stay at?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  For who are you waiting? (formal for people; whom)
  From where does he come? (formal)
WHY: prepositions usually stay at the end in informal English
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.tag
NAME: tag_questions
STRUCTURE: statement + comma + auxiliary + pronoun
POSITIVE_NEGATIVE:
  positive statement -> negative tag: You are tired, aren't you?
  negative statement -> positive tag: You aren't tired, are you?
AUXILIARY_MATCH: the tag repeats the auxiliary of the statement
EXAMPLES:
  She is happy, isn't she?
  They are working, aren't they?
  He likes tea, doesn't he?
  You don't smoke, do you?
  She can swim, can't she?
  It won't rain, will it?
  You have seen it, haven't you?
  Let's go, shall we?
  Open the door, will you?
  I am right, aren't I?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She is happy, isn't it? (pronoun must match subject)
  He likes tea, doesn't he? -> He likes tea, don't he?
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.embedded
NAME: embedded_questions
STRUCTURE: question inside a statement keeps statement word order
EXAMPLES:
  I don't know where he lives. (not: where does he live)
  Can you tell me what time it is?
  I wonder why she left.
  Do you know if he is here? (yes/no -> if/whether)
  She asked me whether I liked it.
  Tell me how it works.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't know where does he live.
  Do you know what time is it?
  She asked me if did I like it.
WHY: embedded questions do not invert subject and auxiliary
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.choice
NAME: choice_questions
STRUCTURE: question + or + alternative
EXAMPLES:
  Do you want tea or coffee?
  Is he coming or not?
  Are you tired or hungry?
  Did she call or write?
  Do you like it or not?
NOTES: intonation rises then falls; answers name the choice
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.negative
NAME: negative_questions
STRUCTURE: auxiliary + n't + subject OR auxiliary + subject + not
MEANING:
  surprise: Didn't you see it? (I expected you did)
  confirmation: Isn't it beautiful? (I think it is)
  polite invitation: Won't you come in?
EXAMPLES:
  Aren't you hungry?
  Haven't you finished yet?
  Can't you help me?
  Didn't they call?
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  You didn't call? (informal echo question)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.echo
NAME: echo_questions
USAGE: repeat part of the statement to ask for confirmation
EXAMPLES:
  I saw the Queen. -> You saw the Queen?!
  We won the lottery. -> You won the lottery?
  I'm leaving tomorrow. -> Tomorrow?
NOTES: echo questions keep statement order, rise in pitch
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.question.wh_exclamations
NAME: wh_exclamations
STRUCTURE: what a/an + adjective + noun / how + adjective
EXAMPLES:
  What a beautiful day!
  What an amazing story!
  What lovely flowers!
  How beautiful!
  How quickly time flies!
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  What beautiful day! (need article for singular countable)
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 12. NEGATION
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.012
NAME: negation
CONTENT: not, never, no, nobody, nothing, neither, nor, and double negative rules
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.not
NAME: negation_with_not
RULES:
  with be: subject + be + not: I am not tired.
  with auxiliaries: subject + auxiliary + not: She has not come.
  with modals: subject + modal + not: You must not go.
  with simple tenses: subject + do/does/did + not + base: I don't like it.
EXAMPLES:
  I am not hungry.
  They are not here.
  She has not finished.
  We will not leave.
  He does not smoke.
  They did not come.
  I cannot swim.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I not like it.
  She doesn't likes it.
  He not came.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.contractions
NAME: negative_contractions
EXAMPLES:
  do not = don't
  does not = doesn't
  did not = didn't
  is not = isn't
  are not = aren't
  was not = wasn't
  were not = weren't
  have not = haven't
  has not = hasn't
  had not = hadn't
  will not = won't
  would not = wouldn't
  cannot = can't
  could not = couldn't
  must not = mustn't
  shall not = shan't
  should not = shouldn't
  am not = I'm not (no 'amn't')
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.never
NAME: negation_with_never
STRUCTURE: never replaces not (never = not ever)
EXAMPLES:
  I never smoke. (= I don't ever smoke)
  She never comes late.
  He has never been abroad.
  They never listen.
  I will never forget you.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't never smoke. (double negative)
  I never don't smoke.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.no
NAME: negation_with_no
USES:
  no + noun = not any: I have no money. (= I don't have any money)
  no = short answer: No, I don't.
  no + comparative: no better than
  no longer = not any more: He no longer lives here.
EXAMPLES:
  There is no time.
  I have no idea.
  No problem.
  No news is good news.
  She is no better today.
  I can stay no longer.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't have no money. (double negative)
  No any money.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.nobody
NAME: nobody_no_one
EXAMPLES:
  Nobody came. (= not anybody)
  No one knows the answer.
  Nobody told me.
  There is nobody at home.
  Nobody is perfect.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Nobody didn't come.
  I saw nobody nobody. (redundant)
WHY: nobody already contains the negation
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.nothing
NAME: nothing
EXAMPLES:
  Nothing happened. (= not anything)
  There is nothing in the box.
  I know nothing about it.
  Nothing is impossible.
  She said nothing.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't know nothing about it. (nonstandard double negative)
  Nothing didn't happen.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.neither
NAME: neither
USES:
  neither + noun (singular): Neither answer is correct.
  neither of + plural: Neither of them knows. (also 'know' informal)
  neither = short answer: I don't like tea. Neither do I.
  neither...nor: Neither John nor Mary was there.
EXAMPLES:
  Neither plan worked.
  Neither of the boys was late.
  I can't swim. Neither can I.
  Neither the dog nor the cat is asleep.
  He neither called nor wrote.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Neither of them are late. (informal)
  I don't like tea. Neither I do. (inversion needed)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.nor
NAME: nor
USES:
  nor after neither: neither...nor
  nor after a negative clause: He didn't come, nor did he call.
EXAMPLES:
  Neither tea nor coffee was available.
  She isn't here, nor is he.
  I haven't seen it, nor have I heard about it.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Neither tea or coffee. (use nor)
  I didn't see it, nor I heard it. (inversion: nor did I hear)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.double_negative
NAME: double_negative_rule
RULE:
  standard English: two negatives in one clause = nonstandard (except litotes)
  use one negative + any-words
EXAMPLES:
  correct: I don't have any money.
  correct: I have no money.
  incorrect: I don't have no money.
  correct: Nobody said anything.
  incorrect: Nobody said nothing.
  correct: She never does anything wrong.
  incorrect: She never does nothing wrong.
  correct: There isn't anyone here.
  incorrect: There isn't nobody here.
WHY: two negatives cancel or mark nonstandard speech
NOTES:
  litotes = deliberate double negative for effect: not uncommon, not bad
  with negative adverbs: hardly, barely, scarcely, rarely take any, not no:
  correct: I could hardly hear anything. incorrect: I couldn't hardly hear anything.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.hardly
NAME: hardly_barely_scarcely
USAGE: negative adverbs; take any-words; follow with any in negatives
EXAMPLES:
  I hardly know him. (= almost don't know)
  She barely slept.
  There was scarcely any food left.
  I could hardly believe my eyes.
  He hardly ever calls.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't hardly know him. (double negative)
  There was hardly no food left.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.both_either
NAME: both_and_neither_in_negation
EXAMPLES:
  I don't like either of them. (= I like neither of them)
  Neither of them is good.
  Both are not good. (rarely used; means not both)
  Either answer is acceptable. (positive: both are acceptable)
  Neither answer is acceptable. (negative)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I don't like neither of them. (double negative)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.negation.prefix
NAME: negative_prefixes
VALUES:
  un- = not: unhappy, unfair, unable
  in- = not: incorrect, invisible (il-, im-, ir- variants)
  dis- = not: dishonest, disagree
  non- = not: nonstop, nonviolent
  mis- = wrongly: misunderstand
  de- = reverse: defrost
  -less = without: careless, hopeless
EXAMPLES:
  happy -> unhappy
  correct -> incorrect
  honest -> dishonest
  stop -> nonstop
  understand -> misunderstand
  care -> careless
  fortunate -> unfortunate
  possible -> impossible
  legal -> illegal
  regular -> irregular
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 13. VOICE
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.013
NAME: voice
CONTENT: active voice, passive voice, transformation rules
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.definition
NAME: active_vs_passive
DEFINITION:
  active: subject performs the action: The boy kicked the ball.
  passive: subject receives the action: The ball was kicked by the boy.
STRUCTURE_PASSIVE: subject + be + past participle + (by + agent)
USES:
  when the agent is unknown: My car was stolen.
  when the agent is obvious: The thief was arrested.
  when the agent is unimportant: The letters are delivered daily.
  when the receiver is more important: The president was elected.
  formal/scientific style: It is believed that...
NOTES:
