  only transitive verbs form the passive
  the agent is omitted when unimportant
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.transformation
NAME: active_to_passive_transformation
STEPS:
  1. object of active -> subject of passive
  2. verb becomes be + past participle (be in the same tense)
  3. subject of active -> by + agent (optional)
EXAMPLES:
  The boy kicked the ball. -> The ball was kicked by the boy.
  She wrote a letter. -> A letter was written (by her).
  They are building a house. -> A house is being built.
  He has finished the work. -> The work has been finished.
  People speak English here. -> English is spoken here.
  They will open the shop. -> The shop will be opened.
  We must clean the room. -> The room must be cleaned.
  Someone stole my bike. -> My bike was stolen. (agent omitted)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The ball was kick by the boy.
  A letter was wrote by her. (needs past participle: written)
  English is speak here.
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.voice.tense_forms
NAME: passive_forms_in_all_tenses
VALUES:
  present simple = is/are + V3: The cake is eaten.
  present continuous = is/are being + V3: The cake is being eaten.
  present perfect = has/have been + V3: The cake has been eaten.
  past simple = was/were + V3: The cake was eaten.
  past continuous = was/were being + V3: The cake was being eaten.
  past perfect = had been + V3: The cake had been eaten.
  future simple = will be + V3: The cake will be eaten.
  future perfect = will have been + V3: The cake will have been eaten.
  modal = modal + be + V3: The cake must be eaten.
  modal perfect = modal + have been + V3: The cake should have been eaten.
  infinitive = to be + V3: I want it to be eaten.
  gerund = being + V3: I hate being watched.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.personal_impersonal
NAME: personal_and_impersonal_passive
STRUCTURES:
  impersonal: It is said that he is rich. (it + be + participle + that)
  personal: He is said to be rich. (subject + be + participle + to-infinitive)
VERBS: say, believe, think, know, report, expect, consider, allege
EXAMPLES:
  It is believed that the plan will work.
  The plan is believed to work.
  It is said that she speaks five languages.
  She is said to speak five languages.
  It was reported that the ship sank.
  The ship was reported to have sunk.
  It is expected that prices will rise.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.get
NAME: get_passive
STRUCTURE: get + past participle
MEANING: change or accident, often negative; informal
EXAMPLES:
  He got hurt in the game.
  My car got stolen.
  She got married last year.
  They got lost in the woods.
  I got paid yesterday.
  The window got broken.
NOTES: get passive = informal; be passive = neutral
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.double_object_passive
NAME: passive_with_two_objects
EXAMPLES:
  They gave Mary a gift.
  -> Mary was given a gift. (person as subject, common)
  -> A gift was given to Mary. (thing as subject, also correct)
  They told him a story.
  -> He was told a story.
  -> A story was told to him.
NOTES: either object can become the subject
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.by_or_with
NAME: by_vs_with_in_passive
EXAMPLES:
  The cake was baked by my mother. (agent: person)
  The cake was cut with a knife. (instrument: tool)
  The window was broken by the boy. (agent)
  The window was broken with a hammer. (instrument)
NOTES: by = agent (doer); with = instrument (tool)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.no_passive
NAME: verbs_that_do_not_form_passive
EXAMPLES:
  Intransitive verbs: sleep, arrive, die, happen, exist (no object -> no passive)
  Stative verbs in some uses: have (=possess), resemble, suit, lack
  Cost-type verbs: cost, weigh (measure), fit, last
  "He has a car" -> not: A car is had by him.
  "She resembles her mother" -> not: Her mother is resembled.
  "The dress suits you" -> not: You are suited by the dress.
  "They lack money" -> not: Money is lacked.
WHY: these verbs have no direct object that can become a passive subject
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.passive_misuse
NAME: avoiding_unnecessary_passive
GUIDELINE:
  use active voice when the agent matters: The dog bit the man.
  use passive only when the agent is unknown, obvious, or unimportant
EXAMPLES:
  The report was written by John. -> John wrote the report. (active clearer)
  My wallet was stolen. (passive needed: agent unknown)
  English is spoken in many countries. (agent unimportant)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The ball was kicked by the boy. (passive where active is clearer)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.voice.causative
NAME: causative_structures
STRUCTURES:
  have + object + past participle: I had my hair cut.
  get + object + past participle: I got the car fixed.
  make + person + base verb: She made him wait.
  have + person + base verb: I had him clean the car. (US)
  get + person + to + verb: I got him to help me.
  let + person + base verb: Let me go.
MEANING: arranging for something to be done
EXAMPLES:
  I need to have my phone repaired.
  She got her picture taken.
  He made me laugh.
  We let the children play.
  They had the house painted.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I had my hair cutted.
  She made him to wait.
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 14. CLAUSES AND PHRASES
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.014
NAME: clauses_and_phrases
CONTENT: clause types, phrase types, main and subordinate clauses
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.definition
NAME: clause_vs_phrase
DEFINITION:
  clause = group of words with a subject + verb: because she was tired
  phrase = group of words without a subject-verb pair: in the garden
EXAMPLES:
  clause: when the rain stopped
  phrase: after the rain
  clause: who lives next door
  phrase: the man next door
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.independent
NAME: independent_clause
DEFINITION: a clause that can stand alone as a sentence
EXAMPLES:
  She went home.
  The sun was shining.
  I like coffee and he likes tea. (two independent clauses)
  She smiled. (one independent clause)
  We waited. (one independent clause)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Because she was tired. (fragment: dependent clause alone)
  When the bell rang. (fragment)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.dependent
NAME: dependent_clause
DEFINITION: a clause that cannot stand alone; needs an independent clause
INTRODUCERS:
  because, when, if, although, while, since, until, unless, that, who, which, where
EXAMPLES:
  Because she was tired, she went home. (dependent + independent)
  I will call you when I arrive.
  If it rains, we will stay inside.
  The man who called is my brother.
  Although he was sick, he came to work.
  We left before the show ended.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Because she was tired. (fragment)
  When I arrive. (fragment)
WHY: a dependent clause is incomplete by itself
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.relative
NAME: relative_clauses
STRUCTURE: relative pronoun + clause; modifies a noun
PRONOUNS:
  who = people (subject): the man who called
  whom = people (object, formal): the man whom I met
  whose = possession: the girl whose dog ran away
  which = things (subject or object): the book which is on the table
  that = people or things (defining): the book that I read
  where = places: the town where I was born
  when = times: the day when we met
  why = reason: the reason why I left
DEFINING: no commas; needed to identify: The man who called was my brother.
NON_DEFINING: commas; extra information: My brother, who lives in Paris, called me.
EXAMPLES:
  The car that I bought is red.
  The woman who lives next door is a doctor.
  The house where I grew up has been sold.
  The day when we met was sunny.
  The reason why he left is unknown.
  The book, which cost fifty dollars, was boring.
  The people whose house burned down are staying with us.
  The man whom she married is a lawyer. (formal)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The car what I bought is red. (use that/which)
  The man which called. (use who for people)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.relative_omission
NAME: omitting_relative_pronoun
RULE:
  object relative pronouns can be omitted in defining clauses
EXAMPLES:
  The book that I read = The book I read
  The man who(m) I met = The man I met
  The film which we saw = The film we saw
  NOT omitted as subject: The man who called. (cannot omit who)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  The man called is my brother. (missing subject pronoun: ambiguous)
WHY: subject pronouns are required; object pronouns are optional
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.noun_clause
NAME: noun_clauses
DEFINITION: clause that acts like a noun (subject, object, or complement)
INTRODUCERS: that, what, whatever, who, whoever, whether, if, when, where, why, how
EXAMPLES:
  What he said was true. (subject)
  I know that she is here. (object)
  I wonder whether he will come.
  Tell me where you put it.
  Do you know who called?
  The problem is that we have no time. (complement)
  Whoever arrives first gets a prize.
  I don't care what you think.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I know that she is here now. -> I know she is here. (that may be omitted in object position)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.adverbial
NAME: adverbial_clauses
TYPES:
  time: when, while, before, after, until, as soon as: Call me when you arrive.
  place: where, wherever: Sit where you like.
  reason: because, since, as: I stayed home because it rained.
  purpose: so that, in order that: She studied so that she could pass.
  result: so...that, such...that: He was so tired that he slept.
  condition: if, unless, whether: If it rains, we stay in.
  concession: although, even though, while: Although it was cold, we went out.
  manner: as, as if: She acted as if she knew.
EXAMPLES:
  After the show ended, we went home.
  Because she was late, she missed the train.
  He spoke loudly so that everyone could hear.
  It was such a hot day that we stayed inside.
  Unless you hurry, you will be late.
  Even though he is old, he is strong.
  She sings as if she were an angel.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.zero_that
NAME: that_omission
EXAMPLES:
  I think (that) she is right.
  He said (that) he would come.
  I hope (that) you are well.
  We know (that) it is true.
NOTES:
  that is usually optional after common verbs (think, say, know, hope)
  that is required after nouns: The fact that he came surprised us.
  that is required at clause start: That he left was a surprise.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.phrase.noun_phrase
NAME: noun_phrases
STRUCTURE: determiner + (adjectives) + noun + (postmodifiers)
EXAMPLES:
  a cat
  the three big red apples
  my best friend
  the man in the blue shirt
  a cup of tea
  the girl who lives next door
  five interesting new books
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  big the apples (determiner must come first)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.phrase.verb_phrase
NAME: verb_phrases
STRUCTURE: (auxiliaries) + main verb
EXAMPLES:
  runs
  is running
  has been running
  will have finished
  might have been sleeping
  should be done
  is being repaired
NOTES: up to 5 auxiliaries possible in English
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.phrase.adjective_phrase
NAME: adjective_phrases
STRUCTURE: (degree adverb) + adjective + (prepositional phrase)
EXAMPLES:
  happy
  very happy
  extremely difficult
  afraid of spiders
  proud of her son
  good at math
  big enough
  too hot to drink
  more interesting than the others
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.phrase.adverb_phrase
NAME: adverb_phrases
EXAMPLES:
  quickly
  very quickly
  quite well
  more slowly than usual
  right here
  straight ahead
  just now
  so fast that we couldn't see it
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.phrase.prepositional
NAME: prepositional_phrases
STRUCTURE: preposition + noun phrase
FUNCTIONS:
  adjective (modify noun): the book on the table
  adverb (modify verb): She ran into the garden.
EXAMPLES:
  at home
  in the morning
  on the roof
  under the bed
  with a smile
  for my mother
  by train
  about the future
  after dinner
  between the two trees
  The man with the hat is my uncle. (adjective function)
  We walked along the river. (adverb function)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.clause.conditionals
NAME: conditional_sentences
TYPES:
  zero = general truth: If you heat ice, it melts. (if + present, present)
  first = real future: If it rains, I will stay home. (if + present, will)
  second = unreal present/future: If I had money, I would travel. (if + past, would)
  third = unreal past: If I had known, I would have come. (if + past perfect, would have)
  mixed = past condition, present result: If I had studied, I would be rich now.
EXAMPLES:
  If you mix red and blue, you get purple.
  If she calls, tell her I'm out.
  If I were you, I would apologize.
  If he had hurried, he would have caught the train.
  If she had taken the job, she would live in London now.
  Unless you study, you will fail.
  I would have called if I had had your number.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  If it will rain, I will stay home. (no will in if-clause)
  If I was you. (were preferred in unreal conditionals)
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 15. CONJUNCTIONS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.015
NAME: conjunctions
CONTENT: coordinating, subordinating, and correlative conjunctions
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.coordinating
NAME: coordinating_conjunctions
MEMORY: FANBOYS (for, and, nor, but, or, yet, so)
FUNCTIONS:
  and = addition: She sings and dances.
  but = contrast: He is rich but unhappy.
  or = choice: Tea or coffee?
  nor = negative addition: He didn't call, nor did he write.
  so = result: It rained, so we stayed in.
  yet = unexpected contrast: He is old yet strong.
  for = reason (formal): We stayed home, for it was raining.
EXAMPLES:
  I like tea and she likes coffee.
  She was tired but happy.
  Would you like tea or juice?
  He was sick, so he stayed home.
  She is young yet wise.
  He didn't come, nor did he apologize.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Because it rained, so we stayed in. (redundant so)
  Although he was tired, but he worked. (redundant but)
WHY: use one connector, not two
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.subordinating
NAME: subordinating_conjunctions
FUNCTION: introduce dependent clauses
COMMON: because, although, while, when, before, after, since, until, unless, if, as, than, that, whether, so that, even though, as soon as, in case
EXAMPLES:
  I called because I missed you.
  Although it was late, she stayed.
  We waited until the rain stopped.
  As soon as I heard the news, I left.
  Take an umbrella in case it rains.
  She is older than I am.
  I doubt whether he will come.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I called because I missed you, so I was happy to hear you. (mixed logic)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.correlative
NAME: correlative_conjunctions
PAIRS:
  both...and: Both Tom and Mary came.
  either...or: Either you leave or I do.
  neither...nor: Neither John nor Mary knows.
  not only...but also: She is not only smart but also kind.
  whether...or: I don't know whether to stay or leave.
  no sooner...than: No sooner had he left than it rained.
  such...that: It was such a good film that I saw it twice.
  so...that: He spoke so fast that I couldn't understand.
EXAMPLES:
  Both the cat and the dog were sleeping.
  You can either call or email.
  Not only did she win, but she also set a record.
  He is neither rich nor famous.
  No sooner did the bell ring than the students left.
  She was so tired that she fell asleep instantly.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.sentence_connectors
NAME: sentence_connectors (linking words)
FUNCTION: connect sentences in writing
COMMON:
  addition = moreover, furthermore, in addition, also
  contrast = however, nevertheless, on the other hand, in contrast
  result = therefore, consequently, as a result, thus
  sequence = first, then, next, finally
  example = for example, for instance, such as
  conclusion = in conclusion, in summary, to sum up
EXAMPLES:
  It was raining. However, we went out.
  She studied hard; therefore, she passed.
  First, mix the flour. Then, add water.
  Many animals, such as dogs, are loyal.
  The plan failed. Nevertheless, we tried our best.
  He was late. As a result, he missed the meeting.
NOTES:
  connectors join independent clauses; commas or semicolons separate them
  do not connect two complete sentences with only a comma (comma splice)
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  It was raining, however, we went out. (comma splice)
  She studied hard, therefore she passed.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.punctuation
NAME: punctuation_with_conjunctions
RULES:
  two independent clauses + and/but/or -> comma: I like tea, but she likes coffee.
  short clauses may skip comma: I came and I saw.
  three or more items -> commas: red, white, and blue
  dependent clause first -> comma: Because it rained, we stayed in.
  dependent clause last -> no comma: We stayed in because it rained.
EXAMPLES:
  She smiled, waved, and left.
  He was tired, so he went to bed early.
  When the show ended, everyone applauded.
  Everyone applauded when the show ended.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  I like tea but, she likes coffee. (misplaced comma)
  Because it rained we stayed in. (comma needed after dependent clause)
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.conj.meaning_map
NAME: connector_meaning_map
VALUES:
  addition = and, both...and, also, moreover, furthermore
  contrast = but, yet, although, however, while, whereas, nevertheless
  choice = or, either...or
  negative choice = nor, neither...nor
  reason = because, since, as, for
  result = so, therefore, consequently, thus, as a result
  purpose = so that, in order that, to
  condition = if, unless, provided that, in case
  time = when, while, before, after, until, as soon as
  concession = although, even though, despite, in spite of
  comparison = as...as, than, like
  example = for example, for instance, such as
  sequence = first, then, next, finally, eventually
  conclusion = in conclusion, to sum up, in short
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.because_vs_so
NAME: because_vs_so
RULE:
  because joins reason to result: reason + because + result clause
  so joins result to reason: reason, so + result clause
EXAMPLES:
  She was tired because she worked late.
  She worked late, so she was tired.
  He missed the bus because he woke up late.
  He woke up late, so he missed the bus.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  She was tired so she worked late. (wrong direction)
  Because she worked late, so she was tired. (double connector)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.conj.although_vs_but
NAME: although_vs_but
RULE:
  although (subordinating) + dependent clause: Although it rained, we went out.
  but (coordinating) + independent clause: It rained, but we went out.
EXAMPLES:
  Although he is rich, he is unhappy.
  He is rich, but he is unhappy.
  Although she was late, she got the job.
  She was late, but she got the job.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  Although it rained, but we went out. (double connector)
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 16. SEMANTICS
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.016
NAME: semantics
CONTENT: meaning relations: synonym, antonym, hypernym, hyponym, part_of, made_of, used_for, located_in, causes, before, after
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.semantic.relations
NAME: semantic_relations
RELATIONS:
  synonym = same meaning: big = large
  antonym = opposite meaning: big <> small
  hypernym = general category: animal is hypernym of dog
  hyponym = specific member: dog is hyponym of animal
  part_of = component: wheel is part of car
  has_part = contains: car has wheel
  made_of = material: table is made of wood
  used_for = purpose: knife is used for cutting
  located_in = place: Paris is located in France
  causes = effect: fire causes smoke
  caused_by = source: smoke is caused by fire
  before = earlier time: dawn is before noon
  after = later time: noon is after dawn
  similar_to = resemblance: cat is similar to tiger
  opposite_of = contrast: hot is opposite of cold
  larger_than = size: elephant is larger than mouse
  faster_than = speed: cheetah is faster than horse
  stronger_than = strength: iron is stronger than wood
  older_than = age: grandfather is older than father
END

TYPE: RELATION
ID: eng.semantic.synonym
NAME: synonym_pairs
PAIRS:
  big = large
  small = little
  happy = glad
  sad = unhappy
  fast = quick
  slow = sluggish
  begin = start
  end = finish
  buy = purchase
  talk = speak
  look = watch (see)
  strong = powerful
  weak = feeble
  clever = smart
  pretty = beautiful
  angry = mad
  afraid = scared
  quiet = silent
  noisy = loud
  correct = right
  wrong = incorrect
  easy = simple
  hard = difficult
  rich = wealthy
  poor = needy
  old = ancient (for things)
  new = modern
  beautiful = pretty
  ugly = unattractive
  true = correct
  false = incorrect
  story = tale
  job = work
  journey = trip
  present = gift
  holiday = vacation
  shop = store
  film = movie
  taxi = cab
NOTES: synonyms share meaning but may differ in formality or context
END

TYPE: TABLE
ID: eng.semantic.hierarchy_example
NAME: example_hierarchy_animal
VALUES:
  animal = hypernym of dog, cat, bird, fish, horse
  dog = hyponym of animal; hypernym of puppy
  puppy = hyponym of dog
  cat = hyponym of animal; hypernym of kitten
  bird = hyponym of animal; hypernym of eagle, sparrow
  fish = hyponym of animal; hypernym of salmon, shark
  salmon = hyponym of fish
  shark = hyponym of fish
  eagle = hyponym of bird
  sparrow = hyponym of bird
  horse = hyponym of animal; hypernym of foal
NOTES: hypernym = wider category; hyponym = narrower member
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 17. CONTEXT
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.017
NAME: context
CONTENT: how surrounding words determine the meaning of ambiguous words
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.bank
NAME: bank
MEANINGS:
  money_bank = a place where money is kept: I put my money in the bank.
  river_bank = the side of a river: We sat on the river bank.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with money/deposit/save -> money_bank
  with river/water/side -> river_bank
EXAMPLES:
  I went to the bank to save money. -> money_bank
  The children played on the bank of the river. -> river_bank
  She works at a bank in the city. -> money_bank
  The bank was flooded. -> river_bank (context: flooded)
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.bat
NAME: bat
MEANINGS:
  animal_bat = a flying animal: The bat flew out of the cave.
  sports_bat = a stick for hitting a ball: He swung the bat.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with cave/wing/fly -> animal_bat
  with baseball/swing/ball -> sports_bat
EXAMPLES:
  A bat sleeps during the day. -> animal_bat
  He hit the ball with the bat. -> sports_bat
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.ball
NAME: ball
MEANINGS:
  sports_ball = round object for games: The ball bounced.
  dance_ball = formal dance: She wore a dress to the ball.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with kick/throw/bounce -> sports_ball
  with dance/party/dress -> dance_ball
EXAMPLES:
  Pass me the ball. -> sports_ball
  Cinderella went to the ball. -> dance_ball
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.ring
NAME: ring
MEANINGS:
  jewelry_ring = circle worn on finger: She wore a gold ring.
  sound_ring = sound of a bell/phone: I heard a ring.
  circle_ring = any circular shape: The children made a ring.
  boxing_ring = fighting area: The boxers entered the ring.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with finger/gold/wedding -> jewelry_ring
  with phone/bell/sound -> sound_ring
  with circle/stand -> circle_ring
  with boxer/fight -> boxing_ring
EXAMPLES:
  The phone gave a loud ring. -> sound_ring
  He put a ring on her finger. -> jewelry_ring
  The boxers fought in the ring. -> boxing_ring
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.light
NAME: light
MEANINGS:
  light_adj = not dark: The room is light.
  light_adj2 = not heavy: The box is light.
  light_noun = brightness: The light hurts my eyes.
  light_verb = to ignite: Light the candle.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with dark/bright/room -> light_adj
  with heavy/weight/box -> light_adj2
  with sun/lamp/sunlight -> light_noun
  with fire/candle/match -> light_verb
EXAMPLES:
  It is light outside now. -> light_adj
  This bag is very light. -> light_adj2
  Please turn on the light. -> light_noun
  She lit the fire. -> light_verb
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.run
NAME: run
MEANINGS:
  run_verb = move fast on foot: She runs every morning.
  run_verb2 = operate/manage: He runs a small shop.
  run_verb3 = flow: The water ran down the street.
  run_verb4 = extend/continue: The road runs along the coast.
  run_verb5 = candidate: She is running for president.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with fast/sport/morning -> run_verb
  with business/shop/company -> run_verb2
  with water/liquid/tap -> run_verb3
  with road/river/along -> run_verb4
  with election/president/candidate -> run_verb5
EXAMPLES:
  The tap is running. -> run_verb3
  She runs a bakery. -> run_verb2
  He runs faster than me. -> run_verb
  The road runs through the forest. -> run_verb4
  She is running for mayor. -> run_verb5
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.can
NAME: can
MEANINGS:
  can_modal = ability: I can swim.
  can_modal2 = permission: Can I leave?
  can_modal3 = possibility: It can rain tomorrow.
  can_noun = metal container: a can of soup
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with ability/personal skill -> can_modal
  with permission/allow -> can_modal2
  with possibility/chance -> can_modal3
  with container/soup/opening -> can_noun
EXAMPLES:
  She can speak French. -> can_modal
  Can I open the window? -> can_modal2
  Open the can of beans. -> can_noun
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.fair
NAME: fair
MEANINGS:
  fair_adj = just: That is a fair decision.
  fair_adj2 = light-colored (hair/skin): She has fair hair.
  fair_adj3 = quite good: His English is fair.
  fair_noun = exhibition: We went to the county fair.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with just/honest/rules -> fair_adj
  with hair/skin/light -> fair_adj2
  with good/average -> fair_adj3
  with show/amusement/exhibition -> fair_noun
EXAMPLES:
  It is not fair to cheat. -> fair_adj
  The fair lasts for three days. -> fair_noun
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.book
NAME: book
MEANINGS:
  book_noun = written work: I read a book.
  book_verb = reserve: I booked a hotel room.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with read/page/write -> book_noun
  with reserve/ticket/hotel -> book_verb
EXAMPLES:
  She wrote a book about birds. -> book_noun
  I booked a table for two. -> book_verb
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.plant
NAME: plant
MEANINGS:
  plant_noun = living organism: The plant needs water.
  plant_verb = put in the ground: We plant trees in spring.
  plant_noun2 = factory: They built a new plant.
DISAMBIGUATION:
  with water/grow/leaf -> plant_noun
  with seed/soil/ground -> plant_verb
  with factory/industry/machine -> plant_noun2
EXAMPLES:
  This plant grows in the shade. -> plant_noun
  We will plant roses tomorrow. -> plant_verb
  The car plant employs thousands. -> plant_noun2
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.context.general_principle
NAME: how_context_resolves_meaning
RULE:
  1. collect the words around the ambiguous word
  2. find which meaning fits all surrounding words
  3. if more than one meaning fits, the sentence is ambiguous
EXAMPLES:
  The bank... -> needs more context
  The bank where I deposit my salary. -> money_bank (deposit/salary fit)
  The bank of the river was muddy. -> river_bank (river/muddy fit)
  She saw the bat. -> ambiguous (animal or sports)
  She saw the bat fly out of the cave. -> animal_bat (fly/cave fit)
  I went to the bank. -> ambiguous (two meanings still possible)
WHY: meaning depends on the surrounding words
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.context.polysemy_vs_homonymy
NAME: polysemy_vs_homonymy
DEFINITION:
  polysemy = one word, related meanings: head (of body / of school)
  homonymy = one word, unrelated meanings: bank (money / river)
EXAMPLES_POLYSEMY:
  head = top of body / leader of school
  foot = part of body / bottom of mountain
  eye = organ / hole in a needle
  mouth = part of body / opening of river
  hand = body part / worker (a farm hand)
  star = sky object / famous person
  table = furniture / list of data
EXAMPLES_HOMONYMY:
  bank = money place / river side
  bat = animal / sports tool
  fair = just / light-colored / exhibition
  light = brightness / not heavy / to ignite
  can = modal / container
  plant = organism / factory / to put in soil
NOTES: context resolves both types
END

TYPE: CONTEXT
ID: eng.context.polysemy_body
NAME: body_part_polysemy
EXAMPLES:
  the head of the school = leader
  the head of the bed = top end
  the foot of the mountain = bottom
  the mouth of the river = opening
  the eye of the needle = hole
  the arm of the chair = side support
  the back of the chair = rear part
  the neck of the bottle = narrow part
  the face of the clock = front surface
  the hands of the clock = pointers
  the tongue of the shoe = flap
  the leg of the table = support
  the teeth of the comb = points
  the nose of the plane = front
  the heart of the city = center
  the skin of the fruit = outer layer
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 18. AMBIGUITY
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.018
NAME: ambiguity
CONTENT: ambiguous sentences and how to resolve them
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.telescope
NAME: I saw the man with the telescope
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = I used the telescope to see the man. (with = instrument)
  reading2 = The man had the telescope. (with = possession)
RESOLUTION:
  add context: I saw the man with the telescope, but I put it down. -> reading1
  add context: I saw the man with the telescope, and he was looking at the moon. -> reading2
WHY: with can mean instrument or possession
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.police
NAME: The police chased the robber with a gun
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = the police had the gun
  reading2 = the robber had the gun
RESOLUTION:
  The police, with a gun, chased the robber. -> reading1 (commas mark non-defining)
  The police chased the robber, who had a gun. -> reading2 (relative clause)
  The police chased the robber holding a gun. -> still ambiguous
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.visiting
NAME: Visiting relatives can be boring
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = The act of visiting relatives is boring. (gerund subject)
  reading2 = Relatives who visit are boring. (participle modifying relatives)
RESOLUTION:
  Visiting relatives can be boring for the host. -> reading1
  Visiting relatives can be boring guests. -> reading2
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.flying
NAME: Flying planes can be dangerous
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = The act of flying planes is dangerous. (gerund)
  reading2 = Planes that are flying are dangerous. (participle)
RESOLUTION:
  Flying planes can be dangerous for pilots. -> reading1
  Flying planes can be dangerous for people on the ground. -> reading2
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.shooting
NAME: The shooting of the hunters
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = the hunters shot someone (subject reading)
  reading2 = someone shot the hunters (object reading)
RESOLUTION:
  The shooting of the hunters shocked the town. -> ambiguous
  The hunters' shooting shocked the town. -> reading1
  The shooting of the hunters by the poachers shocked the town. -> reading2
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.old
NAME: old_man_and_woman
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = old man + old woman
  reading2 = old man + woman (any age)
RESOLUTION:
  The old man and the old woman sat down. -> reading1 (repeat the adjective)
  The old man and woman sat down. -> ambiguous
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.everyone
NAME: Everyone loves someone
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = for every person x, there is a person y that x loves (y may differ)
  reading2 = there is one person y that everyone loves (y is the same)
RESOLUTION:
  Everyone loves someone, even if it is a different person. -> reading1
  Everyone loves someone, and that someone is you. -> reading2
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.horse
NAME: The horse raced past the barn fell
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = The horse that was raced past the barn fell. (reduced relative)
  reading2 = garden path: parse as subject + verb raced, then fall at fell
RESOLUTION:
  The horse that was raced past the barn fell. -> reading1
  The horse raced, then fell. -> different structure
NOTES: this is a garden-path sentence; context or relative clause removes it
END

TYPE: AMBIGUITY
ID: eng.ambiguity.orange
NAME: orange_juice
INTERPRETATIONS:
  reading1 = juice made from oranges (orange = noun modifier)
  reading2 = orange-colored juice (orange = adjective)
RESOLUTION:
  Orange juice made from apples? -> reading2 (unusual but possible)
  Orange juice made from oranges. -> reading1
NOTES: adjective/noun ambiguity is common in compound nouns
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.ambiguity.types
NAME: types_of_ambiguity
TYPES:
  lexical = one word has several meanings: bank, bat, can, light
  structural = one structure has several readings: Visiting relatives...
  scope = quantifiers order: Everyone loves someone.
  attachment = modifier attaches to different parts: with the telescope
  referential = pronoun has several antecedents: John told Bill that he won.
EXAMPLES:
  lexical: I saw the bat.
  structural: Flying planes can be dangerous.
  scope: Everyone loves someone.
  attachment: I saw the man with the telescope.
  referential: John told Bill that he won. (he = John or Bill)
RESOLUTION:
  lexical: choose meaning from surrounding words
  structural: rephrase with clearer structure
  scope: state the intended order explicitly
  attachment: move the modifier next to its target
  referential: name the person explicitly
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.ambiguity.referential
NAME: pronoun_ambiguity
EXAMPLES:
  John told Bill that he won. (he = John or Bill?)
  The dog chased the cat and it ran away. (it = dog or cat?)
  She gave her sister her book. (which her?)
  The teacher scolded the student because he was late. (he = teacher or student?)
RESOLUTION:
  John told Bill, "I won." -> clear
  The dog chased the cat, and the cat ran away. -> clear
  The teacher scolded the student because the student was late. -> clear
WHY: repeat the noun or quote to remove referential ambiguity
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.ambiguity.dangling
NAME: dangling_modifiers
EXAMPLES:
  Walking to school, the sun rose. (who was walking? the sun? incorrect)
  After eating the cake, the table was cleared. (who ate? unclear)
  Broken by the wind, he repaired the window. (what was broken? he?)
FIXES:
  Walking to school, I saw the sun rise.
  After eating the cake, we cleared the table.
  Because the window was broken by the wind, he repaired it.
WHY: the modifier must attach to its logical subject
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 19. CONVERSATION
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.019
NAME: conversation
CONTENT: speech acts: greeting, question, answer, request, command, agreement, disagreement, confirmation, clarification, thanks, apology, farewell
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.greeting
NAME: greetings
GREETINGS:
  Hello.
  Hi.
  Good morning.
  Good afternoon.
  Good evening.
  How are you?
  How are you doing?
  How is it going?
  What's up? (informal)
RESPONSES:
  Hello. -> Hello. / Hi.
  How are you? -> I'm fine, thank you. And you? / Very well, thanks. / Not bad.
  What's up? -> Nothing much. / Not much. / Just working.
  Good morning. -> Good morning.
TIMES:
  before 12 = Good morning.
  12-18 = Good afternoon.
  after 18 = Good evening.
  Good night = farewell at bedtime, not a greeting
END

TYPE: DIALOG
ID: eng.dialog.example_greeting
NAME: example_greeting
TURNS:
  A: Hello, how are you?
  B: I'm fine, thank you. And you?
  A: Very well, thanks.
  B: That's good to hear.
  A: See you later.
  B: See you. Bye!
NOTES: greetings start a conversation; farewells end it
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.farewell
NAME: farewells
EXPRESSIONS:
  Goodbye.
  Bye.
  See you later.
  See you tomorrow.
  Take care.
  Have a nice day.
  Good night.
RESPONSES:
  Goodbye. -> Goodbye. / Bye.
  See you later. -> See you later. / Sure, see you.
  Take care. -> Thanks, you too.
  Have a nice day. -> Thank you, you too.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.thanks
NAME: expressing_thanks
EXPRESSIONS:
  Thank you.
  Thanks.
  Thanks a lot.
  Thank you very much.
  I really appreciate it.
  That's very kind of you.
RESPONSES:
  You're welcome.
  No problem.
  Don't mention it.
  My pleasure.
  Anytime.
EXAMPLES:
  A: Here is your coffee.
  B: Thank you very much.
  A: You're welcome.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.apology
NAME: apologizing
EXPRESSIONS:
  I'm sorry.
  I'm so sorry.
  I apologize.
  Please forgive me.
  My apologies.
  I didn't mean to hurt you.
RESPONSES:
  It's okay.
  No problem.
  Don't worry about it.
  That's all right.
  I forgive you.
EXAMPLES:
  A: I'm sorry I'm late.
  B: Don't worry about it. Come in.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.request
NAME: making_requests
POLITE_FORMS:
  Can you...?
  Could you...?
  Would you...?
  Would you mind...?
  May I...? (asking permission)
  Do you mind if...?
EXAMPLES:
  Can you help me?
  Could you open the window, please?
  Would you pass the salt?
  Would you mind closing the door?
  May I come in?
  Do you mind if I sit here?
RESPONSES_YES:
  Sure.
  Of course.
  Certainly.
  No problem.
  Yes, of course.
RESPONSES_NO:
  I'm afraid I can't.
  Sorry, I can't.
  I'd like to, but I'm busy.
EXAMPLES:
  A: Could you lend me your pen?
  B: Sure, here you are.
  A: Thanks.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.command
NAME: giving_commands
FORMS:
  base verb: Sit down.
  please + base verb: Please sit down.
  don't + base verb: Don't touch it.
  let's + base verb: Let's go.
  let + object + base verb: Let him in.
EXAMPLES:
  Open your books.
  Please be quiet.
  Don't run in the hall.
  Let's start the meeting.
  Tell me the truth.
  Come here.
  Wait a moment.
  Give me the key.
  Be careful!
  Watch out!
RESPONSES:
  Yes, sir. / Okay. / All right. / Fine.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.agreement
NAME: agreeing
EXPRESSIONS:
  I agree.
  That's right.
  Exactly.
  Absolutely.
  You're right.
  I think so too.
  That's a good idea.
  Sure.
  Yes, of course.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.disagreement
NAME: disagreeing (polite)
EXPRESSIONS:
  I disagree.
  I'm not sure about that.
  I see it differently.
  I'm afraid I can't agree.
  That's not how I see it.
  You have a point, but...
EXAMPLES:
  A: This plan is perfect.
  B: I see it differently. There are some problems.
  A: Coffee is better than tea.
  B: I disagree. Tea is healthier.
NOTES: polite disagreement explains the reason
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.confirmation
NAME: asking_for_confirmation
EXPRESSIONS:
  Right?
  Isn't it?
  Aren't you?
  Do you understand?
  Is that clear?
  Is that okay?
  You know?
EXAMPLES:
  The meeting is at 3, right?
  You're coming too, aren't you?
  So the plan is to leave early, is that clear?
RESPONSES:
  Yes, that's right.
  Exactly.
  That's correct.
  Yes, I understand.
  Not quite. Let me explain again.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.clarification
NAME: asking_for_clarification
EXPRESSIONS:
  What do you mean?
  Could you say that again?
  Could you repeat that, please?
  I'm sorry, I didn't catch that.
  Do you mean...?
  Could you speak more slowly, please?
  Could you give an example?
EXAMPLES:
  A: I'll see you at the usual place.
  B: Do you mean the station?
  A: No, the library.
  A: It's kind of expensive.
  B: What do you mean by expensive?
  A: Over fifty dollars.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.questions_conversation
NAME: asking_questions_in_conversation
EXAMPLES:
  What do you do? (job)
  Where are you from?
  How old are you?
  Do you have any hobbies?
  What kind of music do you like?
  Have you been here before?
  How was your weekend?
  Did you watch the game?
  Would you like something to drink?
  Can I get you anything?
NOTES: open questions (wh-) invite long answers; closed questions invite short answers
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.offer
NAME: offering
EXPRESSIONS:
  Would you like...?
  Can I get you...?
  Do you want...?
  How about...?
  Would you care for...?
EXAMPLES:
  Would you like some tea?
  Can I get you a drink?
  Do you want a piece of cake?
  How about going for a walk?
RESPONSES_YES:
  Yes, please.
  That would be great.
  Thank you.
RESPONSES_NO:
  No, thank you.
  No, thanks. Maybe later.
  I'm fine, thanks.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.opinion
NAME: giving_opinions
EXPRESSIONS:
  In my opinion, ...
  I think that ...
  I believe ...
  From my point of view, ...
  As far as I'm concerned, ...
  It seems to me that ...
  I'd say ...
EXAMPLES:
  In my opinion, this movie is the best.
  I think we should leave early.
  As far as I'm concerned, the plan is fine.
  It seems to me that he is wrong.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.advice
NAME: giving_advice
EXPRESSIONS:
  You should ...
  You ought to ...
  Why don't you ...?
  If I were you, I would ...
  I suggest you ...
  You'd better ...
EXAMPLES:
  You should see a doctor.
  Why don't you take a break?
  If I were you, I would apologize.
  I suggest you study more.
  You'd better hurry.
  You ought to call her.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  You should to go. (no to after should)
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.complaint
NAME: complaining
EXPRESSIONS:
  I'm sorry, but ...
  Excuse me, there's a problem.
  I'd like to make a complaint.
  This isn't what I ordered.
  This is too expensive.
RESPONSES:
  I'm very sorry about that.
  Let me fix it right away.
  I apologize for the inconvenience.
  We'll take care of it.
EXAMPLES:
  A: Excuse me, my soup is cold.
  B: I'm terribly sorry. I'll bring you a new one.
  A: Thank you.
END

TYPE: RULE
ID: eng.dialog.small_talk
NAME: small_talk
TOPICS:
  weather: Nice day, isn't it?
  travel: Did you have a good trip?
  work: How is work going?
  family: How is your family?
  weekend: What did you do this weekend?
  food: Have you tried the new restaurant?
SAFE_TOPICS: weather, travel, food, hobbies, work (light)
AVOID: age (for adults), salary, politics (strangers), religion
EXAMPLES:
  A: Nice weather we're having.
  B: Yes, much warmer than yesterday.
  A: Did you have a good weekend?
  B: Yes, I went hiking. You?
END

TYPE: DIALOG
ID: eng.dialog.example_small
NAME: example_small_talk
TURNS:
  A: Hi! How's it going?
  B: Pretty good, thanks. How about you?
  A: Can't complain. Busy week?
  B: Yeah, a bit. But the weekend is coming.
  A: Any plans?
  B: Maybe a movie. You?
  A: I'm going to visit my parents.
  B: That sounds nice. Give them my regards.
  A: Will do. See you around.
  B: See you!
END

TYPE: DIALOG
ID: eng.dialog.example_restaurant
NAME: example_at_a_restaurant
TURNS:
  Waiter: Good evening. A table for two?
  Customer: Yes, please. By the window, if possible.
  Waiter: Of course. Here are your menus.
  Waiter: Are you ready to order?
  Customer: Yes. I'll have the chicken soup, please.
  Waiter: And for you, sir?
  Customer: The grilled fish, please.
  Waiter: Would you like anything to drink?
  Customer: A glass of water, please.
  Waiter: Certainly. Your food will be right out.
  Customer: Thank you.
END

TYPE: DIALOG
ID: eng.dialog.example_phone
NAME: example_phone_call
TURNS:
  A: Hello?
  B: Hi, this is Sam. Is Ann there?
  A: Sorry, she's not here right now. Can I take a message?
  B: Yes, could you tell her I called?
  A: Sure. Does she have your number?
  B: She does.
  A: Okay, I'll let her know.
  B: Thanks a lot. Bye.
  A: Bye.
END

# =============================================================================
# SECTION 20. REASONING
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.020
NAME: reasoning
CONTENT: cause and effect, condition and consequence, comparison, classification, sequence, time, location, ownership, possibility, certainty
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.if_then
NAME: if_then_reasoning
STRUCTURE: IF <condition> THEN <consequence>
EXAMPLES:
  IF it rains THEN the ground gets wet.
  IF you heat water to 100 degrees THEN it boils.
  IF you study THEN you learn.
  IF you eat too much THEN you feel sick.
  IF you touch fire THEN you get burned.
  IF a plant has no water THEN it dies.
  IF you mix red and blue THEN you get purple.
  IF you drive fast THEN you use more fuel.
  IF she is late THEN she misses the bus.
  IF he works hard THEN he earns more money.
  IF you sleep well THEN you feel better.
  IF it is dark THEN you need light.
  IF you drop a glass THEN it breaks.
  IF you push the door THEN it opens.
  IF you pull the door THEN it closes.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.cause_effect
NAME: cause_effect_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <cause> CAUSES <effect>
EXAMPLES:
  rain CAUSES wet ground
  fire CAUSES smoke
  fire CAUSES heat
  ice CAUSES cold
  sun CAUSES light
  sun CAUSES warmth
  snow CAUSES cold
  wind CAUSES leaves to move
  thunder FOLLOWS lightning
  food CAUSES energy
  water CAUSES growth
  sleep CAUSES rest
  exercise CAUSES fitness
  study CAUSES knowledge
  practice CAUSES skill
  smoking CAUSES illness
  tiredness CAUSES low concentration
  anger CAUSES conflict
  kindness CAUSES friendship
  happiness CAUSES smiling
  sadness CAUSES crying
  fear CAUSES running away
  surprise CAUSES shouting
  loud noise CAUSES fear
  argument CAUSES bad feelings
  kindness CAUSES good feelings
  illness CAUSES weakness
  war CAUSES destruction
  flood CAUSES damage
  drought CAUSES hunger
  poison CAUSES death
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.and
NAME: and_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <fact1> AND <fact2> TOGETHER IMPLY <conclusion>
EXAMPLES:
  It is raining AND I have no umbrella IMPLY I will get wet.
  He is sick AND he has a fever IMPLY he should see a doctor.
  The light is red AND cars are crossing IMPLY I must wait.
  The door is locked AND I don't have the key IMPLY I cannot enter.
  She studied hard AND she passed the test IMPLY studying helps.
  The ground is wet AND it rained last night IMPLY rain wet the ground.
  He is over 18 AND he has a driver's license IMPLY he can drive.
  The shop is closed AND it is Sunday IMPLY the shop closes on Sundays.
  I have money AND the shop is open IMPLY I can buy food.
  The sky is dark AND the wind is strong IMPLY a storm may come.
  He left early AND the train was on time IMPLY he caught the train.
  She ate a lot AND she was hungry IMPLY the food was not enough.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.transitive
NAME: transitive_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <a> IS <b> AND <b> IS <c> IMPLY <a> IS <c>
EXAMPLES:
  A dog is an animal AND an animal is alive IMPLY a dog is alive.
  A car is a vehicle AND a vehicle is a machine IMPLY a car is a machine.
  An apple is a fruit AND a fruit is food IMPLY an apple is food.
  A table is furniture AND furniture is equipment IMPLY a table is equipment.
  A shirt is clothing AND clothing is goods IMPLY a shirt is goods.
  A cat is an animal AND animals need water IMPLY a cat needs water.
  Paris is in France AND France is in Europe IMPLY Paris is in Europe.
  A hammer is a tool AND tools are made of metal IMPLY a hammer is likely metal.
  Monday is a day AND days have 24 hours IMPLY Monday has 24 hours.
  A puppy is a dog AND dogs bark IMPLY puppies bark.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.comparison
NAME: comparison_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <a> IS <comparison> <b>; THEREFORE ...
EXAMPLES:
  An elephant is larger than a dog IMPLY an elephant can carry more than a dog.
  A cheetah is faster than a horse IMPLY a cheetah can outrun a horse.
  Iron is stronger than wood IMPLY iron lasts longer under pressure.
  A car is faster than a bicycle IMPLY a car covers more distance per hour.
  A house is bigger than a room IMPLY a house holds more people.
  Gold is more valuable than iron IMPLY gold is rarer.
  Summer is hotter than winter IMPLY people wear less in summer.
  Tea is cheaper than coffee in this shop IMPLY tea is the cheaper choice.
  A plane is faster than a train IMPLY a plane is better for long distance.
  A book is heavier than a paper IMPLY a book is harder to carry.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.classification
NAME: classification_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <item> HAS <property>; ALL <class> HAVE <property>; IMPLY <item> IS IN <class>
EXAMPLES:
  All birds have feathers AND a sparrow has feathers IMPLY a sparrow is a bird.
  All fish live in water AND a salmon lives in water IMPLY a salmon is a fish.
  All mammals feed milk AND a whale feeds milk IMPLY a whale is a mammal.
  All squares have four equal sides AND this shape has four equal sides IMPLY this shape is a square.
  All trees have roots AND this plant has roots... (not enough: many plants have roots)
  All vehicles have wheels AND a car has wheels IMPLY a car is a vehicle. (weak: bikes have wheels too)
NOTES: classification by one property can be wrong; use several properties
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.sequence
NAME: sequence_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <event1> HAPPENS BEFORE <event2>; THEREFORE ...
EXAMPLES:
  First you mix the flour, THEN you add water IMPLY mixing comes before adding.
  The sun rises, THEN it sets IMPLY sunrise is before sunset.
  You wake up, THEN you eat breakfast IMPLY waking is before eating.
  You buy a ticket, THEN you board the train IMPLY buying is before boarding.
  Spring comes, THEN summer comes IMPLY spring is before summer.
  She studied, THEN she passed IMPLY studying caused the pass.
  He ran, THEN he got tired IMPLY running caused tiredness.
  They planted seeds, THEN the plants grew IMPLY planting caused growth.
  The rain stopped, THEN we went out IMPLY going out happened after the rain.
  The phone rang, THEN he answered IMPLY answering followed ringing.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.location
NAME: location_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <object> IS IN <container>; <container> IS IN <place>; IMPLY <object> IS IN <place>
EXAMPLES:
  The key is in the drawer AND the drawer is in the bedroom IMPLY the key is in the bedroom.
  The cat is on the roof AND the roof is on the house IMPLY the cat is on the house.
  The book is on the table AND the table is in the kitchen IMPLY the book is in the kitchen.
  The money is in the bag AND the bag is in the car IMPLY the money is in the car.
  Paris is in France AND France is in Europe IMPLY Paris is in Europe.
  The food is in the fridge AND the fridge is in the kitchen IMPLY the food is in the kitchen.
  The ball is under the bed AND the bed is in the room IMPLY the ball is in the room.
  The ship is on the sea AND the sea is near the land IMPLY the ship is near the land.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.ownership
NAME: ownership_reasoning
EXAMPLES:
  This is Mary's book AND Mary is Ann's sister IMPLY the book belongs to Ann's sister.
  The house is John's AND John is the owner IMPLY John owns the house.
  This is my pen AND I lend it to you IMPLY you may use it now.
  The dog belongs to Tom AND Tom is away IMPLY the dog needs care.
  If she owns the car AND she sells it to him IMPLY he owns the car.
  The garden is ours AND we planted the tree IMPLY we own the tree.
NOTES: possession can transfer; ownership chains are logical
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.possibility
NAME: possibility_and_certainty
DEGREES:
  certain: must be / definitely / surely
  probable: probably / likely / should
  possible: may / might / could / possibly
  improbable: probably not / unlikely
  impossible: cannot / definitely not / couldn't
EXAMPLES:
  The ground is wet. It must be raining. (certain, deduction)
  He has an umbrella. He will probably stay dry. (probable)
  It may rain tomorrow. (possible)
  She is always on time. She will probably arrive soon. (probable)
  The sun rises in the east. It will definitely rise. (certain)
  He is a baby. He cannot drive. (impossible)
  The light is on. Someone must be home. (deduction)
  The door is open. Someone could have come in. (possibility)
  He looks tired. He may not have slept well. (possibility)
  The road is blocked. We can't go that way. (impossible)
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.deduction
NAME: deduction_rules
RULE1: IF <A> THEN <B>; <B> IS TRUE -> <A> IS POSSIBLE (not certain)
RULE2: IF <A> THEN <B>; <B> IS FALSE -> <A> IS FALSE
RULE3: IF <A> THEN <B>; <A> IS TRUE -> <B> IS TRUE
EXAMPLES:
  If it rains, the ground is wet. The ground is wet -> it may have rained (or someone spilled water).
  If it rains, the ground is wet. The ground is dry -> it did not rain.
  If it rains, the ground is wet. It is raining -> the ground is wet.
  If a person is a doctor, they help sick people. This person helps sick people -> they may be a doctor (or a nurse).
  If you study, you pass. You did not pass -> you did not study.
  If you study, you pass. You studied -> you pass.
COUNTEREXAMPLES:
  If it rains, the ground is wet. The ground is wet -> it rained. (WRONG: other causes)
WHY: the first rule is a logical fallacy (affirming the consequent)
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.abduction
NAME: best_explanation_reasoning
STRUCTURE: observe <effect>; consider <possible causes>; choose the most likely
EXAMPLES:
  The street is wet -> likely rain, or a water truck, or a broken pipe -> best: rain (if no truck seen)
  The dog barks at the door -> likely someone is there, or a cat, or a noise -> best: someone
  The phone rings -> someone is calling -> best: someone wants to talk
  The lights are off -> no one is home, or everyone sleeps, or power cut -> check context
  He is coughing -> he has a cold, or dust, or he is sick -> best: check more signs
  The food is gone -> someone ate it, or it was thrown away -> best: ask
WHY: evidence supports the most likely explanation
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.prediction
NAME: prediction_from_rules
EXAMPLES:
  Rule: all dogs bark. Fact: this is a dog. Prediction: it will bark.
  Rule: water boils at 100 degrees. Fact: the pot is at 100. Prediction: it boils.
  Rule: birds fly. Fact: this is a bird. Prediction: it can fly. (but penguins can't)
  Rule: shop opens at 9. Fact: it is 10. Prediction: the shop is open.
  Rule: train leaves at 8. Fact: it is 7:30. Prediction: the train has not left yet.
  Rule: plants need water. Fact: the plant has no water for a week. Prediction: it will die.
  Rule: fire needs oxygen. Fact: cover the fire with sand. Prediction: the fire goes out.
NOTES: predictions follow rules; exceptions weaken them
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.exceptions
NAME: exceptions_weaken_rules
EXAMPLES:
  All birds fly -> except penguins, ostriches, kiwis
  All dogs bark -> most dogs, but some are quiet
  Water boils at 100 degrees -> at high altitude it boils lower
  Fire causes smoke -> clean gas fires produce little smoke
  If you study you pass -> sometimes difficult exams still fail
  Night is dark -> in the far north in summer it stays light
  All children like sweets -> some children don't
  Cats hate water -> some cats like water
NOTES: rules describe general behavior, not absolute laws
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.counterfactual
NAME: counterfactual_reasoning
STRUCTURE: IF <X> HAD BEEN TRUE, THEN <Y> WOULD HAVE BEEN TRUE
EXAMPLES:
  If he had studied, he would have passed. (he did not study)
  If she had left earlier, she would have caught the train. (she left late)
  If it had not rained, we would have gone out. (it rained)
  If I had known, I would have come. (I did not know)
  If they had won, they would be champions now. (they lost)
  If you had asked, I would have helped. (you did not ask)
NOTES: counterfactuals describe what did not happen
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.analogy
NAME: analogy_reasoning
STRUCTURE: <a> IS TO <b> AS <c> IS TO <d>
EXAMPLES:
  Hand is to body as leaf is to tree.
  Word is to sentence as sentence is to paragraph.
  Day is to night as summer is to winter.
  Teacher is to student as doctor is to patient.
  Car is to road as ship is to sea.
  Bird is to sky as fish is to water.
  Page is to book as chapter is to book.
  Father is to child as mother is to child.
  Knife is to cut as pen is to write.
  Rain is to flood as fire is to burn.
END

TYPE: REASON
ID: eng.reason.negation_logic
NAME: negation_logic
RULES:
  NOT(NOT <A>) = <A>
  NOT(<A> AND <B>) = NOT <A> OR NOT <B>
  NOT(<A> OR <B>) = NOT <A> AND NOT <B>
EXAMPLES:
  It is not true that it is not raining = it is raining.
  He is not both tall and strong = he is short OR weak (or both not).
  She is not a teacher or a doctor = she is not a teacher AND she is not a doctor.
  I don't have neither time nor money = I have time AND I have money. (nonstandard: literal)
  It is not the case that all birds fly = some birds do not fly.
  NOT everyone came = someone did not come.
END
# =============================================================================
# SECTION 21. ERRORS AND COUNTEREXAMPLES
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.021
NAME: errors_and_counterexamples
CONTENT: common errors with corrections and reasons for every important rule
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.article
NAME: article_errors
ERRORS:
  I have dog. -> I have a dog. (singular countable needs a/an/the)
  She is teacher. -> She is a teacher.
  He bought car. -> He bought a car.
  Give me book. -> Give me the book. (specific book -> the)
  I saw an useful book. -> I saw a useful book. (u = /ju:/ consonant sound)
  She is an honest woman. -> correct: honest starts with vowel sound /o/
  The love is important. -> Love is important. (no article for general abstract)
  I go to the school to learn. -> I go to school to learn. (institution: no article)
  The water is in the glass. -> correct: specific water -> the
  He plays the football. -> He plays football. (sports: no article)
  I listen to radio. -> I listen to the radio.
WHY: articles mark countable, specific, and general reference
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.plural
NAME: plural_errors
ERRORS:
  two childs -> two children
  three mouses -> three mice
  two sheeps -> two sheep (unchanged)
  many informations -> much information / many pieces of information
  two advices -> two pieces of advice (advice uncountable)
  I have many money. -> I have much money. (money uncountable)
  There are much people. -> There are many people.
  How much apples? -> How many apples?
  How many sugar? -> How much sugar?
  two mans -> two men
  two womans -> two women
  one feet -> one foot
  two tooths -> two teeth
  one children -> one child
WHY: irregular plurals and count/uncount distinction
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.tense
NAME: tense_errors
ERRORS:
  I am agree. -> I agree. (agree is a verb, not adjective)
  She is like tea. -> She likes tea.
  I have lived here since 3 years. -> I have lived here for 3 years. (for + duration)
  I have lived here for 2019. -> I have lived here since 2019. (since + point)
  I am here since Monday. -> I have been here since Monday.
  I live here since 2010. -> I have lived here since 2010.
  He goed home. -> He went home.
  She buyed a car. -> She bought a car.
  I have saw it. -> I have seen it.
  They have came. -> They have come.
  He didn't went. -> He didn't go.
  Did she came? -> Did she come?
  When I arrived, she already left. -> When I arrived, she had already left.
  I will call you when I will arrive. -> I will call you when I arrive.
  If it will rain, we stay. -> If it rains, we will stay.
  I am agree with you. -> I agree with you.
  She is knowing the answer. -> She knows the answer. (stative, no continuous)
  I am loving it. -> I love it. (stative)
  He has been working since 2 hours. -> ... for 2 hours.
  I was born in 1995. -> correct (born is fixed, always was)
  I born in 1995. -> I was born in 1995.
WHY: tense forms, for/since, stative verbs, and sequence of tenses
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.subject_verb
NAME: subject_verb_agreement_errors
ERRORS:
  She don't like it. -> She doesn't like it.
  He go to school. -> He goes to school.
  They is happy. -> They are happy.
  My friends is here. -> My friends are here.
  Everyone are happy. -> Everyone is happy. (indefinite singular)
  Neither of them are wrong. -> Neither of them is wrong. (formal)
  The news are good. -> The news is good.
  Mathematics are hard. -> Mathematics is hard.
  The number of students are large. -> The number of students is large.
  A number of students is absent. -> A number of students are absent.
  One of my friends are here. -> One of my friends is here.
  The man with his dogs walk here. -> The man with his dogs walks here. (subject = man)
  Each of the boys have a book. -> Each of the boys has a book.
WHY: verb agrees with the grammatical subject
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.modal
NAME: modal_errors
ERRORS:
  I can to swim. -> I can swim. (modal + base)
  She musts go. -> She must go. (no third person -s)
  He can swims. -> He can swim.
  I don't can come. -> I can't come.
  She doesn't must go. -> She mustn't go.
  I must to study. -> I must study.
  She cans play piano. -> She can play piano.
  You should to go. -> You should go.
  Will you can help me? -> Can you help me? / Will you be able to help me?
WHY: modals take the base form and no do-support
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.preposition
NAME: preposition_errors
ERRORS:
  I arrived to London. -> I arrived in London. (arrive in/at)
  She is married with him. -> She is married to him.
  I depend from you. -> I depend on you.
  He is good in math. -> He is good at math.
  She is afraid from spiders. -> She is afraid of spiders.
  I listen music. -> I listen to music.
  He discussed about the plan. -> He discussed the plan.
  We arrived at home. -> We arrived home.
  I went to home. -> I went home.
  She is interested on art. -> She is interested in art.
  He is similar with his father. -> He is similar to his father.
  I live at New York. -> I live in New York. (in + city)
  I live in 5th Street. -> I live on 5th Street. (on + street)
  The cat is in the tree. -> correct (in/up a tree)
  He laughed at me. -> correct (laugh at someone)
  She congratulated him for his success. -> She congratulated him on his success.
  I look forward to hear from you. -> I look forward to hearing from you. (to = preposition + gerund)
  She is used to live alone. -> She is used to living alone.
  He is good to speak French. -> He is good at speaking French.
WHY: each adjective/verb pairs with specific prepositions
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.gerund_infinitive
NAME: gerund_and_infinitive_errors
ERRORS:
  I enjoy to swim. -> I enjoy swimming. (enjoy + gerund)
  She avoids to meet him. -> She avoids meeting him.
  He finished to work. -> He finished working.
  I suggested to go. -> I suggested going.
  They postponed to leave. -> They postponed leaving.
  I want going home. -> I want to go home. (want + infinitive)
  She hopes seeing you. -> She hopes to see you.
  He decided going. -> He decided to go.
  I promised helping him. -> I promised to help him.
  She refused to helping. -> She refused to help.
  He offered helping. -> He offered to help.
  I can't afford to buying it. -> I can't afford to buy it.
  She managed passing. -> She managed to pass.
  I'd like going. -> I'd like to go.
  He agreed helping. -> He agreed to help.
  It is important studying. -> It is important to study.
  I look forward to hear from you. -> I look forward to hearing from you.
  She is used to get up early. -> She is used to getting up early.
  He stopped to smoke. -> differs: stopped smoking (quit) / stopped to smoke (paused to smoke)
  I remember to lock the door. -> differs: remember to lock (future) / remember locking (past)
WHY: verbs select gerund or infinitive; some change meaning
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.pronoun
NAME: pronoun_errors
ERRORS:
  Me and John went. -> John and I went. (subject form)
  She gave it to John and I. -> She gave it to John and me. (object form)
  Me like it. -> I like it.
  Him is my friend. -> He is my friend.
  Between you and I. -> Between you and me.
  She is taller than me. -> She is taller than I. (formal; than me informal)
  My brother, he is tall. -> My brother is tall. (redundant pronoun)
  The book is her. -> The book is hers. (possessive pronoun)
  It's me's book. -> It's my book.
  Their coming. -> They are coming.
  His and her books are here. -> Her and his books are here. (polite: others first)
  The dog wagged it's tail. -> The dog wagged its tail. (its = possessive, no apostrophe)
  Its raining. -> It's raining. (it's = it is)
  Who's book is this? -> Whose book is this?
  Whose is coming? -> Who is coming?
  Each of the students must bring their books. -> formal: his or her book; informal: their
WHY: subject/object forms and possessive forms differ
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.adjective_adverb
NAME: adjective_adverb_errors
ERRORS:
  She sings good. -> She sings well. (adverb: well)
  He runs fastly. -> He runs fast. (fast = adjective and adverb)
  I feel badly. -> I feel bad. (feel = linking verb + adjective)
  The soup tastes well. -> The soup tastes good. (linking verb + adjective)
  She works hardly. -> She works hard. (hard = adverb; hardly = almost not)
  He arrived lately. -> He arrived late. (late = adverb; lately = recently)
  She is a well singer. -> She is a good singer. (well = adverb, not adjective for people)
  He is more taller than me. -> He is taller than me. (double comparative)
  She is most happiest. -> She is the happiest. (double superlative)
  This is very more difficult. -> This is much more difficult. (very + comparative = wrong)
  He behaves more better now. -> He behaves better now.
  It is a very big problem. -> correct (very + adjective)
  I very like it. -> I like it very much. (very cannot modify verbs)
WHY: adjective vs adverb, irregular forms, double comparatives
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.word_order
NAME: word_order_errors
ERRORS:
  I have a car red. -> I have a red car.
  She is a woman beautiful. -> She is a beautiful woman.
  He bought a new car big. -> He bought a big new car. (size before age)
  It's a wooden small table. -> It's a small wooden table. (size before material)
  They are all good boys. -> correct (all + adjective + noun; also 'all the good boys')
  I every day go to work. -> I go to work every day.
  She always is late. -> She is always late. (frequency after be)
  He usually doesn't smoke. -> He doesn't usually smoke. (or: He usually doesn't smoke, both possible; prefer not between auxiliary and verb)
  I go never there. -> I never go there.
  Can you tell me where is the station? -> Can you tell me where the station is? (embedded question)
  Who did you meet? -> correct.
  She asked me what was the time. -> She asked me what the time was.
  Enough big. -> Big enough.
  Too much hot. -> Too hot.
  I have enough time. -> correct (enough before noun)
  He is enough old. -> He is old enough.
WHY: English word order: determiner + opinion + size + age + shape + color + origin + material + noun
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.double_negative
NAME: double_negative_errors
ERRORS:
  I don't have no money. -> I don't have any money. / I have no money.
  Nobody said nothing. -> Nobody said anything.
  She doesn't never call. -> She never calls. / She doesn't ever call.
  I can't find it nowhere. -> I can't find it anywhere. / I can find it nowhere.
  There isn't nobody here. -> There is nobody here. / There isn't anybody here.
  He doesn't know nothing about it. -> He knows nothing about it. / He doesn't know anything about it.
  I couldn't hardly hear. -> I could hardly hear.
  We don't need no help. -> We don't need any help.
WHY: one negative per clause; use any-words with the negative
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.fragment
NAME: sentence_fragment_errors
ERRORS:
  Because I was tired. -> Because I was tired, I went to bed. (attach the clause)
  When the bell rang. -> When the bell rang, everyone left.
  The man who called. -> The man who called is my uncle.
  Running in the park. -> I enjoy running in the park.
  A beautiful day. -> It was a beautiful day.
  If it rains. -> If it rains, we will stay home.
  To learn English. -> To learn English takes time.
  After dinner. -> After dinner, we watched TV.
  While waiting for the bus. -> While waiting for the bus, I read a book.
WHY: fragments lack a main clause; add or attach it
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.run_on
NAME: run_on_and_comma_splice_errors
ERRORS:
  I like tea I drink it daily. -> I like tea; I drink it daily. / I like tea, and I drink it daily.
  She was tired, she went to bed. -> She was tired, so she went to bed. (comma splice)
  He studied hard, he passed. -> He studied hard; he passed. / He studied hard, so he passed.
  It was raining, however, we went out. -> It was raining. However, we went out.
  The sun set, the stars appeared. -> After the sun set, the stars appeared.
  I came I saw I conquered. -> I came, I saw, I conquered.
WHY: two independent clauses need a connector, semicolon, or period
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.parallelism
NAME: parallelism_errors
ERRORS:
  She likes reading, to swim, and jogging. -> She likes reading, swimming, and jogging.
  He is smart, funny, and a hard worker. -> He is smart, funny, and hard-working.
  I want to eat, sleep, and relaxing. -> I want to eat, sleep, and relax.
  She is both kind and has courage. -> She is both kind and courageous.
  Not only did he win but also broke the record. -> He not only won but also broke the record.
  I like tea more than coffee and prefer it over juice. -> keep one structure
WHY: parallel items share the same grammatical form
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.redundancy
NAME: redundancy_errors
ERRORS:
  We discussed about the plan. -> We discussed the plan. (about redundant)
  She returned back home. -> She returned home. (back redundant)
  I met him at 3 PM in the afternoon. -> I met him at 3 PM. (PM = afternoon)
  He repeated again. -> He repeated it. (again redundant)
  The two twins arrived. -> The twins arrived. (two redundant)
  Advance planning is needed. -> Planning is needed. (advance redundant)
  I made a mistake error. -> I made a mistake.
  The reason is because... -> The reason is that...
  In my opinion, I think... -> I think... (one opinion phrase)
  Each and every person... -> Each person... (or: every person)
  Free gift -> gift (free redundant)
  Hot water heater -> water heater (heats cold water)
  I will be there in the near future. -> I will be there soon.
WHY: avoid repeating the same meaning twice
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.misplaced_modifier
NAME: misplaced_modifier_errors
ERRORS:
  I only ate the apple. -> ambiguous: only modifies ate (I ate nothing else)
  I ate only the apple. -> clear: only the apple, nothing else
  She almost drove the car for an hour. -> She drove the car for almost an hour.
  He nearly paid fifty dollars. -> He paid nearly fifty dollars.
  The dog bit the man with the red collar. -> ambiguous: who wears the collar?
  The man with the red collar was bitten by the dog. -> clear
  She saw the mountains riding a horse. -> Riding a horse, she saw the mountains. (who rides?)
  I almost forgot to buy the tickets for everyone. -> placement changes meaning
WHY: put the modifier next to the word it modifies
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.false_friend
NAME: false_friend_errors
ERRORS:
  actually (meaning 'in fact', not 'currently'): He actually finished early.
  eventually (meaning 'in the end', not 'possibly'): She eventually arrived.
  sensible (meaning 'reasonable', not 'sensitive'): He is a sensible man.
  sympathetic (meaning 'compassionate', not 'nice'): She was sympathetic to my loss.
  library (meaning 'book place', not 'bookshop'): I borrowed it from the library.
  chemistry (not 'alchemy'): She studies chemistry.
  fabric (meaning 'cloth', not 'factory'): The fabric is soft.
  brave (not 'good'): The brave soldier won a medal.
  pretend (meaning 'act as if', not 'intend'): He pretended to be asleep.
  supportive (not 'suffering'): My family is very supportive.
WHY: words that look like another language may have different meanings
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.quantity
NAME: quantity_errors
ERRORS:
  less books -> fewer books (fewer + countable)
  less water -> correct (less + uncountable)
  fewer water -> less water
  a little books -> a few books (a few + countable)
  a few water -> a little water (a little + uncountable)
  much people -> many people
  many money -> much money
  I have a lot of time. -> correct
  She has lots of friends. -> correct (informal)
  There are fewer cars today. -> correct
  There is less traffic today. -> correct (traffic uncountable)
  one bread -> a loaf of bread / one piece of bread
  two breads -> two loaves of bread
  three sugars, please -> three packets of sugar / three teaspoons of sugar
WHY: count nouns take few/many; mass nouns take little/much
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.condition
NAME: conditional_errors
ERRORS:
  If I will see him, I will tell him. -> If I see him, I will tell him. (no will in if-clause)
  If she would come, I would be happy. -> If she came, I would be happy. (past simple in if-clause)
  If I was you... -> If I were you... (were for unreal)
  If I had knew... -> If I had known...
  If he would have called... -> If he had called...
  If you will go, take an umbrella. -> If you go, take an umbrella.
  Unless it will rain... -> Unless it rains...
  I wish I am taller. -> I wish I were taller.
  I wish I knew the answer. -> correct (wish + past)
  I wish I had known. -> correct (wish + past perfect)
WHY: tense in conditionals: present for future, past for unreal, past perfect for unreal past
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.reported
NAME: reported_speech_errors
ERRORS:
  He said: "I am tired." -> He said that he was tired. (tense backshift)
  She said: "I will come." -> She said she would come.
  They said: "We have finished." -> They said they had finished.
  He said: "I can swim." -> He said he could swim.
  She asked: "Do you like tea?" -> She asked if I liked tea.
  He asked: "Where do you live?" -> He asked where I lived.
  She told me: "Come here." -> She told me to go there. (imperative -> to-infinitive)
  He said me that... -> He told me that... (say + to + person / tell + person)
  She told that she was tired. -> She said that she was tired. (tell needs person)
  He said he will come. -> backshift: He said he would come. (or keep will if still future)
  She said she has seen it. -> backshift: She said she had seen it.
WHY: reporting verbs and tense backshift
END

TYPE: ERROR
ID: eng.error.zero_article_special
NAME: special_article_cases
EXAMPLES:
  go to school / go to work / go to church / go to bed (institution: no article)
  go to the school (visiting the building, not as student)
  play football / play the piano / play chess (sports no article; instruments with the)
  have breakfast / have lunch / have dinner (meals no article)
  at night / in the morning / in the afternoon / in the evening
  by car / by bus / by train / by plane / on foot (transport: by/on)
  in bed / in hospital (UK) / in prison / at home
  the USA / the UK / the Netherlands / the Alps / the Nile (groups and plural names)
  on TV / on the radio / on the phone
  at school / at the school (same as institution rule)
WHY: some fixed phrases use no article; geography uses the for groups
END

# =============================================================================
# CLOSING SUMMARY
# =============================================================================

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.summary.overview
NAME: dataset_overview
CONTENT:
  Section 1 = Foundations: characters, digits, numbers, punctuation, symbols
  Section 2 = Parts of speech: the 12 categories and their functions
  Section 3 = Word knowledge: structured records for core vocabulary
  Section 4 = Morphology: plurals, prefixes, suffixes, derivation, inflection
  Section 5 = Pronouns: all pronoun classes and forms
  Section 6 = Verbs: be, have, do, modals, and conjugation
  Section 7 = Tenses: the 12 tenses with structure and transformation rules
  Section 8 = Sentence structure: patterns, components, and detection rules
  Section 9 = Subject detection: difficult cases and agreement rules
  Section 10 = Object detection: direct, indirect, complement, tests
  Section 11 = Questions: yes/no, wh-, tags, embedded questions
  Section 12 = Negation: not, never, no, nobody, neither, double negatives
  Section 13 = Voice: active, passive, transformations, causative
  Section 14 = Clauses and phrases: types, relative clauses, conditionals
  Section 15 = Conjunctions: coordinating, subordinating, correlative
  Section 16 = Semantics: synonym, antonym, hypernym, part_of, made_of, causes
  Section 17 = Context: how surrounding words resolve word meaning
  Section 18 = Ambiguity: types of ambiguity and resolution strategies
  Section 19 = Conversation: speech acts, dialogs, and responses
  Section 20 = Reasoning: if-then, cause-effect, deduction, prediction
  Section 21 = Errors and counterexamples: common errors with corrections
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.summary.how_to_use
NAME: how_to_use_this_dataset
ACTION:
  1. parse records: TYPE: name, fields KEY: VALUE, record ends at END
  2. learn SECTION order: foundations first, then words, grammar, meaning, use
  3. apply RULE records to generate and check sentences
  4. use TABLE records as lookup tables
  5. use RELATION records to connect words (is_a, has, causes...)
  6. use CONTEXT records to resolve ambiguous words
  7. use AMBIGUITY records to avoid and fix ambiguous structures
  8. use DIALOG records to learn conversation patterns
  9. use REASON records to draw conclusions and make predictions
  10. use ERROR records to recognize and correct mistakes
GENERALIZATION:
  rules apply to unseen sentences
  word categories apply to unseen words in the same category
  semantic relations extend to new members of a category
  tense rules apply to any verb with the same form class
  question and negation rules apply to any sentence with the same structure
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.summary.core_rules
NAME: core_rule_reminder
RULES:
  sentence = subject + predicate
  subject + verb agree in number
  verbs show tense with -s, -ed, -ing and auxiliaries
  questions invert subject and auxiliary; add do in simple tenses
  negation adds not after auxiliary or do; one negative per clause
  adjectives modify nouns; adverbs modify verbs, adjectives, adverbs
  articles: a/an for new, the for known
  pronouns replace nouns; choose subject or object form
  preposition follows the verb or adjective it pairs with
  clauses join with conjunctions; avoid fragments and comma splices
  meaning depends on context; ambiguity resolves by context
  reasoning: if-then, cause-effect, classification, comparison
  errors: apply counterexamples to avoid repeating mistakes
END

TYPE: SUMMARY
ID: eng.summary.data_format
NAME: data_format_summary
FORMAT:
  comments = lines starting with #
  record_start = TYPE: <TYPE_NAME>
  record_end = END
  field = KEY: VALUE
  list_item = two spaces + value or key = value
  id = unique dotted string in every record
  section = SECTION record with ID: eng.section.NNN
END

TYPE: SECTION
ID: eng.section.end
NAME: end_of_dataset
CONTENT: end of the English training dataset
END
